出人意料	chū rén yì liào	"beyond people's expectation" — unexpected; surprising; contrary to what was anticipated — Lit: exceed-people-intention-anticipate. One of the most common ways to say "surprisingly" or "unexpectedly" in formal Chinese. 这个结果出人意料，没有人预测到。"This result was unexpected , no one had predicted it." Also appears in the variant 出乎意料 chū hū yìliào with identical meaning.
穿针引线	chuān zhēn yǐn xiàn	thread the needle and lead the thread: to act as go-between — Literally the seamstress's first move. Figuratively, to broker a connection between two parties, matchmaking a deal, a marriage, a meeting.
船坚炮利	chuán jiān pào lì	sturdy ships and sharp guns: overwhelming naval and military might — A late-Qing phrase for the foreign gunboats whose strength forced the treaty ports open. Now shorthand for raw technological force.
闯南走北	chuǎng nán zǒu běi	"boldly moving through south and north" — widely traveled; experienced in the world — 闯 (to push through boldly) + 南 (south) + 走 (to travel) + 北 (north). A person who has made their way through the whole country, acquiring experience and resilience. The verb 闯 suggests the travel was not comfortable or planned but forced through obstacles.
春暖花开	chūn nuǎn huā kāi	spring is warm and flowers bloom: the good season has come — The stock image of spring at its best. Used literally for the season and figuratively for the right moment, a turn for the better after hardship.
词不达意	cí bù dá yì	the words fail to convey the meaning: to express oneself poorly — Lit: words-not-reach-meaning. Said when the language a speaker or writer chooses does not match what they meant, so the point comes across muddled or wrong. A common way to apologize for an unclear remark.
存天理，灭人欲	cún tiān lǐ , miè rén yù	preserve Heavenly Principle, eliminate human desire — Zhu Xi's formulation of moral cultivation — Perhaps the most quoted and most criticized phrase in Neo-Confucianism. 天理 tiānlǐ = Heavenly Principle (the universal moral norm); 人欲 rén yù = human desires (not all desires but those that conflict with Heavenly Principle: excessive appetite, selfishness).
错综复杂	cuò zōng fù zá	crisscrossed and tangled: intricate and complicated — Lit: interlaced-gathered-doubled-mixed. Keeps the "crossing" sense of 错: relationships or situations so interwoven and layered that they are hard to untangle. 错综复杂的关系 "a web of tangled relationships."
答非所问	dá fēi suǒ wèn	the answer is not what was asked: to give a reply beside the question — Lit: answer-not-what-asked. A reply that fails to address the actual question, whether from evasion or misunderstanding. A near neighbor of 文不对题, but about spoken answers rather than written work.
打抱不平	dǎ bào bù píng	to stand up for the wronged; to right injustices — 打 (to strike at) + 抱 (to hold) + 不平 (unfairness). To beat back unfairness, to intervene on behalf of someone being treated unjustly. The righteous stranger who steps in. A heroic frame common in wuxia literature.
打草惊蛇	dǎ cǎo jīng shé	to beat the grass and startle the snake — to tip off the target through careless action — One of the 36 Stratagems (三十六计). The grass is struck and the snake hiding inside is alerted. Used to warn against premature or clumsy action that reveals your intentions and lets the target escape or prepare. Common in military, political, and business contexts.
大材小用	dà cái xiǎo yòng	great timber put to small use — talent wasted on a trivial task — Lit: great-timber small-use. A large structural beam cut down for use as a chopstick. The standard expression for overqualification or talent misallocation. Said politely of others, rarely of oneself. The 小 here measures not the person but the scope of the task assigned to them.
大吃大喝	dà chī dà hē	to eat and drink lavishly: feast extravagantly — The doubled 大 stresses excess. Modern usage often flags wasteful banqueting or living beyond one's means.
大错特错	dà cuò tè cuò	greatly and especially wrong: utterly, completely mistaken — Lit: big-error-particularly-error. An emphatic doubling that drives home how badly someone has misjudged: 你这样想就大错特错了 "if you think that, you are gravely mistaken."
大大方方	dà dà fāng fāng	generous and unaffected; poised and at ease in manner — The reduplicated form of 大方 dàfang (generous; gracious; not stingy or awkward). Said in praise of someone whose bearing is relaxed and dignified rather than petty or self-conscious.
大哥别说二哥	dà gē bié shuō èr gē	the eldest shouldn't mock the second: pot calling the kettle black — A proverb on shared flaws: 大哥 and 二哥 are equally at fault, so neither has standing to criticize the other. The Mandarin take on glass houses.
大好河山	dà hǎo hé shān	the great and beautiful rivers and mountains; the magnificent homeland — The near-synonym of 大好江山, using 河山 héshān (Yellow River and mountains) instead of 江山 (Yangtze and mountains). 河 hé specifically refers to the Yellow River , 黄河 Huáng Hé , the cradle of northern Chinese civilization.
大喝一声	dà hè yī shēng	to give a great shout: bellow once, loudly — Here 喝 takes its hè reading, "to shout". A stock phrase in classical fiction and storytelling, the warrior or judge who freezes a room with a single roar.
大江东去	dà jiāng dōng qù	the great river flows east — time and history flow on; the brilliant figures of the past are gone — From Su Shi's 苏轼 famous ci poem "Niannujiao · Red Cliff Nostalgia" (念奴娇·赤壁怀古): 大江东去，浪淘尽，千古风流人物. "The great river flows east; its waves wash away the brilliant figures of a thousand ages." Invoked for historical grandeur and the irreversible passage of time.
大街小巷	dà jiē xiǎo xiàng	big streets and small lanes: everywhere in town — The standard way to say all over the city, every street and back alley. Neutral register, extremely common in news and speech: 大街小巷都挂满了灯笼, lanterns hung all over the streets.
大姐大	dà jiě dà	top sister: a commanding, dominant woman — A colloquial trigram, not a classical idiom, for the female leader of a group or a forceful, authoritative woman. Carries a half-admiring, half-wary tone.
大惊小怪	dà jīng xiǎo guài	to make a big fuss over nothing — overreacting to small things — Lit: big-shock small-wonder. To be shocked and amazed by things that are actually trivial. A mild criticism of someone who overreacts or lacks perspective.
大开眼界	dà kāi yǎn jiè	to greatly open one's horizons — to have one's eyes opened — Lit: greatly-open-eye-boundary. The standard phrase for the experience of suddenly seeing something new and broadening one's outlook. Said after a trip, a museum visit, a striking piece of work.
大快人心	dà kuài rén xīn	greatly satisfying to the people's hearts — a righteous outcome that pleases everyone — Lit: greatly-fast/free-people's-hearts. Used when a wrongdoer receives punishment, a long-overdue verdict is handed down, or a corrupt official is finally removed. The 快 here is squarely in the "heart released from obstruction" sense: justice has cleared away what was blocking everyone.
大闹天宫	dà nào tiān gōng	havoc in Heaven — the Monkey King's great rebellion — The celebrated early episode in which Sun Wukong storms the celestial palace, defeats Heaven's armies, and helps himself to the food and treasures of the gods.
大运河	dà yùn hé	the Grand Canal — The Sui's enduring monument: 运 yùn (to transport) + 河 hé (river), a man-made transport river. Built at terrible human cost under Emperor Yang, it linked the rice-rich south to the political north and remained the backbone of Chinese internal trade for over a millennium.
待人接物	dài rén jiē wù	to treat people and handle affairs: the way one conducts oneself with others — Lit: treat-people-engage-things. The whole manner of dealing with others and managing matters; said in praise of someone whose conduct is gracious and well judged.
当机立断	dāng jī lì duàn	to decide decisively at the right moment; not to hesitate when the time comes — Lit: facing-the-opportunity-immediately-decide. The complement of 把握时机 , not just grasping the moment but acting on it decisively. 断 here means to cut through, to decide without wavering. The virtuous counterpart to 犹豫不决 (hesitating and unable to decide).
当局者迷，旁观者清	dāng jú zhě mí , páng guān zhě qīng	those involved are confused; bystanders see clearly — 当局者 = the one who is in the situation; the person directly involved + 旁观者 = the one standing to the side watching. 迷 = confused, lost / 清 = clear, perceptive. Two contrasting 者 nominalizings set in opposition: the agent inside the situation vs. the observer outside it.
当头棒喝	dāng tóu bàng hè	a staff-blow and a shout to the head — a jolt that wakes someone up — From Chan pedagogy at its most physical: Master Deshan was famous for the staff, Master Linji for the shout, both deployed to shatter a student's conceptual dithering. Now any sharp warning that snaps a person out of a bad course: a failed audit, a doctor's blunt sentence.
得心应手	dé xīn yìng shǒu	handy and skillful: to do something with effortless mastery — Lit: the hand answers the heart. What the mind intends, the hand at once carries out. Praise for a skill so practiced it feels automatic. Here 应 takes the yìng reading, "to respond."
地大物博	dì dà wù bó	vast land, abundant resources — used of China's territory — Lit: land-great-things-abundant. The 地 here is the full noun "land, earth" (dì, 4th tone), not the adverbial particle de. Appears frequently in descriptions of China's natural endowment. A reminder: 地 has a full semantic life beyond its particle function.
灯红酒绿	dēng hóng jiǔ lǜ	red lamps and green wine: gaudy nightlife — The glow of pleasure quarters, often tinged with disapproval. Describes a city of bars and neon, sometimes implying decadence or excess.
灯火辉煌	dēng huǒ huī huáng	lamps blazing splendidly: brilliantly lit — A hall or street ablaze with light, suggesting wealth, festivity, or grandeur. A common way to paint a banquet or a lit skyline.
灯火通明	dēng huǒ tōng míng	lamps lit all through: every light blazing — A house, street, or ship lit up from end to end, not one window dark. Plainer than 灯火辉煌: it stresses that everything is lit rather than that the light is splendid.
登科及第	dēng kē jí dì	to ascend the rolls and place in the rankings: to succeed in the civil-service exam — Lit: mount-the-roster-reach-rank. 登科 is to have one's name posted on the pass list, 及第 to be ranked within it. A set phrase of congratulation for examination success.
滴水穿石	dī shuǐ chuān shí	dripping water bores through stone: persistence wins — The image of a single drip, repeated for years, drilling a hole in rock. The standard idiom for patient, steady effort overcoming a hard obstacle.
颠倒黑白	diān dǎo hēi bái	to invert black and white — to deliberately confuse right and wrong — 颠倒 diāndǎo (to turn upside down) + 黑白 hēibái (black and white). To knowingly call wrong right and right wrong, twisting the truth for one's own ends. Stronger and more accusatory than simply lying: it charges the speaker with corrupting the very standard of judgment. 他为了脱罪，竟然颠倒黑白。
颠倒是非	diān dào shì fēi	to invert right and wrong; to call black white and white black — Lit: overturn-invert-right-wrong. A severe accusation: to deliberately misrepresent the facts, to claim something wrong is right and vice versa. The logical extreme of a bad-faith 是…的 , not just correcting a focus, but inverting the entire reality. Common in political rhetoric and moral arguments.
凋零	diāo líng	"to wither and fall" — to fade and drop away; (of people) to pass away — 凋 (to wither) + 零 (to fall in drops). Strictly a two-character word rather than a four-character chengyu, but a fixed literary expression worth knowing.
顶天立地	dǐng tiān lì dì	head touching heaven, feet on earth — a person of complete integrity and heroic stature — 顶 dǐng = to stand at the top of; to press against from below. 立 lì = to stand; to be upright. The image is of a person so morally complete that they fill the entire vertical axis of the universe , feet firmly planted on earth, head pressing against heaven.
鼎力相助	dǐng lì xiāng zhù	to render great help: a respectful thanks for another's strong assistance — Lit: with-tripod-strength-to-help. 鼎 is the massive bronze ritual tripod, so 鼎力 is great strength. A polite phrase used to acknowledge or request another person's powerful support.
丢人现眼	diū rén xiàn yǎn	"lose people, show eyes" — to make a spectacle of oneself; disgraceful public behavior — Lit: lose-person-expose-eyes. A stronger and more colorful version of 丢脸 , specifically about embarrassing public display. 现眼 (to expose one's eyes to public ridicule) adds the dimension of being stared at and judged.
东奔西跑	dōng bēn xī pǎo	"running east and dashing west" — to rush around in every direction — A four-character formula combining 奔 and 跑 across the directional pair 东 dōng / 西 xī. Used for someone constantly on the move, often in service of obligations or in pursuit of a livelihood.
东奔西走	dōng bēn xī zǒu	"rushing east, running west" — frantically busy everywhere; constantly in motion — Lit: east-rush-west-walk. Describes someone perpetually in motion, always somewhere else, never settled. Often implies exhausting, stressful busyness with little to show for it. 他为了这件事东奔西走了好几年。"He spent years running all over the place trying to make this happen."
东拼西凑	dōng pīn xī còu	"pieced together from east and west" — cobbled together from miscellaneous sources — 东拼 (stitched from the east) + 西凑 (gathered from the west). Something assembled by scraping together bits and pieces from wherever they could be found. Used for a speech that draws from random sources, a meal made from leftovers, or a budget patched together with emergency measures.
东山再起	dōng shān zài qǐ	"rise again from the Eastern Mountain" — to make a comeback after defeat — From the biography of Xiè Ān (謝安, 320–385 CE), a Jin dynasty statesman who retired to 东山 (Eastern Mountain, in present-day Zhejiang) and refused office for years. When the court fell into crisis, he was recalled and restored Jin to stability.
东施效颦	Dōng Shī xiào pín	to imitate blindly without understanding, and make things worse — A homely woman copies Xi Shi's famous frown and clears the street: Zhuangzi's parable about form without essence.
洞房花烛	dòng fáng huā zhú	bridal chamber and wedding candles: the wedding night — 洞房 is the bridal chamber, the deep inner 房 of the house; 花烛 are the red wedding candles. The set phrase stands for a marriage celebration, often paired as 洞房花烛夜, the night of the wedding.
独门绝技	dú mén ju é jì	a unique school's matchless skill: a signature technique known to no one else — Lit: sole-school-unmatched-skill. 门 here is a school or lineage of practice. A 独门绝技 is the secret art that belongs to one tradition or master alone.
独占鳌头	dú zhàn áo tóu	to come in first; to stand at the apex — Lit: to stand alone on the giant turtle's head. 鳌 áo is the mythical sea turtle whose head is the highest point, corresponding to the top of the imperial examination standings. Metaphor: to take first place.
短兵相接	duǎn bīng xiāng jiē	short weapons meet: close-quarters combat, a fierce face-to-face clash — From the Chu Ci. Now applied figuratively to any direct, intense confrontation, including debate and competition.
对牛弹琴	duì niú tán qín	playing the qin to a cow — speaking to someone incapable of understanding — From a Han dynasty text attributed to 牟融 Móu Róng. A musician plays the most refined music for a cow; the cow goes on eating grass. The problem is not the cow's intelligence but the category mismatch: the music is addressed to the wrong kind of listener.
对症下药	duì zhèng xià yào	prescribe according to the symptoms; tailor the remedy to the problem — 对 (match) + 症 (symptoms) + 下药 (administer medicine). From the case lore of the Han physician Hua Tuo, who gave two patients with identical headaches different prescriptions because the causes differed.
多才多艺	duō cái duō yì	of many talents and many skills: versatile, multitalented — Lit: much-talent-much-skill. High praise for a person gifted in several arts at once, the painter who also writes and plays music. 艺 yì is craft or art, the paired half of 才.
多此一举	duō cǐ yī jǔ	"one unnecessary action too many" — to do something superfluous — 多 (extra, unnecessary) + 此 (this) + 一举 (one action). An extra action that wasn't needed , wasted effort that doesn't improve the result. A gentle criticism of overcaution or unnecessary elaboration: 你不用解释，多此一举 ("you don't need to explain , that's unnecessary").
恩将仇报	ēn jiāng chóu bào	to repay kindness with enmity: to bite the hand that feeds you — 恩 (kindness) is met with 仇 (enmity) as its 报 (return). A sharp condemnation of ingratitude, common in everyday speech to call out a betrayer.
二虎相争	èr hǔ xiāng zhēng	two tigers fight — when two powerful forces clash, both suffer — The full form is often 两虎相争，必有一伤 liǎng hǔ xiāng zhēng, bì yǒu yī shāng: "when two tigers fight, one is certain to be wounded." Used to counsel against unnecessary conflict between equals, and to describe political or commercial battles where both sides bleed.
二话不说	èr huà bù shuō	without a second word: at once, without hesitation or argument — Lit: a-second-word-not-said. Said of someone who acts immediately, raising no objection and wasting no breath: 他二话不说就答应了 "he agreed without a moment's hesitation."
发扬光大	fā yáng guāng dà	to carry forward and make flourish: to develop a good thing to its fullest — Lit: send-forth-raise-bright-great. Said of carrying on a tradition, virtue, or fine quality and making it grow even greater. The fā reading: to bring something forth and develop it. 把好传统发扬光大 "to carry a good tradition forward."
法不阿贵	fǎ bù ē guì	the law shows no favor to the noble — law applies equally regardless of rank — From Han Feizi. 阿 ē = to show favoritism, to bend. 贵 guì = the noble, the powerful. The complete saying continues: 绳不绕曲 shéng bù rào qū , "the carpenter's line does not bend around curves." Law, like a measuring line, must be straight.
翻江倒海	fān jiāng dǎo hǎi	to overturn rivers and upend seas: overwhelming, world-shaking force — Lit: overturn-river topple-sea. Said of a power great enough to churn the waters of the world, and by extension of turbulent emotion or upheaval: 心里翻江倒海 "the heart in utter turmoil."
翻山越岭	fān shān yu è lǐng	"to cross mountain ridge after mountain ridge" — to traverse difficult terrain, by extension, to overcome great obstacles — A four-character compound combining 翻越 (to climb over) split across 山 (mountain) and 岭 (ridge). Used both literally (an arduous mountain journey) and figuratively (overcoming a series of difficulties to reach a goal). Frequent in war-history and biographical writing. 他翻山越岭，终于来到了家乡。
反客为主	fǎn kè wéi zhǔ	turn from guest to host: to seize the initiative and take control — Lit: reverse-guest-become-host. Once a stratagem of war: the side that arrives in a weaker, guest-like position turns the tables and takes command. Now used of anyone who flips a passive role into a controlling one.
返璞归真	fǎn pú guī zhēn	return to the unpolished, restore the authentic — strip away ornament for plain truth — Lit: return-uncarved-restore-real. Daoist in spirit. 璞 = uncarved jade. The aesthetic and ethical movement back from elaboration to unworked simplicity.
房谋杜断	fáng móu dù duàn	Fáng plans, Dù decides: a perfect pairing of complementary talents — From the Tang statesmen Fáng Xuánlíng, famed for strategy, and Dù Rúhuì, famed for decisiveness. Here 房 is the surname Fáng; the idiom praises two people whose strengths complete each other.
放眼天下	fàng yǎn tiān xià	cast one's eyes across all-under-heaven — to think broadly; to have a global perspective — 放眼 fàng yǎn (to let one's gaze range freely; to look far) + 天下. To look beyond one's immediate circumstances, neighborhood, or nation and think on a civilizational or global scale. The opposite of 鼠目寸光 (shǔ mù cùn guāng , mouse-eyes seeing only an inch; short-sightedness). 要放眼天下，不能只看眼前。
飞黄腾达	fēi huáng téng dá	Feihuang gallops upward: to rise rapidly in rank — 飞黄 (Fēihuáng) is a legendary divine steed; the phrase pictures a career rising as if riding it skyward. The standard idiom for a meteoric rise in office or fortune.
非同小可	fēi tóng xiǎo kě	no small matter: serious, of great importance — Lit: not-the-same-as-trivial. 小可 means a small or trivial thing, and 非同 negates it: this is not on a par with the trivial, so treat it with weight. 这件事非同小可 "this is no small matter."
非我族类	fēi wǒ zú lèi	those not of our kin; those outside our group
封官许愿	fēng guān xǔ yuàn	to dole out titles and make promises: to buy support with offers of office
封妻荫子	fēng qī yìn zǐ	titles for the wife, privilege for the sons: rank and honor extended to one's family — The reward of high office under the empire: a man's success earned a title for his wife and protected status for his sons. Evokes the prestige of the old examination-and-rank system.
烽火戏诸侯	fēng huǒ xì zhū hóu	lighting the beacon fires to toy with the lords — crying wolf at the cost of a dynasty — From the story of King You and Bao Si: the beacon fires lit for entertainment, the vassal lords who stopped responding, and the Quanrong attack that ended Western Zhou. Used to describe any deception that destroys the credibility needed in a real crisis.
否极泰来	pǐ jí tài lái	after extreme obstruction (yin) comes peace (yang) — when things hit rock bottom, improvement follows — From the Yijing hexagrams 否 pǐ (stagnation; obstruction , a yin hexagram) and 泰 tài (peace; harmony , a yang hexagram). The cosmological law: forces cycle, yin cannot dominate forever, the turn of yang is inevitable. A comforting phrase in hard times: 否极泰来，好日子还在后面。
改头换面	gǎi tóu huàn miàn	change the head, swap the face: a superficial makeover — Originally Buddhist, on the soul taking a new body. Now usually pejorative: the form is redone but the substance is unchanged, like a rebranded scam.
改弦更张	gǎi xián gēng zhāng	change the string and retune — fundamental reform, not adjustment — 改弦 = to change the string (of a stringed instrument); 更张 = to restring and retune. The 更 is gēng, first tone. A musician who only retunes (改弦) is making incremental adjustments; one who also rerestrings (更张) is starting fresh.
改弦易辙	gǎi xián yì zhé	"change the bowstring, switch the wheel-rut" — to change direction; to take a new approach — 改 (change) + 弦 (xián, bowstring) + 易 (change) + 辙 (zhé, wheel-rut). The two halves are parallel images of changing tools and changing track. Used for any decisive shift in policy, method, or life-direction.
改邪归正	gǎi xié guī zhèng	to abandon the crooked and return to the straight: to mend one's ways — Lit: change-evil-return-upright. Said of someone who gives up wrongdoing and comes back to honest living. 邪 (crooked, evil) is the standing opposite of 正.
敢作敢为	gǎn zuò gǎn wéi	"dare to do, dare to act" — bold and decisive; willing to take initiative and responsibility — 敢 (to dare) + 作 (to do) + 敢 (to dare) + 为 (to act). The positive image of 为 as confident, owned action , the opposite pole from 无为. Where 无为 counsels releasing the forcing quality of striving, 敢作敢为 describes the quality of someone who acts decisively and stands behind what they do. 这个领导敢作敢为，大家都很佩服。
敢做敢当	gǎn zuò gǎn dāng	to dare to act and dare to answer for it — to own one's actions — 敢 gǎn (to dare) + 做 + 敢 + 当 dāng (to bear, to take responsibility). The mark of a person of integrity: bold enough to act, and equally willing to shoulder the consequences. The phrase praises moral courage paired with accountability, the refusal to act and then duck the blame.
高不可攀	gāo bù kě pān	"too high to climb" — unattainable; inaccessible — 高 (high) + 不可 (cannot) + 攀 (to climb, to reach by climbing). So elevated that it is beyond reach , a person, position, standard, or price that cannot be attained.
高朋满座	gāo péng mǎn zuò	distinguished friends fill the seats: a gathering of honored guests — Lit: high-friends fill the seats. 高朋 are eminent guests, the 高 marking rank or worth. The phrase from Wang Bo's preface to the Tengwang Pavilion praises a host whose hall is full of distinguished company, a warm word for a well-attended feast.
高山流水	gāo shān liú shuǐ	high mountains, flowing water — finding someone who truly hears you — The piece Boya played when Ziqi first identified the mountains and rivers in his music. As a chengyu, 高山流水 has two uses: the meeting of a 知音 (someone who understands you perfectly), and more broadly, music or artistry of the very highest order.
高谈阔论	gāo tán kuò lùn	to talk loftily and expansively: to hold forth grandly, often emptily — Literally "lofty talk, broad discoursing." Describes someone holding forth at length on grand themes, frequently with the implication that the talk is more impressive than substantial. Can be neutral (spirited, wide-ranging conversation) but often carries a faint edge of "all talk."
高瞻远瞩	gāo zhān yuǎn zhǔ	"look high and see far" — farsighted vision; strategic perspective — 高 (high) + 瞻 (to look up, to look ahead) + 远 (far) + 瞩 (to gaze at closely). Looking from an elevated position and therefore seeing farther: the physical metaphor for strategic or visionary thinking. Used to praise leaders, planners, and thinkers who see beyond immediate circumstances.
哥俩好	gē liǎ hǎo	the two of us are tight: a toast and a drinking-game call — A finger-guessing drinking call and a stock phrase for two close male friends. 俩 (liǎ) is "the two of us," so the whole thing means "we two brothers are solid."
割地赔款	gē dì péi kuǎn	cede territory and pay indemnities — the formula of defeat in unequal treaties — Lit: cut-land-compensate-money. The standard shorthand for what unequal treaties required of China: territorial concessions (割地) combined with massive financial reparations (赔款). The phrase encodes the humiliation of the treaty system in four characters.
革故鼎新	gé gù dǐng xīn	reform the old, establish the new — From the Book of Changes. The more active, transformative counterpart to 与时俱进; where 与时俱进 describes keeping pace, 革故鼎新 describes active renovation. The 鼎 (bronze tripod) is a symbol of legitimate authority; 鼎新 means to inaugurate a new order as one would inaugurate a new dynasty.
格物致知	gé wù zhì zhī	investigate things to extend knowledge — the Neo-Confucian epistemological program — From the 大学 Dàxué (Great Learning). 格 gé = to probe, to arrive at; 物 wù = things and affairs; 致 zhì = to extend, to bring to completion; 知 zhī = knowledge.
个个	gè gè	each and every one; all of them — Reduplicating the measure word makes it mean "every single one without exception." 他们个个都很努力 "every one of them works hard." A vivid, emphatic way to say "all."
个中滋味	gè zhōng zī wèi	the flavor within: the inner experience only the one who lived it can know — Lit: the-taste-within-this. 个中 means "within this matter," and 滋味 is flavor. The phrase points to feelings or hardships that an outsider cannot fully grasp, known only to the person who went through them.
各尽所用	gè jìn suǒ yòng	"each fully deploying what it is suited for" — every component serving its proper function — 各 gè = each; every. 尽 jìn = fully; to exhaust. 所用 = what it is for; the use to which it is suited. A principle of social and organizational coordination: when each person, each resource, each role operates at full capacity within its proper domain, the whole system functions well.
各抒己见	gè shū jǐ jiàn	each airs his own view: everyone speaks their mind freely — Lit: each-express-own-view. Said of a discussion where every participant is free to put forward their own opinion, the ideal of open and frank exchange. 抒 shū means to express or pour out.
各有所美	gè yǒu suǒ měi	each has its own beauty — beauty takes many forms — A pluralist aesthetic principle: different things, places, or people each have their own kind of beauty. Used to avoid ranking or comparisons. The civilized response to "which is better?"
各有所能	gè yǒu suǒ néng	each has their own abilities — everyone has something they're good at — Lit: each-has-what-[they]-can[do]. A Confucian-flavored observation about the distribution of talent. Used to affirm that different people excel in different domains , a polite and diplomatic formula. 人各有所能，没什么好比较的 "Everyone has their own abilities; there's nothing to compare."
各种各样	gè zhǒng gè yàng	every kind and every sort: all manner of things — Lit: each-kind-each-sort. The standard everyday phrase for "all kinds of," stressing variety: 各种各样的人 "all sorts of people," 各种各样的问题 "every kind of problem."
公而忘私	gōng ér wàng sī	so devoted to the common good as to forget oneself — Lit: public-and-forget-private. Praise for one who throws themselves into public duty to the point of neglecting personal interest. A close cousin of 大公无私.
公事公办	gōng shì gōng bàn	to handle official business by the book: no favors, no shortcuts — Lit: public-matter-public-handle. To deal with official affairs strictly on their merits, without bending the rules for friendship or private gain. Often said with a touch of formality or coolness.
狗急跳墙	gǒu jí tiào qiáng	a cornered dog leaps the wall: desperation breeds reckless acts — The image is a trapped dog that, with no escape, hurls itself over a wall it could never normally clear. Used of people pushed past the edge who lash out wildly. Common in spoken and written warnings.
狗尾续貂	gǒu wěi xù diāo	patching a sable robe with a dog's tail: a shabby sequel to a fine work — From the Jìn Shū, where lavish marten-fur posts ran short and dog tails were used instead. Now a self-deprecating or critical term for an unworthy continuation of something excellent.
故步自封	gù bù zì fēng	to confine oneself to old ways: to be complacent and refuse to progress — Literally "wall yourself in with your old footsteps." 封 here is the self-imposed boundary. A common criticism of stagnant institutions or people who resist new ideas.
故弄玄虚	gù nòng xuán xū	to deliberately mystify: to make something seem more obscure or profound than it is — 故 (deliberately) + 弄 (nòng, to play with, to manipulate) + 玄 (xuán, mysterious, dark, profound) + 虚 (xū, empty, unreal). To purposely dress something up as mysterious in order to confuse or impress, when in fact there is little substance behind it.
关系密切	guān xi mì qiē	"relationship close and tight" — closely connected; intimately related — A descriptive fixed phrase rather than a classical 成语, but widely used as a set expression in formal Chinese writing and speech. 这两家公司的关系密切，共享很多资源。"These two companies are closely connected and share many resources." The closeness can be personal, institutional, or strategic.
光明正大	guāng míng zhèng dà	open, upright, and honest — nothing to hide — Lit: light-bright-correct-great. Used to describe someone who acts with complete transparency and moral integrity. 他做事光明正大 "He does things openly and uprightly."
归根结底	guī gēn jié dǐ	to return to the root and settle the bottom: in the final analysis, when all is said and done — Lit: return-root-conclude-bottom. Used to introduce the fundamental point after stripping away the surface: 归根结底，还是钱的问题 "in the end, it still comes down to money." Here 结 carries the settling sense.
过河拆桥	guò hé chāi qiáo	cross the river, then tear down the bridge — A vivid picture of ingratitude: discarding the very means or person that got you across once you are safely over. A sharp accusation in modern speech.
哈哈大笑	hā hā dà xiào	to roar with laughter: a full belly laugh — 哈哈 is the sound of laughter itself, 大笑 a big laugh. The everyday, vivid way to say someone burst out laughing. Common in narration and casual speech alike.
海不扬波	hǎi bù yáng bō	the sea does not surge — peaceful passage; a stable, unchallenged realm — Lit: sea-not-raise-waves. A classical phrase for the ideal of peaceful sea passage , exactly what Mazu provides. Used in Mazu temple inscriptions and ritual prayers.
海枯石烂	hǎi kū shí làn	till the seas run dry and the rocks crumble: forever, unchanging — Lit: sea-dry stone-rot. A vow of eternal constancy, used in oaths of love and loyalty: even when the impossible happens and the ocean dries up, the feeling will not change.
海量	hǎi liàng	sea-sized capacity: a vast tolerance or a huge amount — 海 hǎi (sea) + 量 liàng (capacity). A capacity as large as the sea. Traditionally a generous compliment for someone of great tolerance, whether a magnanimous temperament (大人有海量, your honor is broad-minded) or a formidable drinking capacity at the table.
邯郸学步	hán dān xu é bù	losing your own way while trying to imitate another's — ending up with neither — From the Zhuangzi (秋水 chapter). The problem: imitation so total that it erases the imitator's original competence. Used for people who pursue a foreign or fashionable style so completely that they lose their own footing.
汗牛充栋	hàn niú chōng dòng	to sweat the oxen and fill the rafters: a vast number of books — Coined by the Tang writer Liǔ Zōngyuán. So many volumes that hauling them makes the oxen sweat and stacking them fills the house to the roofbeams. Praise for a great library or prolific output.
好汉不吃眼前亏	hǎo hàn bù chī yǎn qián kuī	a wise fellow avoids a loss he can plainly see coming — Lit: a good-fellow does not eat the loss before his eyes. Folk wisdom that real courage includes the sense to step back from a fight you cannot win, rather than charging in for pride.
好景不长	hǎo jǐng bù cháng	good times don't last — a beautiful scene cannot endure — Lit: good-scenery-not-long. An expression of Buddhist impermanence and Chinese realism about fortune. Used when pleasant circumstances are ending or were brief.
好了歌	hǎo le gē	the Song of Good and Done — the vanity of worldly craving — The song sung by a tattered Daoist near the start of the novel, teaching that all the things people chase (rank, gold, lovers, children) end in nothing, and that 好 (good) and 了 (finished with, let go of) are ultimately the same.
好事多磨	hǎo shì duō mó	good things take time — good outcomes require much grinding and effort — Lit: good-things-much-grinding. Used to console someone experiencing delays or obstacles on the path to something valuable. The consolation: friction is the price of worth.
合情合理	hé qíng hé lǐ	in keeping with feeling and reason: fair and reasonable — Lit: accords-with-feeling, accords-with-reason. Said of a decision or request that satisfies both human sentiment and good sense, so no one can fault it. The doubled 合 stresses that it fits on every count.
何去何从	hé qù hé cóng	which way to turn; what course to take — Literally "where to go, what to follow", the weighing of a hard choice between paths. A more literary cousin of 怎么办, asking not just "what to do" but "which direction to commit to". Here 何 is the classical "how / what" that 怎么 replaced in everyday speech.
和光同尘	hé guāng tóng chén	to soften one's light and share the dust: to blend in and live among ordinary people — From the Daoist classics: to dim one's brilliance and mix with the common crowd rather than stand apart. A vision of how the wise move within society, joining its dust instead of towering over it. The ideal of fitting unobtrusively into the social whole.
河东狮吼	hé dōng shī hǒu	the lioness of Hedong roars: a fierce, jealous wife — From a Song poem by Su Shi teasing his friend Chen Jichang (Chen Zao), whose wife's temper made him quail. 河东 (Hedong) names her clan's home east of the Yellow River. Still used, half teasing, for a domineering spouse.
河清海晏	hé qīng hǎi yàn	the river runs clear and the sea is calm: peace under good rule — Since the Yellow River is famously muddy, its running clear is a near-impossible omen of a sage age. Paired with a calm sea, it stands for an era of order and prosperity. Formal, literary register.
鹤立鸡群	hè lì jī qún	a crane standing among chickens: head and shoulders above the rest — The tall white crane towering over a yard of squat fowl. Said of a person whose talent or bearing makes them stand out from an ordinary crowd.
红红火火	hóng hóng huǒ huǒ	red-red fire-fire — flourishing, booming, and full of warmth — A doubled, emphatic form of 红火. The image is a scene so prosperous and lively it seems to glow like fire.
鸿门宴	hóng mén yàn	the Feast at Hong Gate — a banquet set as a trap — The Shiji's most famous set-piece: the banquet at which Xiang Yu's faction plotted to kill the future Han founder Liu Bang, where the sword-dance of 项庄 (项庄舞剑) masked an assassination attempt and Liu Bang escaped by a hair.
后来居上	hòu lái jū shàng	"the latecomer occupies the top" — the newcomer surpasses those who came first — 后来 (later arrival) + 居上 (occupies the upper position). From the Shǐjì (史记), the court historian Sima Qian. A newly promoted official sitting above older ministers in court: the latecomer had outranked his seniors.
后生可畏	hòu shēng kě wèi	"the young are to be feared" — the next generation will surpass those before them — From the Analects of Confucius (论语·子罕): 后生可畏，焉知来者之不如今也？(The young are to be feared , how do we know the ones still to come will not surpass those of today?) 后生 = those born later, the younger generation. 可畏 = worthy of awe, of apprehension.
狐朋狗友	hú péng gǒu yǒu	fox-friends and dog-companions: a bad crowd — Lit: fox-friends, dog-friends. 狐 fox and 狗 dog stand for the sly and the low. The phrase scorns worthless drinking companions and hangers-on, the kind of friends who lead you nowhere good. A parent's word of warning.
湖光山色	hú guāng shān sè	lake light and mountain hues: beautiful lake-and-mountain scenery — A set phrase praising scenic landscape, the stock compliment paid to West Lake and its kin.
虎门销烟	Hǔ mén xiāo yān	the Humen Opium Destruction — Lin Zexu's burning of British opium in 1839 — Commissioner Lin Zexu's destruction of 1,200 tons of British-owned opium at Humen (虎门), Guangdong, in June 1839. The act that triggered the First Opium War. Commemorated as a patriotic symbol in Chinese history education. The date (June 26) is marked as China's National Anti-Drug Day (中国禁毒日).
花好月圆	huā hǎo yu è yuán	flowers in full bloom, moon perfectly round — a moment of perfection and happiness — The classic wedding blessing and New Year wish. Flowers (spring beauty) + full moon (reunion, completeness) = the ideal of a happy, complete life. Often inscribed on wedding gifts and red envelopes.
花红柳绿	huā hóng liǔ lǜ	flowers red, willows green — a riot of bright spring color — 花 huā (flowers) + 红 hóng (red) + 柳 liǔ (willow) + 绿 lǜ (green). A set image of late spring at its most colorful: red blossoms against green willows. By extension it describes anything gaudy and brightly multicolored, sometimes with a faint hint of being overdone. 公园里花红柳绿，游人如织。
花街柳巷	huā jiē liǔ xiàng	flower streets and willow lanes: the pleasure quarters — A classical euphemism for the red-light district, where 花 (flower) and 柳 (willow) stand for courtesans. Literary and old-fashioned; appears in Ming and Qing fiction.
画饼充饥	huà bǐng chōng jī	to draw a cake to ease hunger: to comfort oneself with empty fantasy — Lit: draw-cake-fill-hunger. A painted cake feeds no one. The idiom mocks any plan or promise that satisfies in imagination only, offering the picture of a thing in place of the thing itself.
画虎不成反类犬	huà hǔ bù chéng fǎn lèi quǎn	tried to paint a tiger, ended up with a dog — Colloquial proverb for the same failure: aiming high, imitating without understanding, producing something worse than the original and worse than doing nothing.
话不投机	huà bù tóu jī	the talk does not meet: the conversation does not click — Lit: words-not-matching-the-moment. Said when two people simply cannot find common ground in conversation. The fuller saying 话不投机半句多 adds that "when talk does not click, even half a sentence is too much."
话里有话	huà lǐ yǒu huà	there are words within the words: a remark carries a hidden meaning — Lit: inside-the-words-there-are-words. Said when someone speaks with an implied second meaning, hinting at more than the surface remark admits.
怀才不遇	huái cái bù yù	to harbor talent yet meet no chance: gifted but unrecognized — Lit: cradle-talent-not-encounter. The lament of the able person who never finds a ruler, patron, or era that will use their gifts. A recurring theme in classical poetry, where the unemployed scholar mourns his wasted ability.
坏人坏事	huài rén huài shì	bad people and bad deeds: wrongdoers and their misdeeds — A set four-character phrase pairing 坏人 with 坏事, often in the formula 与坏人坏事作斗争, "to struggle against wrongdoers and wrongdoing." Carries a formal, almost official register.
欢天喜地	huān tiān xǐ dì	wild with joy: overjoyed, beside oneself with delight — Lit: joy-Heaven-glad-Earth, joy that fills the whole sky and ground. Said of someone bursting with happiness at good news, a reunion, or a festival. 一家人欢天喜地过新年 "the family rang in the New Year overjoyed."
欢欣鼓舞	huān xīn gǔ wǔ	elated and inspired: filled with joy and high spirits — Lit: joyful-glad-drum-dance, so cheered that one beats the drum and dances. Used of a group lifted and energized by good news or a great event, joy that rouses people to action.
焕然一新	huàn rán yī xīn	"completely transformed to something new" — completely renewed; a total makeover — 焕然 (huànrán, resplendently bright; the appearance of something that has been renovated or refreshed) + 一新 (completely new). The appearance of something that has undergone a total transformation: 装修完工后，房间焕然一新 (after the renovation was complete, the room was completely transformed).
皇亲国戚	huáng qīn guó qī	imperial relatives and royal in-laws — The emperor's kin and the families connected to the throne by marriage, the privileged inner circle of the imperial house. Now used, often with a sardonic edge, for anyone who enjoys protection or privilege through family connections to power.
恍然大悟	huǎng rán dà wù	suddenly, greatly awakened — the flash of realization — 恍然 (in a flash) + 大悟 (great awakening, 悟 being the enlightenment verb of Chan Buddhism and the Monkey King's name 悟空). The eureka moment: the detective's final chapter, the student when the proof clicks. Chinese lets you borrow the vocabulary of enlightenment for algebra.
回头是岸	huí tóu shì àn	turn your head and there is the shore: it is never too late to repent — Lit: turn-the-head-is-shore. From the Buddhist 苦海无边，回头是岸 ("the sea of suffering is boundless; turn back and the shore is there"). To stop a wrong course and come back is itself salvation.
回心转意	huí xīn zhuǎn yì	to turn the heart and shift the will: to change one's mind, to relent — Lit: turn-heart-shift-intent. Said of someone who softens, reconsiders, or comes back to a relationship or decision they had set aside.
绘声绘色	huì shēng huì sè	painting both sound and color: vivid and lifelike (of a description) — Lit: paint-sound-paint-color. Of telling or describing something so vividly that the listener seems to hear and see it. High praise for a gripping, animated account.
浑然天成	hún rán tiān chéng	seamlessly whole as if formed by nature: without a trace of artifice — Lit: wholly-so-Heaven-made. Praises a work of art or writing so unified that it seems made by nature rather than by human hands, with no visible seams or effort. 浑然 means "in one undivided whole."
活蹦乱跳	huó bèng luàn tiào	alive and bouncing about: brimming with health and energy — Lit: alive-leaping-wildly-jumping. The image of a fish on the deck or a healthy child who cannot keep still. Used for someone bursting with vitality, often after recovering from illness.
活到老，学到老	huó dào lǎo , xu é dào lǎo	live until old, learn until old — lifelong learning — The most common proverb for lifelong learning: you learn for as long as you live. An encouragement to keep studying at any age, and a gentle reminder that no one has ever learned enough to stop. Equivalent in spirit to the English "you're never too old to learn."
活灵活现	huó líng huó xiàn	vivid and lifelike: so vivid it seems to come alive before you — Lit: alive-spirit-alive-appearing, with 活 doubled for emphasis. Describes a story, performance, or portrait so vivid and animated that the thing depicted seems present in the room. High praise for vividness.
火上加油	huǒ shàng jiā yóu	to add oil to the fire — to make a bad situation worse — Lit: fire-above-add-oil. The Chinese equivalent of "adding fuel to the fire." 你别火上加油了 "Don't make things worse." The equivalent English idiom "add fuel to the fire" has exactly the same logic.
鸡蛋里挑骨头	jī dàn lǐ tiāo gǔ tou	to pick bones out of an egg: to nitpick, to find fault where there is none — A vivid colloquial saying: an egg has no bones, so hunting for one means inventing flaws. Used of a critic or boss who complains about everything.
鸡飞蛋打	jī fēi dàn dǎ	the hen flies off and the eggs break: to lose everything, ending up empty-handed — A botched scheme where both the chicken and its eggs are lost. Said when an attempt to grab more leaves you with nothing at all.
鸡飞狗跳	jī fēi gǒu tiào	chickens fly and dogs leap: utter chaos and uproar — A farmyard thrown into panic, hens flapping and dogs bolting. Describes a household or scene in total disorder. Lively and colloquial.
鸡毛蒜皮	jī máo suàn pí	chicken feathers and garlic skins: trivial, petty matters — The kitchen sweepings nobody bothers with. Used to dismiss a quarrel or complaint as not worth the fuss: 别为这点鸡毛蒜皮的事吵架.
积非成是	jī fēi chéng shì	errors accumulated until they harden into accepted norm — Lit: accumulate-wrong-become-right. When a mistake or deviation is repeated so long it calcifies into convention. Applied in linguistics (describing language change), ethics, and institutional analysis. A cautionary concept: not all norms are correct simply because they persist.
积少成多	jī shǎo chéng duō	accumulate the few and it becomes the many — The thrift-and-study chengyu: small savings, daily vocabulary reviews, and minor improvements compound. The financial-app era has given it a second life in savings-product marketing, and teachers deploy it against the student who dismisses ten minutes of practice as pointless.
及第成名	jí dì chéng míng	to pass the exam and make a name: to win rank and renown at once — Lit: reach-rank-become-famous. The double prize of the imperial scholar, to place in the examination and so earn lasting reputation. The dream that drove generations of candidates to the 科举.
急如星火	jí rú xīng huǒ	urgent as a shooting star — desperately urgent — Lit: urgent-like-shooting-star-fire. A shooting star (流星) appears briefly and is gone: something that needs to be acted on instantly before the moment passes. Used in military dispatches and urgent appeals.
己所不欲，勿施于人	jǐ suǒ bù yù , wù shī yú rén	do not impose on others what you yourself do not want — the Silver Rule — Analects 15.24. Confucius called this 恕 shù (reciprocity, forgiveness), the negative formulation of the Golden Rule. One of the most famous sentences in Chinese. Appears in the UN Declaration of Human Rights as a cross-cultural parallel. 己 = oneself; 施 = to impose/apply; 于人 = onto others.
己所不欲，勿施于人。	jǐ suǒ bù yù , wù shī yú rén .	What you do not want done to yourself, do not do to others. — Appears twice in the Analects (12.2, 15.24) as the Confucian formulation of the negative Golden Rule. Called the doctrine of 恕 shù (sympathetic understanding). This formulation was inscribed on the UNESCO building in Paris as one of humanity's foundational ethical principles.
记忆犹新	jì yì yóu xīn	the memory is still fresh: as vivid as if it just happened — Lit: memory-still-new. Said of an event or scene one recalls so clearly that time seems not to have dulled it. 至今记忆犹新 "to this day it remains fresh in my mind."
既生瑜，何生亮？	jì shēng yú , hé shēng liàng ?	Since you bore Zhou Yu, why also bear Zhuge Liang? — The dying lament attributed to the Wu commander 周瑜 Zhou Yu, repeatedly outmatched by his rival Zhuge Liang.
家常便饭	jiā cháng biàn fàn	an everyday home meal: a common, unremarkable occurrence — Lit: home-ordinary-simple-meal. Literally plain home cooking, used figuratively for anything that happens so routinely it is no big deal: 加班对他来说是家常便饭 "working late is routine for him."
家徒四壁	jiā tú sì bì	home with only four walls — extreme poverty; owning nothing — Lit: home-merely-four-walls. 徒 = only; merely; bare. The household contains nothing but the four walls themselves. Used in literature to describe destitution. Often contrasted with 家财万贯 jiācái wànguàn (immense family wealth). A stock phrase for total financial ruin.
家喻户晓	jiā yù hù xiǎo	known to every household — a household name; universally known — Lit: family-understood-household-known. 喻 = understood; 晓 = known; aware. Every family understands it; every household knows it. 这首歌已经家喻户晓了。(This song is now known to everyone.) The go-to phrase for "universally famous" in Chinese.
坚定不移	jiān dìng bù yí	firm and unshakable — Literally "firm and settled, not shifting", steadfast in resolve, never wavering. The 定 ("settled, fixed") at its heart is the same 定 in 一定; here it describes a will that has fixed on its course. The deepest form of the resolve 一定要 voices.
兼爱天下	jiān ài tiān xià	love all under Heaven impartially — the Mohist manifesto in four characters — 兼 jiān (jointly; all together) + 爱 ài (love) + 天下 tiānxià (all under Heaven; the world). The compressed statement of Mozi's central ethical position: love extended equally to every person without gradation by family, state, or social proximity.
见笑	jiàn xiào	to be laughed at: forgive my poor effort — Lit: be-seen laughed-at. A set humble phrase: 让您见笑了 ("I've let you laugh at me") deflects praise for one's own work or skill. Polite, slightly formal register.
江湖骗子	jiāng hú piàn zi	a wandering swindler, a roving con artist — The 江湖 of itinerant healers and fortune-tellers turned into a label for a charlatan who works the crowds.
江郎才尽	jiāng láng cái jìn	Jiang's talent is exhausted — creative powers have run dry; the burnt-out genius — From the Southern Qi dynasty writer Jiang Yan (江淹), famed for his prose, who stopped producing good work later in life. Legend says he dreamed that a man demanded his writing brush back; after that, his talent was gone. 他感觉自己江郎才尽了。
江山如画	jiāng shān rú huà	rivers and mountains like a painting; the land is breathtakingly beautiful — From Su Shi's 念奴娇·赤壁怀古 (Remembering Chibi, to the tune of Nian Nu Jiao), written in 1082 at the site of the famous Three Kingdoms battle. 江山如画，一时多少豪杰 (the rivers and mountains like a painting , how many heroes in that one moment).
将错就错	jiāng cuò jiù cuò	to take the mistake and run with it: to leave a wrong thing as it is — Lit: take-the-error-go-with-the-error. Rather than undo a mistake already made, one accepts it and proceeds on that footing, often because correcting it would cost more than living with it.
教我如何不想她	jiào wǒ rú hé bù xiǎng tā	"How could I not think of her" — Liu Bannong's 1920 poem, set to music by Zhao Yuanren — Not classical, but the most famous early appearance of 她 in print. The poem is addressed to "her", variously read as a lover, the homeland (Liu was abroad in Britain when he wrote it), or both. The song became one of the defining works of early modern Chinese music.
教学相长	jiào xu é xiāng zhǎng	teaching and learning grow each other: the teacher learns by teaching — Lit: teach-learn-mutually-grow. From the Liji (Record of Rites). Teaching exposes what the teacher does not yet understand, so instructing others deepens one's own knowledge; the two advance together.
皆大欢喜	jiē dà huān xǐ	all are greatly delighted — everyone ends up happy — Originally the closing formula of Buddhist sutras, where the assembly rejoices at the teaching and departs; now the phrase for win-win endings, amicable settlements, and comedies. It is also the standard Chinese title of Shakespeare's As You Like It, a translation with better provenance than most.
揭竿而起	jiē gān ér qǐ	to raise a pole and rise up — to start a popular rebellion — 揭 (raise, lift) + 竿 (pole, bamboo staff) + 而起 (and rise up). The idiom originates in the Qin dynasty collapse (221–206 BCE), when the rebel leader Chen Sheng reportedly rallied followers with nothing but a raised pole: no weapons, no banners, no organization, just the act of standing up.
街谈巷议	jiē tán xiàng yì	street talk and lane discussion: common gossip, popular chatter — The talk of the town, idle public discussion of an affair. Carries a faintly dismissive tone, the chatter of ordinary streets as opposed to considered opinion.
街头巷尾	jiē tóu xiàng wěi	street corners and lane ends: all about town, on everyone's lips — Names the public places where people gather and gossip. Often used of news or rumor that has spread everywhere: 这件事在街头巷尾传开了, the story spread to every corner of town.
节节上升	jié jié shàng shēng	"rising section by section" — steady, continuous increase — 节 jié = section; segment; node. Each node advances the rise. Used for prices, temperatures, performance metrics, or any quantity that climbs steadily over time. Common in economic and news reporting.
节外生枝	jié wài shēng zhī	a branch sprouts from the joint: new complications arise unexpectedly — Lit: joint-outside-grow-branch. A side branch springs from the node of a stalk where none was wanted. Said when an already settled matter throws up fresh, unwelcome problems.
节衣缩食	jié yī suō shí	to scrimp on clothes and food: to live frugally and pinch every penny — Lit: economize-clothing-cut-back-food. To deny oneself even basics in order to save, said of those who tighten the belt hard, often to support a family or reach a goal.
结草衔环	jié cǎo xián huán	to knot grass and carry rings: to repay a kindness, even after death — Lit: tie-grass-hold-ring. From two old tales of gratitude: a ghost knotted grass to trip an enemy and save his benefactor, and a magpie brought jade rings to repay a rescuer. Together they stand for never forgetting a debt of kindness.
姐妹情深	jiě mèi qíng shēn	deep sisterly affection — A common modern four-character set phrase for the strong bond between sisters or close female friends. Frequent in titles, toasts, and warm descriptions of female friendship.
借刀杀人	jiè dāo shā rén	borrow a knife to kill a man: destroy a rival by another's hand — The third of the Thirty-Six Stratagems (三十六计). You stay clean while a third party does the deed; still vivid in business and political commentary today.
借题发挥	jiè tí fā huī	seize on a topic to make a wider point: use a pretext to vent — Literally "borrow the subject to elaborate." Common for someone who turns a small matter into a platform for a bigger grievance.
巾帼不让须眉	jīn guó bù ràng xū méi	"the woman's headdress yields nothing to the man's beard" — a woman who is the equal of any man — 巾帼 jīnguó , a woman's hair ornament, by metonymy "woman." 须眉 xūméi: beard and brows, by metonymy "man." Used in praise of female generals (Mulan, Hua Mulan; Liang Hongyu), athletes, and accomplished women.
今非昔比	jīn fēi xī bǐ	the present cannot compare with the past — Literally "today is not what the past compared to," meaning things have changed greatly, usually for the better, so that now far surpasses before. 今 (now) versus 昔 (former times). A common way to mark dramatic progress or transformation.
锦上添花	jǐn shàng tiān huā	"adding flowers on top of brocade" — making something excellent even better — Brocade (锦 jǐn) is already the finest silk, already decorated with woven patterns. Adding flowers on top of it is a gift that was not needed but is delightful. Contrasted with 雪中送炭 (sending coal in the snow), helping someone in genuine need. 锦上添花 is the luxury gesture; 雪中送炭 is the necessary one.
进退两难	jìn tuì liǎng nán	hard to advance and hard to retreat: caught in a dilemma — Lit: advance-retreat-both-hard. To be stuck where neither going forward nor falling back will do, the classic no-win bind. The 进 / 退 pairing makes the trap vivid.
经久不衰	jīng jiǔ bù shuāi	to endure long without fading: standing the test of time — Lit: pass-through long, not declining. Said of a song, a brand, a custom, or a reputation that stays popular and vigorous over many years rather than fading away.
惊魂未定	jīng hún wèi dìng	the startled spirit has not yet settled: still shaken after a fright — Lit: startled-soul-not-yet-settled. Describes someone whose nerves are still rattled in the aftermath of a shock or narrow escape, the heart not yet returned to rest.
惊天动地	jīng tiān dòng dì	"startling heaven, shaking earth" — earth-shaking; of tremendous power or significance — 惊 jīng (to startle; to alarm) + 动 dòng (to move; to shake). When something is so significant that heaven is startled and earth trembles, the hyperbole names genuine magnitude. Used for historic events, monumental achievements, and powerful works. 他们完成了一项惊天动地的壮举。
兢兢业业	jīng jīng yè yè	diligent and careful in one's work — A phrase of high praise for someone who does their work with constant care and seriousness, never cutting corners. 兢兢 conveys cautious diligence; 业业 the steady attention to one's 业 (work, duties). Common in performance reviews and tributes, the model of the conscientious worker.
精诚团结	jīng chéng tuán jié	to unite with wholehearted sincerity: to stand together in good faith — Lit: pure-sincere-unite. A call for a group to bond in genuine, undivided solidarity rather than in mere appearance. 结 here is the uniting sense, the bond made fast among people.
精忠报国	jīng zhōng bào guó	serve the country with utmost loyalty — the supreme patriotic commitment — The four characters said to have been tattooed on Yue Fei's back by his mother (精忠 = utmost loyalty; 报国 = repay/serve the country). Whether the tattooing story is historical or legendary, the phrase became the supreme expression of Chinese patriotic loyalty.
九九八十一难	jiǔ jiǔ bā shí yī nàn	the eighty-one tribulations — a long sequence of trials that must all be passed — From 西游记: the pilgrims must survive exactly eighty-one ordeals (九九, "nine nines," a number of completeness) before the scriptures can be won.
九牛一毛	jiǔ niú yī máo	"one hair from nine oxen" — a trifling amount; a drop in the bucket — 九牛 (nine oxen) and 一毛 (a single hair). A lone hair plucked from nine whole oxen is utterly negligible against the mass it comes from. The phrase comes from Sima Qian, who wrote that his own death would weigh on the world no more than one hair lost from nine oxen.
久病成医	jiǔ bìng chéng yī	long illness makes the patient a doctor — Chronic experience becomes expertise. Said of patients who know their condition better than junior doctors, and by extension of anyone whose long exposure to a problem has made them its reluctant expert: the veteran user who knows the software's bugs better than the developers.
就地取材	jiù dì qǔ cái	to use local materials; to make do with what is immediately available — Lit: just-ground-take-materials. 就地 = "right here on the spot" (from 就 = to approach what is near). 取材 = to gather materials. The idiom celebrates resourcefulness , using what is immediately at hand rather than waiting for something better. The 就 captures the "readiness without delay" quality.
举棋不定	jǔ qí bù dìng	to hold a chess piece without setting it down: to hesitate and waver — Lit: raise-chesspiece-not-settle. The image of a player holding a piece in the air, unable to commit to a move. Said of anyone caught in indecision who cannot fix on a choice.
据理力争	jù lǐ lì zhēng	to argue strongly on principle: to stand on reason and contend hard for it — Lit: rely-on-principle-strongly-contend. To take one's stand on what is right and fight for it in argument, not backing down so long as reason is on your side.
君子不器	jūn zǐ bù qì	the exemplary person is not a vessel — Confucius says the junzi is not a utensil: not defined by function, not limited to one use. The Analects' argument against reducing a person to their usefulness.
开天辟地	kāi tiān pì dì	"to split open heaven and cleave earth" — to create the world; a groundbreaking, unprecedented achievement — From the Pangu myth (盘古 Pángǔ). In the beginning, all was 混沌 hùndùn: undifferentiated chaos. Pangu broke it open. The light, clear elements rose and became Heaven; the heavy, turbid elements sank and became Earth. Pangu stood between them for 18,000 years until the separation was permanent.
开源节流	kāi yuán jié liú	open up the source and restrain the flow: increase income and curb spending — Lit: open-source-restrain-flow. The classic principle of sound finance: widen what comes in while narrowing what goes out. Applied to household budgets, companies, and the state alike.
可歌可泣	kě gē kě qì	worthy of song and worthy of tears — so moving as to deserve both celebration and mourning — 可 + 歌 (to sing) + 可 + 泣 (to weep). Used for heroic, sacrificial stories: deeds of such weight they call for both elegy and lament. 革命先烈的事迹可歌可泣。(The deeds of the revolutionary martyrs are worthy of song and tears.) High formal register.
快马加鞭	kuài mǎ jiā biān	whip a fast horse — press on faster when already moving well — Lit: fast-horse-add-whip. The image is of a courier or cavalry rider who is already galloping but spurs the horse harder anyway. Metaphorically: when momentum is good, don't coast. Used to encourage continued effort or accelerated progress, often in workplace or project contexts.
来得及	lái de jí	to have enough time; to make it in time — A potential complement construction: 来 (come/arrive) + 得 + 及 (reach in time). The negative 来不及 is one of the highest-frequency colloquial expressions. 来不及了！ "There's no time!" Both forms exemplify the potential complement use of 得/不.
来去自如	lái qù zì rú	"coming and going as one pleases" — complete freedom of movement; unhindered — 来去 (coming and going, the full freedom of movement) + 自如 (naturally; freely; as one wishes). The ideal of total freedom: moving in any direction without restriction or obstacle.
来日方长	lái rì fāng cháng	the days to come are still long — there is still much time ahead — 来日 = the coming days, the future (days that come toward us). 方长 = just beginning to be long. A comfort phrase: things not accomplished now can still be done; relationships not deepened yet have time. 我们来日方长，不必着急 "We still have plenty of time ahead , no need to rush."
来者不拒	lái zhě bù jù	turn no one away; welcome all comers; refuse nothing that comes — 来者 = those who come + 不拒 = do not refuse. Originally a statement of generous hospitality or all-accepting policy. Used in commercial contexts (a shop that accepts all customers), in descriptions of a person with an open-door policy, and ironically for someone who takes on too much work.
狼心狗肺	láng xīn gǒu fèi	wolf's heart, dog's lungs: cruel and ungrateful — A vivid curse for someone heartless who repays kindness with betrayal. The wolf and dog organs stand for savagery and baseness. Strong, accusatory register.
浪迹江湖	làng jì jiāng hú	to drift across rivers and lakes: to roam without a fixed home — The romantic image of the wanderer with no settled address, common in martial-arts fiction and travel writing.
老当益壮	lǎo dāng yì zhuàng	old but growing ever stronger — vigorous in old age — Lit: old-yet-increasingly-vigorous. From the Book of the Later Han: the general Ma Yuan said, "A man of ambition grows more vigorous as he grows old; a hero grows stronger with age." Used to praise an older person who retains energy, capability, and drive. A compliment, never ironic.
老马识途	lǎo mǎ shí tú	an old horse knows the road — experience guides better than intelligence — From the Han Feizi: Duke Huan of Qi was lost in the mountains; an advisor suggested releasing an old war horse, which led the army home by the familiar road. Used to justify consulting experienced people rather than relying on theory or intelligence alone. One of the most famous 老 idioms.
老少皆宜	lǎo shào jiē yí	"appropriate for old and young alike" — suitable for everyone — 老 (old) + 少 shào (young) + 皆 (all, both) + 宜 (appropriate, fitting). The old-young pair covers the full lifespan: if something suits both extremes, it suits everyone.
老生常谈	lǎo shēng cháng tán	an old scholar's stock talk: a hackneyed, oft-repeated remark — Lit: old-scholar-often-says. A truism so worn from repetition that it carries little weight, the Chinese equivalent of a tired platitude.
乐极生悲	lè jí shēng bēi	joy at its extreme gives birth to sorrow — From the Records of the Grand Historian: the wit Chunyu Kun warned King Wei of Qi, over wine, that drinking to the limit ends in disorder and joy at its limit turns to grief (酒极则乱，乐极则悲).
离宫别馆	lí gōng bié guǎn	detached palaces and separate lodges: the many residences a ruler kept beyond the main palace — A classical 成语 for the scattered country retreats and travelling-lodges of an emperor or great house. Here 馆 carries its oldest sense, the official lodge where the powerful stayed on the road.
离题万里	lí tí wàn lǐ	to stray ten thousand li from the topic: to wander hopelessly off subject — Lit: leave-topic-ten-thousand-li. The 万里 (ten thousand li) is hyperbole for an enormous distance, so the phrase describes talk or writing that has drifted so far it has lost the thread entirely.
离乡背井	lí xiāng bèi jǐng	to leave one's native land — Literally "to leave the village and turn one's back on the well", to go far from home, often driven by hardship. The 井 (well) stands for the home community that gathered around shared water. Here 离 is the verb of parting at its most poignant, leaving the place one belongs to.
里应外合	lǐ yīng wài hé	"inside responds, outside coordinates" — inside-outside cooperation; a coordinated attack from within and without — 里 (inside) + 应 (yīng, to respond, to correspond) + 外 (outside) + 合 (to coordinate, to join). A military and strategic idiom: the force inside a city or position responds to the force attacking from outside, each coordinating with the other.
理所当然	lǐ suǒ dāng rán	as it should be by reason; a matter of course — Literally "what reason makes ought-to-be-so," something so right and natural that it needs no defense. 当然 ("of course") and 应当 ("ought to") share the 当 of fitting necessity. Sometimes used critically, of someone who takes a kindness as 理所当然 ("for granted").
理一分殊	lǐ yī fēn shū	Principle is one, but its manifestations are many — the unity of diversity in Neo-Confucian metaphysics — Formulated by Cheng Yi, developed by Zhu Xi. 理一 = principle is singular and universal; 分殊 = its particularizations are diverse. The classic image: the moon reflected in ten thousand pools of water.
力不从心	lì bù cóng xīn	strength falls short of the heart: the will is there but the ability is not — Lit: strength-not-follow-heart. Said when one wants to do something but lacks the strength or means to carry it through, the gap between intention and capacity.
力所能及	lì suǒ néng jí	within one's power; as far as one is able — Literally "what one's strength can reach," the things that lie within one's ability to do. The bright mirror of 无能为力: 我会尽力所能及 ("I'll do all I can"). Again built on 能, the capacity at the root of "possible."
练兵秣马	liàn bīng mò mǎ	drill the troops and feed the horses: ready oneself for the contest — 练 here is its oldest active sense, to drill. The image is an army putting in the reps before battle, so the phrase travels easily to any team or person training hard ahead of a test.
练达老成	liàn dá lǎo chéng	practiced and worldly, mature and steady — 练达 means seasoned in the ways of the world; pairs with 老成 (mature beyond one's years). A literary compliment for someone whose poise comes from experience, not age alone.
两败俱伤	liǎng bài jù shāng	"both defeated, both hurt" — a mutually destructive outcome; both sides lose — 两败 (both defeated) + 俱伤 (both injured). A conflict in which neither side wins and both come away damaged, the lose-lose result of a fight pushed too far: 这样争下去只会两败俱伤 (going on fighting like this will only hurt both sides).
两情相悦	liǎng qíng xiāng yu è	two hearts delighting in each other — mutual affection — A classical phrase for mutual romantic fondness, where 悦 yuè (to delight in, akin to 喜欢) is shared by both sides. It names the ideal of love that is returned, two people who like and are gladdened by each other, and is often quoted in the context of relationships and marriage.
量入为出	liàng rù wéi chū	measure income to set spending: live within your means — 量 liàng (to gauge) + 入 rù (income, what comes in) + 为 wéi (to make, to determine) + 出 chū (expenditure, what goes out). Let the size of your income decide the size of your spending.
零零星星	líng líng xīng xīng	"scattered like stray stars" — sparse and scattered; bits and pieces here and there — A reduplication of 零星 (scattered, sporadic), itself 零 (scattered drops) plus 星 (stars, scattered points of light).
六月飞霜	liù yu è fēi shuāng	"frost flies in the sixth month" — a sign of grievous injustice — 六月 (the sixth lunar month, high summer) plus 飞霜 (flying frost). Frost in midsummer is an impossibility that heaven produces only in response to a wrong so great it disturbs the natural order.
路不拾遗	lù bù shí yí	"on the road no one picks up what others have dropped": the mark of a well-governed society — From the Hánfēizǐ (Legalist text, 3rd century BCE), describing the ideal state under good rule. Paired with 夜不闭户 yè bù bì hù ("at night doors are not bolted").
乱七八糟	luàn qī bā zāo	"chaotic seven, messy eight" — in complete disorder; a total mess — 乱 (chaotic) + 七八 (seven-eight) + 糟 (rotten, spoiled). One of the most common expressions in spoken Mandarin for utter disorder, whether of a messy room, a tangled situation, or muddled thinking. The embedded 七八 again uses the clashing seven-and-eight to mean jumbled, every-which-way.
马马虎虎	mǎ mǎ hū hū	horse-horse tiger-tiger: so-so, careless, passable — The doubled form of 马虎. Used both for sloppy work and, self-deprecatingly, to answer "how are you?" with a shrugging "just so-so."
满街都是圣人	mǎn jiē dōu shì shèng rén	saints fill the streets — everyone already has the innate moral knowledge to be a sage — Said to have been uttered by Wang Yangming after a student asked skeptically whether ordinary people could really possess 良知. His response: walk out onto any busy street and observe; anyone who acts from their innate moral sense in that moment is a sage.
满腔热血	mǎn qiāng rè xu è	a chest full of hot blood: brimming with fervor — 满腔 a full chest, 热血 hot blood. Describes youthful idealism and the will to throw oneself into a cause. Warm and slightly literary in tone.
满室生辉	mǎn shì shēng huī	the whole room glows — a radiant, welcoming presence fills the space — Lit: room-full-give-off-radiance. Used for a beautiful person or a remarkable guest whose presence lights up a room. A formal compliment in social and literary contexts.
满载而归	mǎn zài ér guī	to return fully laden — to come home with rich gains — 满载 (loaded full) + 而归 (and return). The image of Xuanzang re-entering Chang'an with 657 manuscripts after sixteen years abroad. Now a common phrase for any return rich in results , from a successful trip, a fruitful search, or a profitable venture. The opposite is 一无所获 "to come back empty-handed."
漫不经心	màn bù jīng xīn	careless and inattentive: with no thought passing through the mind — Lit: idly not passing-through the heart. 经心 means to take to heart, to attend to. To do something 漫不经心 is to do it absent-mindedly, paying it no real attention.
美不胜收	měi bù shèng shōu	beauty beyond what the eye can take in — overwhelmingly beautiful — Lit: beauty-not-able-to-receive/contain. Used for spectacular natural scenery, festivals, or exhibitions where there is simply too much beauty to absorb at once.
门当户对	mén dāng hù duì	matched gates and doors: of equal social standing (for a marriage) — Lit: gate-matching-door-matching. The old principle that the two families in a marriage should be of comparable rank and wealth. Still used, sometimes critically, of pairing by status.
门庭若市	mén tíng ruò shì	the gateway like a marketplace: thronged with visitors — Lit: gate-courtyard-like-market. Said of a household or office so busy with callers that the entrance resembles a crowded market. Its opposite is 门可罗雀, a gate so deserted you could net sparrows in it.
扪心自问	mén xīn zì wèn	"to press one's hand to the heart and question oneself" — honest self-examination; to look inward without flinching — 扪 mén (to press or feel with the hand) + 心 + 自 (self) + 问 (to question). The gesture of placing one's hand over one's heart and asking honestly: what do I know? what did I actually do? It is the physical enactment of consulting 良心 directly, without evasion.
懵懵懂懂	měng měng dǒng dǒng	muddled and only half-aware: in a fog — A vivid reduplication pairing 懵 (měng, dazed) with 懂 (dǒng, understand) to describe a hazy, half-comprehending state, like a child too young to grasp what is happening or a person fresh out of sleep.
孟母三迁	Mèng mǔ sān qiān	Mencius's mother moves three times — the importance of environment in education — Not from the Mencius text itself but from the Lienüzhuan 列女传 (Biographies of Exemplary Women), attributed to Liu Xiang 刘向 (77–6 BCE).
迷途知返	mí tú zhī fǎn	To Realise You Are Lost and Turn Back — Four characters that describe one of the most human experiences: taking a wrong road, recognising it, and finding the way home. The Chinese idiom for course-correction done with dignity.
面不改色	miàn bù gǎi sè	"face doesn't change color" — unruffled; keeping composure under pressure; poker-faced — Lit: face-not-change-color. The mark of composure under pressure , your face doesn't betray fear, surprise, or discomfort. 他面不改色地接受了批评，继续进行演讲。"He received the criticism without changing expression and continued his speech." Often admired as a sign of courage or self-discipline.
面面俱到	miàn miàn jù dào	every aspect covered; thorough in all directions — 面 miàn (face; surface; aspect) + 面 + 俱 jù (all; together) + 到 (arrived at). Arrival has reached every face, every surface, every angle of the matter. Used to praise comprehensive coverage , a plan that misses nothing, a speech that addresses every concern, an analysis that leaves no gap.
面有菜色	miàn yǒu cài sè	a face with the color of greens: gaunt and sallow from hunger — Lit: face-has-vegetable-color. 菜色 is the wan, greenish pallor of someone who has eaten nothing but wild greens, with no grain or meat. The phrase paints a face worn down by famine or hardship.
妙笔生花	miào bǐ shēng huā	a wondrous brush gives birth to flowers — gifted writing that makes words bloom — Lit: wondrous-brush-give birth-flowers. From a Tang dynasty story about the poet Li Bai dreaming that his brush sprouted flowers, foretelling his literary gifts. Used to praise writing of exceptional beauty or imagination , prose or verse that transforms language into something alive.
妙手回春	miào shǒu huí chūn	miraculous hands bring back the spring; masterly healing — 妙手 (wondrous hands) + 回春 (return spring, restore life's season). The stock phrase of gratitude to a doctor, embroidered on the silk banners (锦旗 jǐnqí) that patients present to hospital departments, a living custom: the walls of a respected clinic hang red with them.
名不副实	míng bù fù shí	name does not match reality — the condition 正名 diagnoses — 名 (name) + 不 (does not) + 副 (match, correspond to) + 实 (reality). The set phrase for the gap that 正名 targets.
名存实亡	míng cún shí wáng	existing in name but not in reality — Literally "the name survives, the reality is dead," describing something that keeps its title or form while its substance has vanished, an alliance, a rule, or an institution that is hollow inside. The 名 versus 实 contrast, the gap between what is called and what truly is, that 其实 itself points to.
名实相符	míng shí xiāng fú	name and reality correspond — the goal of 正名 — 名 (name) + 实 (reality) + 相符 (mutually correspond, match). The affirmative formulation of what 正名 aims to restore. Where 名不副实 names the problem and 名正言顺 names the condition of legitimacy in action, 名实相符 states the standard of alignment itself.
明察秋毫	míng chá qiū háo	to perceive the finest detail — exceptional discernment — Lit: bright-observe-autumn-hair-tip. 秋毫 = the impossibly fine tip of an animal's autumn coat. Used of leaders and judges who miss nothing. Its opposite: 不见舆薪 bùjiàn yú xīn "unable to see a cartload of firewood", missing what's obvious.
明日黄花	míng rì huáng huā	yesterday's news — past its prime, no longer relevant — Lit: tomorrow's chrysanthemum. Chrysanthemums peak at the Double Ninth Festival (重阳节); by tomorrow they are wilted and past. Used of news, opportunities, or people who are no longer timely.
明月几时有？把酒问青天。	míng yu è jǐ shí yǒu ? bǎ jiǔ wèn qīng tiān .	When did the bright moon first appear? Cup in hand, I ask the blue sky. — Opening of Su Shi's 水调歌头 (Prelude to Water Melody), written on the Mid-Autumn Festival while missing his younger brother Su Zhe.
明哲保身	míng zhé bǎo shēn	the wise protect themselves; prudent self-preservation — From the Book of Odes, where it praised wise self-conduct. Two millennia of court politics turned it ambivalent: today it usually criticizes the colleague who sees the problem and says nothing, keeping their own 身 safe. A compliment in the Zhou dynasty, a workplace accusation now.
明知故犯	míng zhī gù fàn	to knowingly do wrong: to break a rule while fully aware it is wrong — 明 (míng, clearly) + 知 (zhī, to know) + 故 (deliberately, the willed-action sense) + 犯 (fàn, to violate, to offend). To commit an offense while clearly knowing it is forbidden, the opposite of an honest mistake.
明知故问	míng zhī gù wèn	to ask knowing the answer — a rhetorical or teasing question — Lit: clearly-know-yet-ask. For when someone already knows the answer but asks anyway, either to tease, to confirm, or to draw out a confession. 你明知故问！"You already know the answer; why are you asking?"
摸着石头过河	mō zhe shí tou guò hé	crossing the river by feeling for stones — Deng's gradualist pragmatism — The most important political metaphor of the reform era. 摸 (to grope, to feel one's way) + 着 (progressive aspect) + 石头 (stones) + 过河 (cross the river).
莫须有	mò xū yǒu	perhaps he did — a charge without evidence; judicial murder by innuendo — Chancellor Qin Hui's response when asked what evidence existed for Yue Fei's treason charge: 莫须有, variously translated as "perhaps," "maybe there was a reason," or "need it necessarily be?" The phrase captures the essence of political show-trial logic: the accusation is sufficient; evidence is irrel…
墨守成规	mò shǒu chéng guī	to stick rigidly to established rules — The natural antonym of 与时俱进. 墨守 refers to Mozi's legendary skill at defensive warfare: holding a position without yielding. The phrase criticizes inflexibility and mechanical adherence to convention.
南辕北辙	nán yuán běi zhé	"going south in the shafts, tracks heading north" — completely self-contradictory — From the Zhànguó Cè: a man in the state of Wèi claimed he was going to the state of Chǔ (which lies south) but pointed his carriage north. When told he was heading the wrong way, he replied that his horse was fast, his driver skilled, and he had plenty of provisions, all the wrong justifications.
内外有别	nèi wài yǒu bié	inside and outside are distinct: insiders and outsiders are treated differently — Lit: inside-outside-have-distinction. The principle that there is a proper line between those within a family, group, or nation and those without, and that the two are not handled the same way.
能者多劳	néng zhě duō láo	the capable bear the heavier burden; the able do more work — 能者 = the capable one; one who can + 多劳 = works more, bears more labor. A maxim that can be used approvingly (the talented should take on more responsibility) or ironically (the competent get punished with extra tasks).
泥菩萨过河	ní pú sà guò hé	a clay Bodhisattva crossing a river — unable to save even oneself — Lit: clay-bodhisattva-cross-river. 菩萨 púsà (Bodhisattva) is made of clay , crossing water would destroy it. Used to say someone is in no position to help others when they can barely save themselves. 泥菩萨过河，自身难保。
你觉得呢？	nǐ ju é de ne ?	What do you think? — An extremely common conversational turn, throwing the question back to the other person. The particle 呢 ne softens it and invites their view. One of the most natural ways to ask for an opinion in spoken Chinese, and a good phrase to keep a conversation going.
拈花微笑	niān huā wēi xiào	holding up a flower, smiling faintly — wordless understanding — Chan's founding legend, recorded in Song-era lamp histories: the Buddha silently held up a flower before the assembly, and only Mahākāśyapa smiled, receiving the transmission outside words. Cited for any perfect unspoken communication between two people who need no explanation.
