宁为玉碎，不为瓦全	níng wéi yù suì , bù wéi wǎ quán	Better to be shattered jade than intact tile — choose integrity over ignoble survival — Lit: rather be jade-shattered, not be tile-whole. The contrast is between two kinds of material: jade, which is rare, beautiful, and breaks rather than bends; and roof tile (瓦 wǎ), which is common, dull, and merely functional.
牛刀小试	niú dāo xiǎo shì	a small test of the ox-cleaver: great talent on a minor task — From the cook Páo Dīng of the Zhuāngzǐ, whose ox-carving blade glides through every joint. A heavy cleaver lightly tried, said when a capable person handles an easy job and shows the skill in reserve.
弄假成真	nòng jiǎ chéng zhēn	a pretense becomes real — what was performed turns out to be true — Lit: do-fake-become-real. For situations where pretending leads to the actual outcome: fake engagement turns into a real one, a play-acted role becomes the person's real life.
排兵布队	pái bīng bù duì	to arrange soldiers and deploy units: to marshal forces — A military set phrase, sibling to 排兵布阵. Describes a commander laying out troops in formation; now applied to organizing a team or lineup before a contest or launch.
跑了和尚跑不了庙	pǎo le hé shàng pǎo bu liǎo miào	"the monk may run, but the temple cannot run" — even if the person flees, the institution stays put — A folk saying, not a classical chengyu, but functionally fixed. The proverb captures a specific reasoning about accountability: a debtor or wrongdoer may try to flee, but the property, family, or institution they are tied to will remain, and can be held responsible.
朋比为奸	péng bǐ wéi jiān	to band together for evil: to collude in wrongdoing — Lit: friends-allying to-do evil. 朋比 is to band together as a clique, 为奸 to commit villainy. A sharply negative idiom for cronies who join forces for corrupt or treacherous ends, the dark side of company kept on a level footing.
朋友遍天下	péng you biàn tiān xià	to have friends all over the world — A warm saying praising someone with a wide circle of friends everywhere. 遍 biàn means "all over," 天下 "under heaven, the whole world." Often paired with the encouraging proverb 在家靠父母，出门靠朋友 ("at home you rely on parents; away from home you rely on friends"), which captures the practical importance of…
披星戴月	pī xīng dài yu è	cloaked in stars, capped with the moon — toiling from before dawn until after dark — To wear the stars as a cape and the moon as a hat is to be outdoors working while they are up. Said admiringly of farmers, delivery riders, and anyone on the early-and-late grind: 他披星戴月地工作，就为了孩子上大学。He works from starlight to moonlight, all so his child can attend university.
劈头盖脸	pī tóu gài liǎn	splitting the head and covering the face: full in the face, without holding back — Describes a torrent that lands straight on you, a downpour of rain or a barrage of scolding, leaving no chance to dodge.
平分秋色	píng fēn qiū sè	to split the autumn colors evenly: to share equally, to be evenly matched — Lit: evenly-divide-autumn-scenery. Two sides each take half, as if dividing the beauty of autumn. Used when rivals are so closely matched that neither comes out ahead.
萍水相逢	píng shuǐ xiāng féng	to meet by chance: like duckweed drifting together on water — Lit: duckweed-water mutually-meet. Floating duckweed has no fixed place, so the phrase describes strangers who meet briefly by accident and then drift apart again.
普度众生	pǔ dù zhòng shēng	to universally deliver all sentient beings from suffering — A Buddhist phrase (from Guanyin's vow) applied also to Mazu, who is often assimilated with the Goddess of Mercy in folk practice. 普度 pǔdù = universally deliver; 众生 zhòngshēng = all living beings.
七上八下	qī shàng bā xià	"seven up, eight down" — nervous and unsettled; anxious about the outcome — Seven and eight are the neighbors of ten who cannot agree on direction. Seven goes up, eight goes down: the image is of something that cannot find rest, oscillating without resolution. The idiom describes the feeling of anxiety before a result, the inner turbulence of waiting without knowing.
七嘴八舌	qī zuǐ bā shé	"seven mouths, eight tongues" — everyone talking at once; a clamor of voices — 七嘴 (seven mouths) and 八舌 (eight tongues): so many people speaking at the same time that no single voice can be made out. Describes a crowd all offering opinions at once, a noisy discussion where everyone interrupts everyone.
齐心协力	qí xīn xié lì	to make a concerted effort — Literally "aligned hearts, joined strength", everyone striving together as one. A close cousin of 同心协力, stressing that all hearts are lined up in the same direction. The collective togetherness that 一起 points toward when a group acts as a team.
恰到好处	qià dào hǎo chù	just right; arriving at exactly the right point — 恰 qià (just; exactly) + 到 dào (arrive at) + 好 hǎo (good; right) + 处 chù (place; point). Arriving at precisely the right place: the perfectly calibrated amount, the perfectly timed remark, the execution that is neither too much nor too little.
千方百计	qiān fāng bǎi jì	a thousand methods and a hundred schemes: by every conceivable means — Lit: thousand-methods-hundred-plans. Stronger than 想方设法, often with a hint that the effort is dogged or even scheming. 千方百计地想办法 "to try every trick in the book."
千军万马	qiān jūn wàn mǎ	"a thousand armies, ten thousand horses" — an overwhelming force; a vast host — 千军 (a thousand armies) and 万马 (ten thousand horses): a military host so large it cannot be counted. The phrase pictures an unstoppable onrush of force and is applied to any overwhelming mass, an army, a flood of competitors, a tide of demand.
千钧一发	qiān jūn yī fà	"a thousand jun hanging by one hair" — a moment of extreme peril; hanging by a thread — 千钧 (a thousand jun, an enormous weight, the 钧 being an ancient heavy measure) suspended by 一发 (a single hair). The image is of a crushing weight held by the thinnest possible thread, an instant of utmost danger in which the slightest thing decides catastrophe or escape.
千人千面	qiān rén qiān miàn	a thousand people, a thousand faces — each one different — Often used to describe the terracotta warriors, whose faces are all individual. As a general chengyu it means that every person (or every case, or every product) is distinct, with no two alike, and it can praise rich variety or warn against treating different things as the same.
千丝万缕	qiān sī wàn lǚ	a thousand threads and ten thousand strands: countless intricate connections — Lit: thousand-silk-ten-thousand-threads. Used of relationships or matters knit together by innumerable fine ties: 千丝万缕的联系 "connections beyond counting." The literal silk threads behind every abstract 系.
谦谦君子	qiān qiān jūn zǐ	"the humbly modest exemplary person" — the junzi whose modesty is doubled and genuine — From the 易经 (Yijing), first line of hexagram 15 谦 Qiān: 谦谦君子，用涉大川，吉 (The humbly modest exemplary person , it is advantageous to cross the great river. Good fortune). The doubling of 谦谦 marks the quality as thorough and genuine rather than surface-level.
前仆后继	qián pū hòu jì	"the front one falls, the next one follows" — wave after wave of sacrifice; undeterred succession — 前 (front; those ahead) + 仆 pū (to fall forward, to fall face-down) + 后 (back; those behind) + 继 jì (to continue; to succeed). Those in the front ranks fall; those behind step up to continue.
前因后果	qián yīn hòu guǒ	the full cause and effect — Literally "the prior cause and the later result", the whole chain from why to what-happened. To know the 前因后果 is to understand a matter completely, from the reason a 为什么 seeks to the outcome it explains. 因 (cause) and 果 (effect, "fruit") are the two poles of explanation.
亲贤臣，远小人	qīn xián chén , yuǎn xiǎo rén	keep worthy ministers close and petty men far — From Zhuge Liang's Chushibiao (出师表), the memorial he submitted before the northern campaigns, diagnosing why the Former Han rose and the Later Han fell.
琴瑟和鸣	qín sè hé míng	qin and se playing in harmony — a happy and loving marriage — 琴 qín (seven-string zither) and 瑟 sè (larger twenty-five string zither) are the classical paired instruments. When they sound together in harmony, they are the image of a couple in accord. A literary idiom for marital happiness, used in wedding toasts and poetry.
青出于蓝	qīng chū yú lán	The Student Surpasses the Teacher — Indigo is made from the indigo plant, yet it is bluer than the plant itself. Xunzi's image for the student who surpasses the teacher through sustained effort.
青出于蓝而胜于蓝	qīng chū yú lán ér shèng yú lán	Blue comes from indigo but surpasses indigo — the student surpasses the teacher — From 劝学 (Encouraging Learning), the opening chapter of the Xunzi. The original context is an argument about the power of education to transform: the product (blue dye) surpasses its source (the indigo plant) through processing.
轻举妄动	qīng jǔ wàng dòng	to act rashly and recklessly: to move without due thought — Lit: light-lifting-reckless-moving. A warning against hasty, ill-considered action, often 不要轻举妄动 "do not act rashly," said when caution and patience are called for.
清真	qīng zhēn	pure and true — the Chinese term for halal and for Islam's monotheism — 清 qīng (pure, clear) + 真 zhēn (true, genuine). The standard Chinese translation of the Islamic concept of divine purity and absolute transcendence. Applied in Chinese to: halal food (清真饮食), mosques (清真寺 , "pure truth temple"), and informally to Islamic identity.
情不自禁	qíng bù zì jīn	unable to restrain one's feelings: cannot help oneself — Lit: feeling-not-self-restrain. When emotion wells up too strongly to hold back: 她情不自禁地哭了 "she could not help crying." 禁 here is jīn, "to bear; to restrain."
穷途末路	qióng tú mò lù	"the road's end, the last stretch": at the end of one's resources; cornered, no way forward — 穷 qióng (exhausted) + 途 tú (way) + 末 mò (final) + 路 lù (road). Used for someone whose options have run out: a bankrupt business, a defeated army, a person at the end of their rope. Slightly literary in register.
求同存异	qiú tóng cún yì	seek common ground while keeping differences: agree where you can, set the rest aside — Lit: seek-sameness-preserve-difference. A principle of diplomacy and negotiation: build on what is shared without forcing every disagreement to be resolved first.
曲则全	qū zé quán	yield and you will be preserved — the paradox of strength through softness — Daodejing 22: 曲则全，枉则直，洼则盈. "Yield and you will be preserved; bend and you will be straightened; hollow and you will be filled." The full paradoxical sequence of Daoist counterintuition.
屈指可数	qū zhǐ kě shǔ	countable on bent fingers, a mere handful — 屈指 (bend the fingers) + 可数 (can be counted). The vivid opposite of 不计其数: so few you can tally them on one hand. 像他这样的人才屈指可数 "Talents like him can be counted on one hand." Note 数 here is shǔ (to count), the verb reading, against shù (number) in 不计其数.
取经	qǔ jīng	to fetch the scriptures — to go and learn from a master source — Literally "to obtain the sutras," from Xuanzang's mission and its retelling in 西游记.
取长补短	qǔ cháng bǔ duǎn	take strengths to補 compensate weaknesses — learn from others — Lit: take-long-补-short. The standard phrase for learning from others to compensate for one's own deficiencies. 互相取长补短 "learn from each other's strengths to make up for each other's weaknesses" is the common collaborative formulation.
去而复返	qù ér fù fǎn	"to go and then come back again" — to depart and return unexpectedly — 去 qù (to leave) + 而 ér (but; and then) + 复 fù (again; to return) + 返 fǎn (to turn back). Someone who had departed comes back, often unexpectedly or after a significant interval.
去伪存真	qù wěi cún zhēn	"remove the false, preserve the true" — to sift out falsehood and keep what is genuine — 去 wěi = send away the false. 存 cún = preserve; 真 zhēn = the true. The epistemological program of discernment: not just believing what you are told but actively removing the counterfeit to preserve what is real.
全力以赴	quán lì yǐ fù	to go all out: to throw one's full strength at a task — Lit: with-full-strength-to-go-toward. To commit every ounce of effort to something, holding nothing back. The everyday phrase for total commitment, whether to a contest, a project, or a rescue.
全心全意	quán xīn quán yì	with whole heart and whole mind: wholeheartedly, with total devotion — Lit: whole-heart-whole-intent. The 心 (heart) and 意 (intent) doubled to mean nothing held back: 全心全意为人民服务 "to serve the people wholeheartedly." The strongest everyday way to say one is fully committed.
热泪盈眶	rè lèi yíng kuàng	hot tears filling the eye sockets: moved to tears — 热泪 hot tears, 盈眶 brimming the eye rims. For the moment emotion wells up and the eyes flood. Used for reunions, awards, and stirring speeches.
人定胜天	rén dìng shèng tiān	human will can overcome heaven — perseverance defeats fate — The Maoist rejoinder to classical fatalism. 人定 = human resolution and determination; 胜 = to overcome; 天 = heaven/fate. Against the traditional acceptance of 天命, this phrase asserts that collective human effort can reshape nature and destiny.
人面兽心	rén miàn shòu xīn	a human face with the heart of a beast: a cruel person behind a civil mask — Not built on 社 itself, but a classic measure of how a person fits or fails the bonds of human society: someone who looks like one of us yet behaves with brute cruelty. A reminder that belonging to a 社会 is judged by conduct, not appearance.
人山人海	rén shān rén hǎi	a mountain of people, a sea of people — an enormous crowd — Lit: person-mountain-person-sea. The go-to phrase for describing massive crowds: national holidays, concerts, train stations during Spring Festival rush. Pairing 山 (mountain) with 海 (sea), the two largest natural forms, captures the sense of scale without irony.
人算不如天算	rén suàn bù rú tiān suàn	human plans yield to Heaven's plans — fate overrides strategy — Lit: human calculations are no match for Heaven's calculations. A fatalist proverb at the heart of folk cosmology: 谋事在人，成事在天 (planning is human, success is Heaven's). Used when the best-laid plans go awry.
人心向背	rén xīn xiàng bèi	whether hearts face toward or turn away: popular support or opposition — Lit: people's-hearts, facing-or-backing. 向 is to face toward (support), 背 to turn one's back (oppose). The phrase names the balance of public sentiment that decides whether a cause or a ruler will stand.
人在曹营心在汉	rén zài Cáo yíng xīn zài Hàn	body in Cao's camp, heart in Han — physically present but mentally elsewhere; loyalty divided — From Romance of the Three Kingdoms: Guan Yu was held in Cao Cao's camp but his heart remained loyal to Liu Bei's Han cause. The 在…在 parallel structure contrasts physical location (body) with emotional location (heart).
人在江湖身不由己	rén zài jiāng hú shēn bù yóu jǐ	in the rivers-and-lakes world, one's body is not one's own — once entered, you are bound by its rules — The defining 江湖 saying. Used seriously to explain why one cannot simply walk away from obligations , and humorously to explain why you have to attend yet another obligatory dinner, meeting, or social engagement.
任人宰割	rèn rén zǎi gē	to be at the mercy of others; to be slaughtered at will by whoever wants to — Lit: let-people-slaughter-carve. The extreme end of the 被 spectrum , not just being acted upon but being vulnerable to anyone who chooses to exploit you. 宰割 = to slaughter and carve up (animals, and by extension, the weak).
日积月累	rì jī yu è lěi	accumulate day by day, build up month by month — the power of small consistent effort — Lit: day-accumulate-month-build. The Chinese formulation of compounding: small daily efforts accumulate into transformative results over time. Used to encourage consistent study, practice, and work. 日积月累，功到自然成 "accumulate consistently and success will naturally follow."
日新月异	rì xīn yu è yì	changing by the day and the month — rapid, ceaseless progress — Literally "new each day, different each month," for change so fast it is visible from one stretch of time to the next. The standard phrase for the breakneck pace of modern development, especially technology and the transformation of cities, the felt texture of living in the 现代 age.
融会贯通	róng huì guàn tōng	"melt together, thread through": to master a subject by integrating all its parts — 融 róng (to melt; to merge) + 会 huì (to come together) + 贯 guàn (to thread through; to run a string through) + 通 (to penetrate fully).
如火如荼	rú huǒ rú tú	like fire and like white reeds: in full, blazing swing — Lit: like-fire-like-white-flowers. 荼 is the white flower of a reed. From an account of an army arrayed in red and white, the phrase now describes a movement or campaign surging ahead at full intensity.
如日中天	rú rì zhōng tiān	like the sun at its zenith — at the height of power or fame — Lit: like-sun-middle-sky. Used to describe someone or something at the apex of their power, influence, or popularity. 他的事业如日中天 "his career is at its zenith." Can refer to a person, a dynasty, a company, or a cultural trend.
如释重负	rú shì zhòng fù	as if a heavy load were set down: deeply relieved — Lit: as-if-releasing-a-heavy-burden. The feeling of relief once a worry or duty is finally over, the body lightening as the weight comes off. 长舒一口气 "to let out a long breath" is its companion image.
如鱼得水	rú yú dé shuǐ	like a fish that has found water — to be in one's element; perfectly suited to one's environment — A fish out of water dies; a fish in water is entirely itself. This chengyu describes the state of someone who has found the exact environment where they thrive , a new job that suits them perfectly, a community where they feel at home. 他在新公司如鱼得水，如虎添翼。
如愿以偿	rú yuàn yǐ cháng	to achieve one's wish; things went as hoped — The positive counterpart: wishes fulfilled, intention and outcome aligned. 如愿以偿 and 事与愿违 are natural antonyms: the same structure (relation of wish to outcome) with opposite results. Pairing them in commentary about uncertain situations (whether things went well or not) is a common rhetorical move.
锐不可当	ruì bù kě dāng	too sharp to be withstood: an irresistible force — Lit: so sharp it cannot be matched. Here 当 means to stand against or block. Said of an advance, an army, or a momentum that nothing can stop.
瑞雪兆丰年	ruì xu ě zhào fēng nián	a timely snow foretells a good harvest — 瑞 ruì (auspicious) + 雪 xuě (snow) + 兆 zhào (to portend) + 丰年 fēngnián (a year of abundance). A farmer's proverb with real agronomy behind it: a heavy winter snow insulates the wheat from killing cold, kills overwintering pests, and melts into spring soil moisture.
塞翁失马	sài wēng shī mǎ	The Old Man at the Frontier Loses His Horse — His horse ran off. His neighbours grieved. He shrugged. The Daoist parable that became China's word for the unknowability of fortune.
三更半夜	sāng ēng bàn yè	the dead of night — the third watch, past midnight — 三更 (third night watch, 11 pm to 1 am) + 半夜 (middle of the night). The 更 is gēng, first tone. A fixed phrase for any unreasonably late hour. 你三更半夜打电话干什么? "What are you calling at this hour for?" Still in active everyday use; most speakers do not connect it to the premodern watch system.
三顾茅庐	sān gù máo lú	"three visits to the thatched cottage" — to seek out a person of talent with great persistence and sincerity — The most celebrated episode from Romance of the Three Kingdoms. Liu Bei, ruler of Shu, visits the hermit Zhuge Liang three times at his remote thatched cottage before Zhuge Liang agrees to leave seclusion and serve as his strategist.
三人成虎	sān rén chéng hǔ	three people make a tiger — A minister warns his king: if three people all claim there is a tiger in the marketplace, even the skeptical will believe it. The Zhanguo Ce parable of rumor that outlasts the warning against it.
三头六臂	sān tóu liù bì	"three heads and six arms" — superhuman ability; able to do many things at once — 三头 (three heads) and 六臂 (six arms), the form of certain fierce Buddhist and Daoist deities such as Nezha, who can fight and act on many fronts at once.
三心二意	sān xīn èr yì	"three hearts, two intentions" — indecisive; half-hearted; wavering — Three hearts pulling in different directions, two sets of intentions competing: the image is of someone who cannot commit. Used to criticize a lack of decisiveness or sincerity in a relationship, a task, or a position. 做事不能三心二意 (You can't do things half-heartedly).
三阳开泰	sān yáng kāi tài	three yang lines open into prosperity: an auspicious fresh start, a New Year blessing — From the hexagram 泰 (peace) in the Book of Changes, whose lower three lines are 阳, marking yang rising as winter turns to spring. A traditional New Year greeting wishing renewal and good fortune for the year ahead.
色厉内荏	sè lì nèi rěn	fierce in appearance but cowardly within — all bark and no bite — Lit: color-fierce-inside-timid. A Confucian observation from the Analects: those who appear stern and imposing externally (色厉) but are weak-willed inside (内荏). The opposite of the virtuous person, whose outer bearing matches inner substance.
僧多粥少	sēng duō zhōu shǎo	many monks, little porridge — too many claimants for scarce resources — The monastery breakfast as economic model. Used for job markets (百个人抢一个岗位，僧多粥少), housing, research funding, and concert tickets. The pictorial economy of the phrase, one queue and one pot, is why it beats the abstract word for scarcity in daily speech.
杀鸡儆猴	shā jī jǐng hóu	kill the chicken to warn the monkey: punish one to frighten the rest — Make an example of a minor offender so the watching crowd falls into line. Common in talk of office discipline and political crackdowns.
歃血为盟	shà xu è wéi méng	to smear blood on the lips as an oath — to seal a solemn covenant — The ritual procedure of the Spring and Autumn covenant assembly (盟会 ménghuì): participants touched their lips with the blood of a sacrificial animal , usually a bull or horse , while swearing the covenant terms. The act bound the parties through ritual sanction, not just political agreement.
山高水长	shān gāo shuǐ cháng	"mountains high, water long" — virtue or reputation that endures across time — From a Northern Song memorial by Fan Zhongyan (范仲淹) describing the legacy of a virtuous official: 云山苍苍，江水泱泱；先生之风，山高水长 ("the cloudy mountains stretch dark; the rivers run wide; the master's spirit , high as the mountains, long as the rivers").
山清水秀	shān qīng shuǐ xiù	Mountains Clear, Waters Lovely — Clear mountains, lovely waters. The standard Chinese phrase for a picturesque landscape: four characters that compress the whole aesthetic vocabulary of natural beauty.
山穷水尽	shān qióng shuǐ jìn	"the mountains are exhausted, the waters are at their end" — at the end of one's resources — From Lu You (陆游, Song dynasty), 游山西村: 山穷水尽疑无路，柳暗花明又一村 ("when the mountains are spent and the waters are done and there seems to be no road , there, beyond the dim willows and bright flowers, is another village").
山重水复	shān chóng shuǐ fù	mountains piled on mountains, waters winding through waters — a seemingly hopeless impasse — From a poem by Lu You 陆游. Full line: 山重水复疑无路，柳暗花明又一村, "Mountain on mountain, water on water, seemingly no path; then through dark willows and bright flowers, a village appears." The proverb for finding hope when all seems lost.
少见多怪	shǎo jiàn duō guài	"seen little, amazed by much" — making a fuss about ordinary things — 少见 (having seen few things) + 多怪 (finding much to be strange/surprising). When you haven't seen much of the world, ordinary things seem remarkable. A mild criticism of provinciality or naïvety: someone who marvels at things a more experienced person takes for granted.
舍己为人	shě jǐ wèi rén	to sacrifice oneself for others: selfless devotion to others' good — Lit: give-up-self-for-others. The highest praise for selflessness, setting one's own interests aside for the sake of other people. The 己 here is exactly the self that 舍 (to give up) renounces.
身不由己	shēn bù yóu jǐ	the body is not controlled by oneself — driven by circumstances beyond one's will — 身 shēn (body; self) + 不 bù (not) + 由 yóu (controlled by; following from) + 己 jǐ (oneself). One's own body belongs to external forces , obligation, circumstance, social pressure, or fate. The counterpart to agency: when 他 (the other, external) overrides 己 (the self).
慎终追远	shèn zhōng zhuī yuǎn	careful attention to death rites and honoring distant ancestors — From the Analects (论语): 慎终追远，民德归厚矣. "When the people are careful about death rites and diligently honor distant ancestors, the virtue of the people will return to richness." Confucius's most concise statement of why ancestor veneration matters: it cultivates moral seriousness in the living.
生生不息	shēng shēng bù xī	life generating life without ceasing — the endless renewal of existence — A Daoist cosmological phrase: life perpetually generating more life. Used in nature writing, philosophical prose, and speeches about cultural continuity.
声东击西	shēng dōng jī xī	"feign east, strike west" — strategic deception; misdirection before attacking — Lit: declare-east-strike-west. A classic military stratagem from the Sun Tzu tradition , make noise in the east to draw attention, then attack from the west. Used broadly for any deceptive misdirection tactic in business, politics, or argument. 他用声东击西的策略迷惑了对手。
十年寒窗	shí nián hán chuāng	ten years at a cold window — long, austere years of study — The image of the exam candidate reading through winters without heat. The full couplet adds the payoff: ten years at the cold window unknown, one exam success and the world knows your name (十年寒窗无人问，一举成名天下知). Modern parents invoke it during gaokao season with full sincerity.
十年树木，百年树人	shí nián shù mù , bǎi nián shù rén	"ten years to grow a tree, a hundred years to cultivate a person" — education takes far longer than any other cultivation — From the classical text 管子 Guǎnzi. Growing a tree requires ten years of patience: planting, watering, waiting. Cultivating a human being , educating, civilizing, forming character , requires ten times longer. The century-scale horizon is a deliberate rebuke to impatience.
十全十美	shí quán shí měi	perfect in every way — ten-complete and ten-beautiful, with nothing lacking — The most-used idiom of perfection in Chinese. The doubling of 十 means completeness is measured twice , in wholeness and in beauty , so that neither a technical nor an aesthetic standard is left unmet.
时不我待	shí bù wǒ dài	time will not wait for me — seize the moment; there is no time to lose — Lit: time-not-me-wait. A classical inversion , the subject (时, time) is placed before the negation, with "me" as object, producing the archaic feel of a proverb. The phrase frames time as an agent with no personal loyalty: it moves whether you are ready or not.
时过境迁	shí guò jìng qiān	time passes, circumstances change — things are different now; that was then — 时过 (time has passed) + 境迁 (the situation has shifted , 境 jìng: circumstances, context; 迁 qiān: to move, to change). Used to acknowledge that what was true, appropriate, or possible in the past no longer applies , without judgment, simply as a statement of temporal fact.
时候不早	shí hou bù zǎo	the hour is not early: it is getting late — A common set expression rather than a classical chengyu. Used to signal politely that time has slipped on, often as a cue to wrap up or take one's leave: 时候不早了，我该走了 "it is getting late, I should go."
时来运转	shí lái yùn zhuǎn	when the time comes, fortune turns — one's luck changes for the better — Lit: time-comes fortune-turns. Fortune is not fixed; it turns like a wheel. Used when someone's luck dramatically improves after a period of misfortune. The Daoist wheel of fortune encoded in a four-character phrase.
时势造英雄	shí shì zào yīng xióng	the times produce their heroes — Great figures are made by the conditions of their age, not by innate destiny alone, the circumstances create the opportunity for greatness.
实事求是	shí shì qiú shì	to seek truth from facts — address problems realistically — A classical phrase, later a famous modern slogan, meaning to approach a 问题 on the basis of facts rather than wishful thinking or ideology. 实事 (real matters) + 求是 (seek what is so). The standard expression for a pragmatic, evidence-based way of analysing and solving problems.
使坏心眼	shǐ huài xīn yǎn	to harbor a wicked scheme: to act with malicious intent — A colloquial set phrase, 使坏 (to scheme) plus 心眼 (mind, intention). Describes someone secretly plotting harm rather than acting openly. Everyday spoken register, not classical.
事不过三	shì bù guò sān	"nothing happens more than three times" — the third time is the last; do not push beyond three — A folk saying rather than a classical text quotation: three is the outer limit of tolerance or recurrence.
事出有因	shì chū yǒu yīn	everything happens for a reason — Literally "when a matter arises, there is a cause", nothing comes out of nowhere. It is the quiet conviction behind every 为什么: ask the question and there will be a 因 (cause) to find. The 因 here is the same one in 因为 ("because").
事到如今	shì dào rú jīn	now that things have come to this — Literally "matters have arrived at the present," a set opener used when a situation has reached its current, often difficult, point and one must face it: 事到如今，只能这样了 ("now that it's come to this, this is all we can do"). Built on 如今 (nowadays, the present).
事过境迁	shì guò jìng qiān	the affair has passed and the circumstances have changed — 事 (the matter) 过 (has passed) 境 (circumstances) 迁 (have shifted). Note 过 in its full verbal sense, the same morpheme that grammaticalized into the experiential particle. Used to wave off old grievances: 事过境迁，别再提了 "It's all in the past, don't bring it up again."
事与愿违	shì yǔ yuàn wéi	things go contrary to ones wishes — Traced to Ruan Ji of the Seven Sages of the Bamboo Grove: a literary formulation of the gap between intention and outcome, resigned rather than accusatory, with no blame assigned.
事在人为	shì zài rén wéi	things depend on human effort — nothing is impossible with effort — Lit: matters-located-in-human-doing. The outcome of things lies in human action, not fate or circumstance. An optimistic proverb of agency: no matter how difficult, the result is in human hands. 不要灰心，事在人为。
是非颠倒	shì fēi diān dǎo	right and wrong turned upside down — moral inversion — Lit: right-wrong-overturned-upended. Used when injustice prevails, when blame is assigned to the innocent, or when someone deliberately distorts moral reality. 他把是非颠倒，让人无法接受。Strong condemnation in formal and classical register.
是非对错	shì fēi duì cuò	right and wrong, correct and incorrect — the full moral spectrum of a situation — Lit: right-wrong-correct-incorrect. A compound of two pairs: 是非 (right/wrong as a classical pair) and 对错 (correct/incorrect as a modern pair). Used to describe careful moral judgment of a complex situation. 分清是非对错 = "to clearly distinguish right from wrong in the situation."
是非分明	shì fēi fēn míng	right and wrong clearly divided: drawing a sharp line between them — Lit: right-wrong-divide-clear. Said in praise of someone with firm moral judgment who does not blur what is right with what is wrong.
是非曲直	shì fēi qū zhí	right and wrong, crooked and straight: the true merits of a case — Lit: right-wrong-crooked-straight. The four poles by which a dispute is judged. To 分清是非曲直 is to sort out who is truly in the right, free of bias.
手不释卷	shǒu bù shì juǎn	hand never releasing the scroll — to be an insatiable reader — Lit: hand-not-release-scroll. To be so devoted to books that you never put them down. A compliment for scholars. 卷 juǎn = scroll; the classical form of the book.
手到擒来	shǒu dào qín lái	the hand arrives and the capture is made — easily accomplished — Lit: hand-arrives-capture-comes. Used to describe a task accomplished with ease and confidence , the expert reaches out and the thing is done. An expression of mastery making difficulty look effortless. 对他来说，这件事手到擒来 "For him, this was a cinch."
守候多时	shǒu hòu duō shí	to have kept watch for a long while: to wait faithfully and at length — Lit: keep-watch-much-time. Said of someone who has been standing by patiently for a long stretch, often awaiting a person or an awaited moment. 守候 pairs the vigil of 守 with the waiting of 候.
受制于人	shòu zhì yú rén	to be controlled by others; to be subject to another's power — Lit: receive-control-by-people. The standard formal expression for being under another's power, being constrained by external forces rather than acting freely. Common in diplomatic, strategic, and business contexts. The grammatical structure 受…于 is classical , modern equivalent would be 被人控制.
水到渠成	shuǐ dào qú chéng	when water flows, a channel forms — Literally "when the water arrives, the channel is made", success comes naturally once conditions ripen, with no forcing. It is the gentle counterpart to 终于's hard-won arrival: the long-awaited outcome that, in the end, simply falls into place.
水滴石穿	shuǐ dī shí chuān	water drips and the stone is bored through: small force, long time — The same image reordered, traced to the Han text Hàn Shū. Stresses the result, the worn-through stone, as proof that small efforts accumulate.
水泄不通	shuǐ xiè bù tōng	"not even water can leak through": packed solid; completely blocked or surrounded — 水 shuǐ (water) + 泄 xiè (to leak; to drain out) + 不 bù (not) + 通 (to pass through). So densely packed that not even a drop of water could trickle out.
水涨船高	shuǐ zhǎng chuán gāo	when the water rises the boat rises too: gains that lift everything along with the tide — Originally a Chán Buddhist phrase. Used when one rising element pulls others up with it, whether prices, status, or a whole industry.
水中捞月	shuǐ zhōng lāo yu è	fishing the moon out of water — chasing an illusion — Lit: water-inside-fish-moon. The moon's reflection in a pond looks real but cannot be grasped. Used for futile efforts chasing something impossible. A Buddhist parable: mistaking the reflection for reality.
顺水推船	shùn shuǐ tuī chuán	push the boat along with the current: go with the flow and make the easy move — When circumstances already lean your way, you give a light push and take the credit. Often used for a decision that costs nothing because the moment favors it.
顺天应人	shùn tiān yìng rén	"accord with Heaven, respond to humanity" — acting in alignment with both cosmic order and popular sentiment — Lit: accord-Heaven-respond-humanity. A classical formula used to describe righteous political action or revolution: the actor is both aligned with Heaven's pattern and responding to the genuine needs of the people.
说曹操，曹操到	shuō Cáo Cāo , Cáo Cāo dào	speak of Cao Cao and Cao Cao arrives — speak of the devil — The exact Chinese equivalent of "speak of the devil." Said the instant someone shows up just as you were talking about them. It is the most common everyday phrase in which Cao Cao's name survives, owed to his reputation, in the stories, for moving with sudden speed.
说曹操，曹操到。	shuō cáo cāo , cáo cāo dào .	Speak of Cao Cao and Cao Cao arrives. — The Chinese equivalent of "speak of the devil," drawn from the novel's portrait of Cao Cao as swift and unexpectedly present. Said when a person appears just as they are being talked about. One of the clearest examples of how thoroughly the Romance's characters entered everyday speech.
说到做到	shuō dào zuò dào	to do what one says — to be as good as one's word — 说 shuō (to say) + 到 dào (reach the point of) + 做 + 到. Literally "say it to the point, do it to the point." A high compliment: this is a person whose words and deeds match, who does not promise idly. The structure leans on 做到 (to achieve, to follow through).
说来话长	shuō lái huà cháng	to start telling it makes a long story: it is a long story — Lit: to-speak-of-it-the-words-grow-long. The standard preface to a complicated explanation, the Chinese equivalent of "well, it's a long story."
说一不二	shuō yī bù èr	say one, not two — a person whose word is final; absolute authority — Lit: say-one-not-two. To speak once and have it stand; not to be contradicted. Describes a figure of absolute authority within their domain. Can be used admiringly (a decisive leader) or critically (an autocrat). 他在公司里说一不二。Tā zài gōngsī lǐ shuō yī bù èr. "In the company, what he says goes."
四海为家	sì hǎi wéi jiā	to make one's home in all four seas — at home anywhere; cosmopolitan; rootless wandering — 四海 = the four seas = everywhere under heaven. 为 = to treat as; to make into. The whole globe is one's household. Used for travelers, exiles, and those rootlessly at ease anywhere. Both admiring (cosmopolitan freedom) and elegiac (loss of fixed roots) , depending on context.
四面八方	sì miàn bā fāng	"four faces, eight directions" — from every direction; from everywhere — The most common chengyu using 方. 四面 (the four faces of a square; the four sides) + 八方 (the eight directions). Used for crowds, news, demands, attacks , anything converging from every direction. 来自四面八方的客人 (guests from all over).
四面楚歌	sì miàn chǔ gē	"Chu songs on all four sides" — besieged on every side; utterly isolated — From the fall of Xiang Yu, the Hegemon-King of Chu, at the battle of Gaixia (202 BCE). Surrounded by Han forces, Xiang Yu heard his enemies singing the songs of his own homeland of Chu from every direction, and concluded that Chu had already fallen and his men had deserted to the enemy.
四通八达	sì tōng bā dá	"reaching in four and through eight" — accessible in every direction; well-connected — 四通 (passing through in the four directions) plus 八达 (extending out the eight ways). Used of roads, railways, and transport networks that radiate to everywhere, and by extension of any hub that connects in all directions. A 交通四通八达 city is one you can reach and leave by any route.
似是而非	sì shì ér fēi	seemingly right but actually wrong; plausible but false — Lit: seem-right-but-wrong. Used for arguments, claims, or reasoning that appear correct on the surface but are fundamentally flawed. The danger zone of logical errors , specious reasoning that passes for truth. Appears in classical philosophy and remains common in intellectual criticism.
送往迎来	sòng wǎng yíng lái	to send off those leaving and welcome those arriving: the endless round of social hospitality — Traces to the Lǐjì (Book of Rites). Describes the constant ceremony of receiving guests and seeing them off, often with a faint note of how exhausting such social duty becomes.
岁月不饶人	suì yu è bù ráo rén	the years spare no one — time is merciless; age catches up with everyone — 岁月 suìyuè (years and months , a classical compound for the flow of time) + 不饶 bù ráo (shows no mercy, does not spare). A cousin-phrase to 时不我待, but the mood is different: less urgent, more resigned. Where 时不我待 urges action, 岁月不饶人 acknowledges what cannot be undone.
岁月如梭	suì yu è rú suō	the years pass like a weaver's shuttle — time flies — Lit: years-months-like-shuttle. 岁月 = years and months (time). 梭 suō = the weaver's shuttle that flies back and forth at great speed. The Chinese "time flies," usually said with a mixture of nostalgia and wonder at how quickly life passes.
所谓伊人	suǒ wèi yī rén	"the one of whom I speak" — the beloved, the one I yearn for — From the Shijing (诗经) poem 蒹葭 ("Reeds"). One of the most famous lines of classical poetry, and the locus classicus for 伊 as feminine third person. The poem itself is gender-ambiguous in the classical text but read across two millennia as referring to a beloved woman across the river.
所向披靡	suǒ xiàng pī mǐ	all before one is swept aside: to carry all before one, irresistible — Lit: wherever-faced, grass-flattens. 所向 is "wherever one turns," 披靡 the way grass bends flat before the wind. Said of an army or a force so strong that everything in its path gives way.
太公钓鱼	tài gōng diào yú	Grand Duke Jiang fishes: those who are willing will take the bait — From 太公钓鱼，愿者上钩. The sage Jiang Ziya fished with a straight, baitless hook, waiting for King Wen to seek him out. Said of letting the willing come of their own accord rather than forcing them.
昙花一现	tán huā yí xiàn	to flower briefly and vanish — Literally "the night-blooming cereus shows itself once," for something splendid that lasts only a moment and is gone. The flower opens for a single short while in the night, then withers. A poignant image of fleeting brilliance, the shadow side of "a little while."
叹为观止	tàn wéi guān zhǐ	to sigh that one need look no further: the very acme of perfection — Lit: sigh-that-viewing-stops-here. From the Zuozhuan, where a guest praised a performance as so fine that watching could end there, nothing better remaining to see. High praise for the best of its kind.
堂堂正正	táng táng zhèng zhèng	dignified and upright: open, honorable, with nothing to hide — Reduplicated from 堂正. Said of someone who acts squarely and aboveboard, facing things head-on rather than scheming in the shadows. 堂堂正正做人 "to live as an upright person."
桃李满天下	táo lǐ mǎn tiān xià	peaches and plums fill the world: a teacher's students are everywhere — Lit: peach-plum-fill-the-world. 桃李 (peach and plum trees) stand for a teacher's 弟子, the disciples cultivated over a lifetime. To say someone's 桃李满天下 is high praise for a great educator.
啼笑皆非	tí xiào jiē fēi	unable to laugh or cry: caught between amusement and dismay — Lit: crying-laughing-both-wrong. Neither weeping nor laughing fits the situation, the awkward state of finding something at once absurd and exasperating. 令人啼笑皆非 "leaves one not knowing whether to laugh or cry."
天赐良缘	tiān cì liáng yuán	"heaven-bestowed good fate" — a heaven-sent destined match; a blessed union — Lit: heaven-bestow-good-fate. The most auspicious framing for a destined romantic match , one that heaven itself has arranged and blessed. Used in wedding toasts, blessings, and romantic contexts when the match seems particularly right. 他们的婚姻真是天赐良缘，两个人都幸福极了。
天地良心	tiān dì liáng xīn	heaven and earth as my witness — swearing to one's sincerity before the cosmos — A folk oath formula invoking Heaven and Earth as moral witnesses. 我天地良心地告诉你. "I'm telling you in all sincerity, with heaven and earth as my witness." Reveals how folk religion makes the cosmos itself a moral guarantor.
天翻地覆	tiān fān dì fù	"heaven overturned, earth capsized" — a total upheaval; world-reversing change — 翻 fān (to overturn; to flip) + 覆 fù (to capsize). When the cosmos itself is described as flipped, the image captures change so total that the existing order has been inverted. Used for dramatic historical upheavals or situations where everything has changed beyond recognition. 经历了这场变革，国家发生了天翻地覆的变化。
天高皇帝远	tiān gāo huáng dì yuǎn	Heaven is high and the emperor is far away — In a remote place, central authority cannot reach, so local powers do as they please. A wry comment on how rules dissolve with distance from the capital, still used for any situation where oversight is too far off to matter and people act with impunity.
天后圣母	Tiān hòu Shèng mǔ	Heavenly Empress, Holy Mother — Mazu's full honorific — The full formal title combines 天后 (Heavenly Empress, the highest imperial designation) with 圣母 Shèngmǔ (Holy Mother, a term shared with the Blessed Virgin in Catholic contexts, creating interesting resonance in Fujian where Portuguese missionaries arrived in the 16th century).
天南地北	tiān nán dì běi	heaven south, earth north — from all corners of the world; talking about everything under the sun — 南 nán = south; 北 běi = north. The phrase works in two ways. Used of people: 天南地北的朋友 (friends from all corners of the world , scattered to the four directions, heaven-south and earth-north).
天人一体	tiān rén yī tǐ	"Heaven and humanity as one body" — the complete unity of cosmic and human orders — A variant formulation of 天人合一, with 体 (body; substance) replacing 合一 (united as one). 一体 emphasizes organic embodied unity , Heaven and humanity are not merely in accord but constitute a single body, a single substance.
天时地利人和	tiān shí dì lì rén hé	"Heaven's timing, Earth's advantages, human harmony" — the three conditions for success — From Mencius (2A.1): the convergence of the right moment from Heaven, favorable terrain from Earth, and human solidarity. A practical expression of 天人合一 in strategic thought: success requires alignment with all three orders simultaneously.
天下太平	tiān xià tài píng	"all under heaven is at peace" — universal peace and order — 天下 tiānxià (all under heaven , the entire world as governed territory) + 太平 (great peace). The ideal end-state of Confucian governance and the dream of every dynasty: a world so well-ordered that there is nothing left to struggle against.
天下为公	tiān xià wéi gōng	the world belongs to all — the realm is the public good, not private property — Sun Yat-sen's personal motto, drawn from the ancient Book of Rites. Inscribed on buildings and monuments across Taiwan and mainland China. The phrase directly opposes the Confucian concept of dynasties as family property (家天下 jiā tiānxià, "the realm as one family's possession").
天下乌鸦一般黑	tiān xià wū yā yī bān hēi	all crows under heaven are equally black — it's the same everywhere; they're all the same — Usually cynical: no matter where you go, officials/bosses/people of that type are all equally corrupt/problematic/the same. 当官的天下乌鸦一般黑 (Officials everywhere are equally corrupt , all crows under heaven are black.) A statement of resigned cynicism about systemic problems.
挑拨离间	tiǎo bō lí jiàn	to stir up trouble and drive a wedge: to set people against each other — Lit: stir-up and sow-division. To deliberately create a gap between people who were close, by rumor or provocation. 离间 (jiàn) is to open a rift where there was none.
铁哥们儿	tiě gē men r	iron buddies: rock-solid male friends — 铁 (iron) intensifies 哥们儿. The metaphor is metal: a friendship that won't bend or rust. Reserved for the few friends you would drop everything for.
听而不闻	tīng ér bù wén	"listen and yet not hear" — to hear without registering; selective inattention — 听 (the act of listening) + 而 (and yet, connecting contrast) + 不闻 (not actually hearing). The distinction between 听 (the directed act) and 闻 (what the ear actually receives) sharpens into an idiom: you can perform the physical act of listening without anything penetrating.
通灵	tōng líng	spirit communication — the shamanic gift — 通 tōng (to connect, to pass through) + 灵 líng (spirit; efficacious; miraculous). The ability to communicate with spirits , the defining capacity of a shaman. In contemporary Chinese, 通灵 also appears in discussions of psychic ability, mediumship, and supernatural sensitivity more broadly.
通情达理	tōng qíng dá lǐ	"through to feeling, reaching to reason": sensible, reasonable, understanding — 通 (to be through to) + 情 qíng (human feeling; the way people are) + 达 dá (to reach) + 理 lǐ (reason; principle). A person who is 通情达理 understands both how people feel and what reason requires, and weighs the two fairly.
通俗易懂	tōng sú yì dǒng	plain and easy to understand — High praise for writing or speech: 通俗 (accessible, popular) and 易懂 (easy to understand) together describe an explanation pitched so clearly that anyone can follow it. The standard compliment for good teaching.
同病相怜	tóng bìng xiāng lián	sharing the same sickness, they pity each other: fellow sufferers sympathize — From the Wú Yuè Chūnqiū. People who endure the same hardship feel a natural bond, the way two patients in one ward understand each other without explanation.
同心协力	tóng xīn xié lì	to work together with one heart — Literally "same heart, joined strength", to pull together toward a shared goal in full unity. It is the spirit behind 一起努力 ("work hard together") raised to its ideal: not just acting jointly, but acting as one mind and one effort.
同舟共济	tóng zhōu gòng jì	in the same boat, crossing together: united by a common difficulty — Lit: same-boat-jointly-cross. People who, sharing one boat, must row together to reach the far shore. The classical image for setting aside differences in the face of a shared crisis.
投桃报李	tóu táo bào lǐ	throw a peach, return a plum: to repay a kindness in kind; mutual gift-giving — This idiom shares the verb 投 tóu (to throw; to give) that drives 投票, though it does not contain 票 itself, so it sits here as a near neighbor on the 投 thread rather than a 票 idiom. 投桃报李 comes from the Shījīng (Book of Songs): you toss me a peach, I send back a plum.
突飞猛进	tū fēi měng jìn	to advance by leaps and bounds: to progress at great speed — Lit: sudden-fly-fierce-advance. Said of rapid, dramatic progress in study, technology, or an economy. 科技突飞猛进 "technology advances by leaps and bounds."
徒有虚名	tú yǒu xū míng	nothing but an empty name — a title with no substance behind it — 徒 (merely, only) + 有 (have) + 虚名 (empty name). The sharpest everyday formulation of what 正名 opposes. A famous general who wins no battles is 徒有虚名. A top university whose graduates learn nothing is 徒有虚名.
推陈出新	tuī chén chū xīn	push out the old and bring forth the new — To discard the outdated and create something new, the constant renewal that drives a thing forward into its modern form. Used of art, technology, and institutions that modernize by building fresh work on inherited foundations rather than simply repeating the past.
脱胎换骨	tuō tāi huàn gǔ	shed the womb, swap the bones: a total transformation — From Daoist alchemy, on the adept reborn into an immortal frame. Modern usage praises a genuine, root-and-branch change for the better.
脱颖而出	tuō yǐng ér chū	the awl-tip comes through the bag — talent that emerges despite obstacles — From the Shǐjì (史记, Records of the Grand Historian). 颖 yǐng = the pointed tip of an awl. 脱 tuō = to escape; to break free. An awl placed in a bag will always eventually poke through: genuine talent, given any opportunity, will emerge and make itself visible.
外儒内法	wài Rú nèi Fǎ	Confucian on the outside, Legalist on the inside — the structural formula of Chinese imperial governance — A modern scholarly formulation summarizing two thousand years of Chinese governance. 外 wài = outer, exterior; 内 nèi = inner, interior. The outer face of government (edicts, titles, rituals, examination texts) was thoroughly Confucian.
完璧归赵	wán bì guī zhào	returning the jade intact to Zhao — restoring something to its owner undamaged — From the Shiji biography of 蔺相如 Lin Xiangru, who carried the priceless 和氏璧 jade to the powerful state of Qin, saw through Qin's bad faith, and brought the jade safely home to Zhao through sheer nerve and cunning.
完蛋	wán dàn	finished egg: to be done for, ruined, all over — Not a four-character chengyu but a fixed colloquial expression. 完 (wán, to finish) + 蛋 marks total collapse: 完蛋了 (wándàn le) means we're finished, it's all over.
完事大吉	wán shì dà jí	the matter is finished and all is well — done and dusted — 完 (to finish) + 事 shì (matter) + 大吉 dàjí (greatly auspicious, all is well). Said with relief when a task is over and one can stop worrying about it , "that's that, we're done." Sometimes carries a faint note of cutting corners, of treating the mere finishing as the whole point.
万里长征	wàn lǐ cháng zhēng	"the ten-thousand-li long march" — the 1934–1935 retreat of the Red Army; any prolonged and arduous undertaking — 万里 (ten thousand li , vast) + 长征 (long expedition, long march). The Chinese Communist Party's Long March (1934–1935) covered approximately 12,000 kilometers as the Red Army retreated from Nationalist forces. 万里 again functions as rhetorical superlative rather than measurement.
万象更新	wàn xiàng gēng xīn	all things renewed — the entire world refreshed and reborn — 万 (ten thousand; all) + 象 (phenomena; appearances) + 更新 (to renew). The 更 is gēng, first tone. A classic New Year phrase; appears on Spring Festival banners alongside 春回大地 (spring returns to the earth).
万丈高楼平地起	wàn zhàng gāo lóu píng dì qǐ	A tower ten thousand zhang tall rises from flat ground — The folk proverb that answers impatience: every immeasurable height begins on ordinary earth.
万众一心	wàn zhòng yī xīn	ten thousand hearts as one — united as one body — 万众 wànzhòng = the ten thousand people / the multitudes. 一心 = one heart / one mind. Used for collective unity in times of national effort or crisis. A rallying phrase in political rhetoric and public discourse , the aspiration that multiplicity collapses back into the cosmological One.
万紫千红	wàn zǐ qiān hóng	ten thousand purples, a thousand reds — a riot of color; abundant beauty — From the Song dynasty poet Zhu Xi 朱熹: 等闲识得东风面，万紫千红总是春 , "It is easy to recognize the face of the east wind: ten thousand purples and a thousand reds , it is all spring." The phrase moved from the poem into common use as the standard expression for spring's visual abundance.
亡羊补牢，为时未晚	wáng yáng bǔ láo , wéi shí wèi wǎn	mend the pen after the sheep is lost — it is not yet too late — The extended form of the idiom, almost always cited together. The second half (为时未晚, not yet too late) is the direct answer to the fatalist in the original story. The full eight-character phrase is common enough to function as a single fixed saying.
亡羊得牛	wáng yáng dé niú	lose a sheep, gain an ox — A related phrase (not a classical chengyu in the same sense) used to describe a small loss that leads to an unexpected larger gain: the silver lining formulation. Where 亡羊补牢 is about repair, 亡羊得牛 is about serendipitous compensation. The shared 亡羊 makes the pair memorable.
往来不绝	wǎng lái bù ju é	a constant stream of coming and going — uninterrupted traffic — 往 wǎng (to go toward) + 来 (to come) + 不绝 (without cessation). Describes a place or relationship with continuous back-and-forth movement: traffic, commerce, correspondence, social connection. 街上人来人往，好不热闹 "People coming and going on the street , what a lively scene!"
往事如烟	wǎng shì rú yān	the past is like drifting smoke — Literally "bygone matters are like smoke", the past fading and dispersing beyond grasp. Here 往 takes its time sense, 往事 ("past events"), and the phrase carries a gentle melancholy about how what is gone slips away. The mirror of 往 reaching backward rather than ahead.
忘我工作	wàng wǒ gōng zuò	to work with selfless devotion; absorbed work
韦编三绝	wéi biān sān ju é	the leather cord worn through three times — the archetype of devoted scholarship — 韦 wéi = leather; 编 biān = cord, binding; 三绝 sān jué = worn through three times. Classical books were written on bamboo strips bound together with cords of leather or silk.
委曲求全	wěi qū qiú quán	to compromise to keep the peace: to bend and yield so the whole holds together — Lit: bend-crooked-seek-wholeness. To swallow grievances and make concessions in order to preserve a relationship or an arrangement intact. Here 全 is the intact whole one bends in order to save.
为非作歹	wéi fēi zuò dǎi	to do evil; to commit crimes and wrongdoing — Lit: do-wrong-make-evil. 非 (wrong) and 歹 dǎi (bad, evil) pair up to name flagrant wrongdoing. Said of those who run riot doing harm: 为非作歹的歹徒 "evildoers who run amok."
为虎作伥	wèi hǔ zuò chāng	to be a henchman for the tiger; to help the powerful oppress others — Lit: for-tiger-act-as-ghost-of-the-slain. In Chinese folklore, the ghost of someone eaten by a tiger becomes a 伥 , a spirit that lures future victims to the tiger. To 为虎作伥 is to be co-opted into serving the very power that devoured you.
为人师表	wéi rén shī biǎo	to be a teacher and model for others: to set an example worthy of imitation — 为人 wéi rén = to act as, to be for others. 师 shī = teacher. 表 biǎo = model; the standard others measure themselves against. To serve as a moral and intellectual example, especially said of teachers who must embody what they teach.
未雨绸缪	wèi yǔ chóu móu	prepare before the rain comes — From the Book of Songs (诗经, Shījīng): the bird weaves its nest before the rains arrive. The counterpart to 亡羊补牢 , prevention before the fact. Together the two idioms cover the complete cycle of crisis management: prepare if you can, and repair decisively if prevention fails.
温故知新	wēn gù zhī xīn	"review the old to understand the new" — the scholar's relationship to tradition — 温 (wēn, to warm; to review , warm water used to reheat food, hence to re-examine) + 故 (gù, old; former) + 知 (zhī, to know) + 新 (new). From the Analects of Confucius, Chapter 2.11: 子曰，温故而知新，可以为师矣。
文不对题	wén bù duì tí	the writing does not match the topic: off the point, irrelevant — Lit: writing-not-match-topic. Said of an essay or answer that wanders away from what was asked, or of a reply that misses the actual question. The classic teacher's note on a stray composition.
文房四宝	wén fáng sì bǎo	the four treasures of the study: brush, ink, paper, inkstone — 文房 is the scholar's study, the writing 房; 四宝 are its four treasures: 笔 (brush), 墨 (ink), 纸 (paper), 砚 (inkstone). The phrase names the classical toolkit of Chinese calligraphy and painting.
文质彬彬	wén zhì bīn bīn	refined in manner and solid in substance — the cultivated gentleman — From the Analects: 文质彬彬，然后君子. "When refinement (文) and substance (质) are balanced, then you have the gentleman." The ideal of 文 is not mere surface polish but the harmonious integration of outward culture with inner integrity. 彬彬 = harmonious, balanced.
闻鸡起舞	wén jī qǐ wǔ	hear the cock and rise to practice swordplay: diligent self-discipline — From the Jin Shu account of Zǔ Tì (祖逖), who woke at the rooster's first crow to drill with his sword. A praise word for someone who studies or trains relentlessly.
稳如泰山	wěn rú Tài Shān	as stable as Mount Tai — utterly immovable, rock solid — Mount Tai (泰山, Shandong), the most sacred of the Five Peaks and site of imperial Heaven-sacrifice. Used to praise a person's composure, a government's stability, or a company's financial health.
问鼎中原	wèn dǐng Zhōng yuán	to aspire to rule China — lit. "to inquire about the cauldrons of the Central Plains" — From a Spring and Autumn anecdote: the king of Chu asked Zhou envoys about the weight of the Nine Cauldrons (九鼎), signaling his ambition to displace the Zhou. 问鼎 became permanently associated with political ambition at the highest level.
问题的关键	wèn tí de guān jiàn	the crux of the problem; the key issue — A very common phrase pairing 问题 with 关键 (key, crux) to point at the heart of a matter: 问题的关键在于… ("the crux of the problem lies in…"). Used constantly in analysis and discussion to single out what really matters, the decisive point on which everything turns.
五光十色	wǔ guāng shí sè	"five lights, ten colors" — multicolored and dazzling; richly varied — 五光 (five kinds of light) and 十色 (ten colors) together evoke a brilliant, shifting display. Used both literally, for lights and decorations that glitter in every hue, and figuratively for a world or a scene that is varied, glamorous, and a little dazzling.
五湖四海	wǔ hú sì hǎi	the five lakes and four seas: from every corner of the land — A sweep of the whole country. Used warmly for people gathered from everywhere: 我们来自五湖四海。
五体投地	wǔ tǐ tóu dì	"five limbs to the ground" — to prostrate fully; to admire with complete humility — 五体 (the five parts of the body: the two knees, the two elbows or hands, and the head) plus 投地 (cast to the ground). It names the Buddhist full prostration in which all five touch the floor, the deepest bodily gesture of reverence.
五颜六色	wǔ yán liù sè	five shades, six colors — a riot of colors; multicolored — Lit: five-shades-six-colors. The standard idiom for anything vibrantly multicolored. 五 and 六 are figurative numbers (like English "many"); the compound just means extremely colorful. 市场上五颜六色的花朵 "the market's riot of colorful flowers."
物换星移	wù huàn xīng yí	things change, stars shift — time passes and the world turns over — From Wang Bo's Tang-dynasty Pavilion of Prince Teng poem: 物换星移几度秋, "things change, stars shift, how many autumns now?" The stars' slow precession as the clock of dynasties. Used for nostalgia at scale: returning to a transformed hometown, rereading an old diary.
物是人非	wù shì rén fēi	things remain but people have changed — the bittersweet feeling of returning to a place where everything looks the same but everyone is gone — Lit: things-are-people-not. A key phrase in classical Chinese poetry for the emotional experience of historical rupture: looking at unchanged landscapes after dynasties have fallen. Deeply appropriate to the Yuan-Ming transition, when the same cities stood but an entire ruling order had vanished.
物以类聚	wù yǐ lèi jù	things gather by kind: like attracts like, birds of a feather flock together — Lit: things-by-kind-gather. From the idea that creatures of a sort come together; often paired with 人以群分 "people divide into groups," and used, sometimes pointedly, of who keeps company with whom.
雾里看花	wù lǐ kàn huā	"looking at flowers through mist" — seeing something obscurely, without clarity — 雾 (mist, fog) + 里 (inside, within) + 看花 (looking at flowers). The object of perception is real, but the medium prevents clear sight. Used for situations where the truth is obscured , murky political signals, ambiguous intentions, confusing circumstances.
夕阳西下	xī yáng xī xià	"the evening sun descends in the west" — dusk; things in decline — 夕阳 (setting sun) + 西 (west) + 下 (to descend). A classic image from Tang dynasty poetry: the sun dropping below the western horizon. Used literally for beautiful sunsets and metaphorically for the decline of an era, a career, or a life.
西方极乐	xī fāng jí lè	the Western Pure Land — and by extension, any paradise or blissful realm — The formal name 西方极乐世界 Xīfāng Jílè Shìjiè is abbreviated to 西方极乐 in common usage. In pure religious context: Amitābha's Buddha-field. In broader cultural use: any ideal realm of beauty, abundance, and peace.
息息相关	xī xī xiāng guān	"every breath mutually connected" — closely and inextricably interconnected — Lit: breath-breath-mutually-connected. 息 = breath. The image: two systems breathing in such synchrony that they cannot be separated. Used for things that are organically and necessarily connected. 这两个问题息息相关，解决一个就能推动另一个。"These two issues are inextricably linked , solving one will advance the other."
嬉皮笑脸	xī pí xiào liǎn	grinning cheekily: to behave with a flippant, unserious grin — A face stuck in a smirk, used to describe someone who refuses to take a moment seriously. Often a parent's rebuke to a child who jokes through a scolding.
习以为常	xí yǐ wéi cháng	to grow so used to a thing that it seems ordinary — Lit: practice-take-to-be-normal. What was once strange or hard becomes routine through repetition, until one no longer notices it. Often said of bad habits or hardships one has stopped questioning.
徙木立信	xǐ mù lì xìn	move the post, establish credibility — demonstrate that stated commitments will be honored — Shang Yang's wooden post demonstration before his Qin reforms: post a reward, honor it fully, establish that the state means what it says.
喜欢就好	xǐ huan jiù hǎo	as long as you like it, that's enough — A warm everyday phrase, not a classical chengyu, used when giving a gift or making a choice for someone: if you like it, that's all that matters. It captures a generous, easygoing attitude and is one of the most common natural uses of 喜欢 in spoken Chinese.
喜闻乐见	xǐ wén lè jiàn	glad to hear and pleased to see — popular and well-liked — Describes something the public genuinely enjoys and welcomes, literally "glad to hear it and happy to see it." 喜 (glad) and 乐 lè (happy) pair with 闻 (hear) and 见 (see). Often used of art, entertainment, or activities that are widely and warmly received.
先来后到	xiān lái hòu dào	first come, first served — Literally "the early-comers and the later-arrivers", the order of precedence set by who came first. It rests on the same 先 ("first") and 后 ("after") that frame 然后, here applied to fairness in turn-taking and priority. The natural order of a queue.
先入为主	xiān rù wéi zhǔ	what enters first becomes master: first impressions dominate later judgment — Lit: first-enter-become-master. The idea that lodges in the mind earliest takes the seat of authority and colors everything after it, the classical name for the grip of a first impression or a prior bias.
先小人后君子	xiān xiǎo rén hòu jūn zǐ	petty men first, gentlemen after — settle terms up front — The contract-signing proverb: state the money, the split, and the deadlines with un-gentlemanly explicitness now, so that everyone can afford to be a gentleman later. Spoken at the start of partnerships between friends precisely because friendship is what it protects.
显而易见	xiǎn ér yì jiàn	obvious; plain to see: needing no explanation — Lit: evident-and-easy-to-see. The 而 joins "evident" to "easily seen," so plain that it is grasped at a glance. Often opens a sentence stating something taken as self-evident.
现身说法	xiàn shēn shuō fǎ	to appear in person and teach by example: to instruct from one's own experience — Lit: show-the-body-and-expound-the-teaching. Originally Buddhist, of a deity appearing in bodily form to preach. Now it means to teach others by drawing on what one has personally lived through.
相辅相成	xiāng fǔ xiāng chéng	to complement each other: two things that reinforce and complete one another — Lit: mutually-assist mutually-complete. Said of two factors that work together so that each makes the other stronger, like theory and practice or form and content.
相亲相爱	xiāng qīn xiāng ài	to be close and loving — mutual affection between people — Lit: mutually-close mutually-love. The standard phrase for loving harmony in a family or couple. Often used in toasts and wedding speeches.
相濡以沫，不如相忘于江湖	xiāng rú yǐ mò , bù rú xiāng wàng yú jiāng hú	Better to forget each other in the rivers and lakes than to sustain each other with spit — From 大宗师. The first half, 相濡以沫, has become an independent chengyu meaning "mutual support and care in difficult circumstances" (the fish sustaining each other in a drying puddle). Used positively for devoted couples or friends who help each other through hardship.
想方设法	xiǎng fāng shè fǎ	to think of squares and devise methods: to try every possible means — Lit: think-method-set-up-method. To rack one's brains for a way through, doing whatever it takes. 方 and 法 here both carry the "method" sense, doubled for emphasis.
想入非非	xiǎng rù fēi fēi	to let the mind wander into improper territory; lost in fantasy — 想 xiǎng (to think; to imagine) + 入 rù (to enter; to go into) + 非非 fēifēi (beyond right and wrong; in Buddhist usage, the realm beyond ordinary thought).
小菜一碟	xiǎo cài yī dié	a small dish on a plate: a piece of cake, an easy matter — Lit: small-dish-one-saucer. A 小菜 is a minor side dish, trivial next to the main course, so 小菜一碟 means a task so easy it is hardly worth mentioning. 这对我来说是小菜一碟 "this is a piece of cake for me."
小孩没娘，说来话长	xiǎo hái méi niáng , shuō lái huà cháng	the child has no mother: it's a long story — A 歇后语 (xiēhòuyǔ) two-part saying. The literal image, a motherless child, sets up the punchline 说来话长, "it's a long story." Spoken as a preface when the backstory is tangled and the speaker hardly knows where to begin.
小孩子家	xiǎo hái zi jiā	just a kid, only a child — Colloquial set phrase. The trailing 家 marks a category of person; said dismissively or fondly of someone too young to know better. "你一个小孩子家懂什么。"
小人得志	xiǎo rén dé zhì	the petty man gets his moment — power in unworthy hands — 得志 is achieving one's ambitions; attached to 小人 it names the special insufferability of the schemer promoted. The stock description of the new supervisor swaggering through a department, and of every court favorite in the historical novels.
小题大做	xiǎo tí dà zuò	to make a big production of a small topic — to overreact — 小 xiǎo (small) + 题 tí (topic, subject) + 大 dà (big) + 做 (to make, to treat). Originally from the examination tradition, where a trivial topic was expanded into a grand essay; now it means blowing a minor matter out of proportion.
小心翼翼	xiǎo xīn yì yì	with extreme care and caution; treading very lightly — Lit: small-heart wing-wing. The 翼翼 reduplication comes from the Shijing (Classic of Poetry) where it described birds folding their wings carefully.
笑里藏刀	xiào lǐ cáng dāo	a dagger hidden in a smile: friendly on the surface, treacherous beneath — Lit: laugh-inside hide-knife. Traced to the Tang minister Lǐ Yìfǔ, whose mild smile masked a ruthless heart. Names a person whose warmth is a weapon, the dark twin of 微笑.
挟天子以令诸侯	xié tiān zǐ yǐ lìng zhū hóu	hold the emperor to command the lords — rule through a puppet figurehead — Cao Cao's signature strategy: control a powerless legitimate ruler and issue your own orders in their name. Now a standard idiom for anyone who wields real power from behind a respectable figurehead, in politics or business. It names the gap between formal authority and actual control.
心不在焉	xīn bù zài yān	absent-minded; not mentally present — Lit: heart-not-present-there. 焉 yān is a classical demonstrative "there." The heart/mind (心 xīn , the seat of consciousness in Chinese tradition) is absent from the place where the body sits. Used to describe distraction, daydreaming, or emotional distance during a conversation or task. 他上课时总是心不在焉。
心急火燎	xīn jí huǒ liǎo	heart-urgent, fire-scorching — desperately impatient and anxious — Lit: heart-urgent-fire-scorch. The sensation of being so anxious that you feel fire scorching your insides. Used for people in unbearable suspense, waiting for important news.
心急如焚	xīn jí rú fén	heart anxious as if burning: consumed with worry — 焚 (fén) is to set ablaze. The heart so 急 it feels on fire, used for someone waiting in agony for urgent news. Literary and intense.
心想事成	xīn xiǎng shì chéng	may what the heart wishes come true — 心 xīn (heart) + 想 xiǎng (think; wish) + 事 shì (matter; affair) + 成 chéng (to succeed; to be accomplished). A blessing structure: heart-wishes turn into accomplished realities. Among the most common felicitations in Chinese , standard at New Year (新年快乐，心想事成！), birthdays, and before examinations.
心心相印	xīn xīn xiāng yìn	heart imprinting on heart — deep mutual understanding; soul connection — 印 yìn = seal; to stamp. Like two wax seals pressed together making a perfect impression , silent, complete correspondence between two people's inner worlds. 他们心心相印，不需要多说话就能理解彼此。
心有灵犀	xīn yǒu líng xī	hearts share the rhinoceros horn — telepathic understanding; being on the same wavelength — From a Tang poem by Li Shangyin (李商隐): 身无彩凤双飞翼，心有灵犀一点通. The rhinoceros horn was believed in classical natural philosophy to have a channel running through its center , an image of two hearts with a shared passage between them, allowing understanding to flow without words.
心猿意马	xīn yuán yì mǎ	mind like a monkey, will like a horse — a restless, unsettled mind — A Buddhist-Daoist image, central to the novel's allegory, of the mind as an unruly monkey and the will as a bolting horse, leaping and galloping where it pleases. The Monkey King is literally the 心猿, the mind-monkey, who must be tamed.
心知肚明	xīn zhī dù míng	the heart knows and the belly is clear — everyone understands, nobody says it — Knowledge distributed through the torso: heart knowing, belly bright. The phrase for open secrets: the office 心知肚明 about why the manager left; both negotiators 心知肚明 about the real bottom line. Understanding at its most silent.
欣欣向荣	xīn xīn xiàng róng	flourishing and thriving: full of vigor and growth — Lit: joyful-joyful, facing-flourishing. 欣欣 is the look of glad, vigorous growth; 向荣 is turning toward flourishing. Said of plants in full bloom, and by extension of a thriving enterprise or society.
信手拈来	xìn shǒu niān lái	pick up casually with a free hand — to write or create with effortless skill — Lit: trust-hand-pick up-come. Used of writers, calligraphers, and scholars who produce refined work without apparent effort. The idiom implies deep mastery: the hand moves freely because the mind no longer needs to deliberate. Often said of someone who writes beautifully on demand.
星罗棋布	xīng luó qí bù	arrayed like stars, spread like chess pieces — dotted everywhere — 星罗 (netted like stars) + 棋布 (laid out like go stones). The standard descriptor for anything scattered densely across a landscape: lakes on a delta, islands in an archipelago, convenience stores in Shanghai. The two images, night sky and go board, are both grids the classical eye knew intimately.
兴高采烈	xìng gāo cǎi liè	spirits high, expression ablaze — in jubilant high spirits — Originally literary criticism: Liu Xie's Wenxin Diaolong used 兴高而采烈 to praise the poet Ji Kang's lofty inspiration and brilliant style. The phrase migrated from describing writing to describing faces, and now marks any scene of visible collective joy: children on the last day of school arrive 兴高采烈.
兴亡教训	xīng wáng jiào xùn	the lessons of rise and fall — the standard analytical frame for the Taiping in modern Chinese historiography — 兴 (rise, flourish) + 亡 (fall, perish) + 教训 (lesson, warning). Not a fixed classical chengyu but a recurring analytical phrase in modern Chinese historical writing, particularly in discussions of the Taiping.
凶多吉少	xiōng duō jí shǎo	much ill fortune, little good — the outlook is grim — 多 and 少 as a probability report: on the scale of outcomes, the bad end is heavy. The stock phrase of thriller narration and worried families when someone has gone missing; expect it in every wuxia novel's darkest chapter.
兄弟姐妹	xiōng dì jiě mèi	brothers and sisters; siblings — The full set of siblings named in birth-order pairs: 兄 (elder brother), 弟 (younger brother), 姐 (elder sister), 妹 (younger sister). A staple four-character phrase, and one of the few common ones to carry 姐 directly.
休戚相关	xiū qī xiāng guān	sharing joys and sorrows: bound together by a common fate — Lit: joy-sorrow-mutually-connected. Two parties so closely tied that whatever befalls one befalls the other, the deep 关系 in which fortunes rise and fall as one.
袖手旁观	xiù shǒu páng guān	to watch from the sidelines with folded arms: to look on without lifting a finger — Lit: sleeve-hands-aside-watch. With hands tucked in the sleeves, the onlooker stands by and does nothing while others struggle. A reproach for passivity and refusal to help.
喧宾夺主	xuān bīn duó zhǔ	the noisy guest usurps the host: a secondary thing overshadows the main one — Lit: clamoring-guest-seizes-host. Said when something minor draws all the attention away from what should be central, like a sideshow that swallows the main event or a flashy detail that buries the point.
削尖脑袋往里钻	xu ē jiān nǎo dai wǎng lǐ zuān	to sharpen one's head and bore in: to push to the front of the queue — A colloquial saying, not a classical 成语, paired here with 加塞儿 (jiāsāir, to cut the line). Mentioned to contrast the orderly 排队 ideal with the elbows-out reality of a crowded queue.
学不可以已	xu é bù kě yǐ yǐ	Learning cannot stop — the process of cultivation has no natural endpoint — Opening line of the Xunzi and of the 劝学 chapter, the programmatic statement of Xunzi's entire educational philosophy. 已 yǐ: to stop, to cease. The philosophical force follows from the 性恶论: if goodness is not innate but must be cultivated, then the cultivation is never complete by its own logic.
学而优则仕	xu é ér yōu zé shì	excel in learning, then enter officialdom — From the Analects (子张 chapter): the formulation attributed to Confucius's disciple Zixia establishing the connection between scholarly achievement and public service. 优 (yōu) means to excel, to have surplus capacity; 仕 (shì) means to serve in office.
学以致用	xu é yǐ zhì yòng	learn in order to apply — practical application of knowledge — Lit: learn-by-means-of-reach-use. The Confucian insistence that learning is not ornamental but practical: knowledge must be applied in action to have value. The opposite of empty book-learning (书呆子 shū dāizi, "bookworm" in the pejorative sense).
雪上加霜	xu ě shàng jiā shuāng	frost on top of snow — to make a bad situation even worse — 雪 xuě (snow) + 上 shàng (on top of) + 加 jiā (to add) + 霜 shuāng (frost). Frost piled on snow already covering the ground: one hardship stacked on another. The everyday equivalent of "to rub salt in the wound" or "when it rains, it pours." 他刚失业，妻子又生病了，真是雪上加霜。
雪中送炭	xu ě zhōng sòng tàn	to send charcoal in the snow: timely help when it is needed most — The image is bringing fuel to someone freezing in a blizzard. Praises aid that arrives at the moment of real need, the warm counterpart of 锦上添花 (adding flowers to brocade), which only embellishes what is already fine. Still in everyday use.
循序渐进	xún xù jiàn jìn	to advance step by step in order — Literally "following the sequence, advancing gradually", to progress in proper order without rushing. It is the principle behind any 先…然后…最后 plan: each step in its turn, the next only after the last. A watchword in learning and training.
训练有素	xùn liàn yǒu sù	trained to a standard: well-drilled, disciplined — 素 here means a long-cultivated grounding. Praises a team, unit, or staff whose competence is visibly the product of systematic 训练 rather than luck.
严于律己	yán yú lǜ jǐ	strict in disciplining oneself: hold yourself to a high standard — Lit: strict-toward-disciplining-self. Often paired with 宽以待人, "lenient toward others," to describe the admired character who is hard on the self and gentle with everyone else.
眼花缭乱	yǎn huā liáo luàn	eyes blurring, vision tangling — dazzled, overwhelmed by abundance — Lit: eye-flower-tangle-disorder. Used for the sensory overload of a busy market, a fireworks display, a dazzling performance: too much beauty or activity to take in at once. Almost always positive in tone (the overload of richness, not chaos).
扬长避短	yáng cháng bì duǎn	play up strengths, avoid weaknesses: lean into what you do well — Common in advice and strategy contexts; the practical companion to 取长补短.
咬文嚼字	yǎo wén jiáo zì	to chew and gnaw on words — nitpicking at wording; pedantic attention to phrasing — 咬 = to bite; 嚼 = to chew. Vividly imagines someone masticating language rather than simply reading it. Can be used approvingly (careful, attentive reading) or critically (pedantry, missing the point). Context determines the register.
药到病除	yào dào bìng chú	the medicine arrives, the illness is gone: an instant, effective cure — A praise phrase for a skilled doctor or a decisive remedy. Extended to any solution that fixes a problem cleanly and at once.
业精于勤	yè jīng yú qín	mastery of one's work comes from diligence: skill is born of effort — Lit: work, refined-through, diligence. From Han Yu's essay on study: 业精于勤，荒于嬉, "mastery comes from diligence and is ruined by play." A maxim that one's craft or learning is perfected only through steady, hard application.
一笔勾销	yī bǐ gōu xiāo	to cancel with one stroke of the pen: to write off entirely — Lit: one-stroke-cross-out. With a single line drawn through the ledger, a debt or a past grievance is wiped clean. Used of forgiving a debt or letting an old matter be settled and forgotten.
一唱百和	yì chàng bǎi hè	one sings, a hundred answer: a single voice draws a crowd of agreement — One person 唱 starts the note and a hundred others 和 (hè) chime in. Used for an idea or call that instantly wins broad, eager support.
一唱一和	yì chàng yì hè	one sings, the other echoes: to chime in and back each other up — Originally one voice leads and another harmonizes (和 hè, to respond in tune). Now usually mildly negative: two people egging each other on or colluding to push the same line.
一触即发	yī chù jí fā	touch and it ignites — a situation on the verge of explosion — Lit: one-touch-immediately-fire. A crisis situation where the slightest action will trigger an explosion: political tensions, a fragile negotiation, volatile emotions. Originally describing hair-trigger mechanisms, now widely applied to any situation of extreme tension.
一蹴而就	yí cù ér jiù	to succeed in one step — Literally "one stamp of the foot and it is done," to accomplish something in a single easy stroke. Most often used in the negative, to warn that hard things cannot be done all at once: 学语言不能一蹴而就 ("you can't master a language in one go"). The single-action 一 again, the cousin of 一下子.
一点一滴	yì diǎn yì dī	a dot and a drop: bit by bit, little by little — Lit: one-dot-one-drop. The small increments by which patient effort accumulates. 一点一滴地积累 "to build up bit by bit."
一动不动	yí dòng bú dòng	not moving a muscle: perfectly still, motionless — Lit: one-move-not-move. The everyday phrase for total stillness, a person frozen in place, an animal holding its breath. The doubled 动 frames the stillness as the complete absence of motion.
