一而再，再而三	yī ér zài , zài ér sān	once, then again, then a third time — time after time; repeatedly — The fullest expression of Chinese repetition idiomatics. 一而再 (once to twice), 再而三 (twice to three times): the escalating structure conveys frustration or persistence. Almost always used with a negative coloring: someone has done something they shouldn't have, repeatedly. 他一而再，再而三地失约，让人很失望。
一分钱一分货	yī fēn qián yī fēn huò	you get what you pay for — one fen of money, one fen of goods — The Chinese equivalent of "you get what you pay for." 分 fēn is both a fraction of a yuan and a measure of fairness. A practical consumer proverb that endorses quality over cheapness.
一概而论	yī gài ér lùn	to lump together and judge as one: to treat distinct cases as if identical — Literally "to discuss everything by one measure." Almost always negated: 不能一概而论 bùnéng yī gài ér lùn "you cannot lump them all together," a standard caution against treating different cases with a single blanket judgement.
一个个	yí ge gè	one by one; one after another — The triple form stresses that things happen or are dealt with individually, in sequence. 学生一个个走进教室 "the students filed into the classroom one by one."
一见如故	yī jiàn rú gù	to feel like old friends at first meeting: instant rapport — 一 (yī, one) + 见 (jiàn, to meet) + 如 (rú, as if, like) + 故 (an old friend, drawing on the 故人 sense). To meet someone for the first time and feel as though you have known them for years.
一见钟情	yí jiàn zhōng qíng	to fall in love at first sight — Lit: one-look-concentrate-feeling. 钟 zhōng here means to gather or focus one's feeling on someone. The standard phrase for instant romantic attraction on a first meeting.
一件不漏	yī jiàn bù lòu	not letting a single item slip: accounting for every piece, leaving nothing out — 漏 lòu = to leak; to omit. A colloquial set phrase built on 件, used when an inventory, a checklist, or a list of tasks is completed in full with no item missed. 他把行李一件不漏地清点了一遍。(He counted every piece of luggage without missing one.)
一件件	yī jiàn jiàn	one item at a time: piece by piece, one matter after another — A reduplicated measure-word phrase rather than a classical chengyu, but extremely common in speech. Doubling 件 turns it into an adverbial sense of taking things one at a time. 我们一件件地处理这些问题。(Let us handle these problems one by one.)
一举两得	yī jǔ liǎng dé	one move, two gains — kill two birds with one stone — Lit: one-raise/move-two-gains. 举 here is a verbal measure word (one lift/movement). A perfect analogue of "kill two birds with one stone." 这样做一举两得 "Doing it this way achieves two goals at once." Among the most common four-character idioms in business and daily speech.
一马当先	yī mǎ dāng xiān	one horse takes the lead: to be out front, to take the initiative — The lead horse of the charge. Praises someone who steps forward first and sets the pace for others.
一脉相承	yī mài xiāng chéng	descended from one bloodline: an unbroken line of inheritance — Lit: one-vein-mutually-inherit. Said of ideas, schools, or traditions that pass down through a single connected lineage with nothing broken in between. The image is of a single connecting thread, the same 系 logic of strands held together across generations.
一毛不拔	yī máo bù bá	Not Give Even a Hair — Refuse to give even a single hair from your body. Mencius's phrase for the Yangist philosophy of radical self-preservation, and the word for a miser that it became.
一模一样	yì mú yí yàng	cast from one mould: exactly alike, identical — Lit: one-mould-one-shape. Said of two things so alike they might have come from a single cast: 这对双胞胎长得一模一样 "the twins look exactly alike."
一票否决	yí piào fǒu ju é	a single vote vetoes it: one negative vote overrides the rest; absolute veto power — 一票 yí piào = a single vote. 否决 fǒujué = to vote down; to veto. A decision rule in which one opposing vote is enough to block a proposal outright, the power a permanent member holds on the UN Security Council.
一石二鸟	yī shí èr niǎo	one stone, two birds — accomplish two goals with a single action — The Chinese and English idioms are structurally identical. The image of a single stone bringing down two birds appears to have developed independently in both traditions, though the Chinese form likely circulated in literary Chinese before modern contact. In use: 这个方案真是一石二鸟。
一时半会儿	yì shí bàn huì r	in a short time; for a little while — A colloquial set phrase, "one moment, half a spell," meaning a brief span of time, most often in the negative: 这事一时半会儿说不清 ("this can't be explained in a moment"). It builds directly on the 会儿 of 一会儿, stretching it into a fixed expression for "any time soon."
一心一意	yī xīn yī yì	single-minded; wholeheartedly devoted — one heart, one mind — Lit: one-heart-one-intention. Used for unwavering focus or devotion , to a person, a task, or a cause. 他一心一意地爱着她。(He loves her with his whole heart and mind, without wavering.) The antonym is 三心二意 sān xīn èr yì , "three hearts, two intentions" , indecisive and halfhearted.
一眼万年	yī yǎn wàn nián	a single glance, ten thousand years — a look that lasts forever — Lit: one-glance-ten-thousand-years. Modern romantic register, especially in song lyrics and pop fiction: the glance at first meeting that settles a lifetime. Builds on 眼 as the measure word for a single act of looking.
一字不漏	yī zì bù lòu	not missing a single character — word-for-word; complete and verbatim — 漏 lòu = to leak; to omit; to miss. 他把合同一字不漏地读了一遍。(He read the contract verbatim, not missing a single character.) Used to emphasize thoroughness, total quotation, or painstaking completeness. Related: 一字不差 yī zì bù chā (not one character wrong, absolutely accurate).
一字一句	yī zì yī jù	word by word, line by line: spoken or read with careful deliberation — Lit: one-character-one-sentence. Describes speaking, reading, or copying out so slowly and clearly that each character and each phrase lands on its own. 一字一句地说 "to say it word by word."
以和为贵	yǐ hé wéi guì	to hold harmony as precious — harmony as the highest value — From the Analects I.12 (有子's saying): 礼之用，和为贵，先王之道，斯为美。 — "In the application of ritual, harmony is what is precious; this is the beauty of the ways of the former kings." Often cited as the Confucian reason why formal rules (礼) exist: they are instruments toward harmony, not ends in themselves.
以卵击石	yǐ luǎn jī shí	to throw an egg at a rock: to attempt the hopelessly outmatched — Here the egg is 卵 (luǎn), the classical word for egg, but the image is the same fragile shell shattering on stone. Describes a doomed fight against overwhelming force.
以人为本	yǐ rén wéi běn	to put people first; human-centered governance — Lit: use-person-as-root. A classical Confucian governance principle revived as modern policy language. 本 běn (root; foundation) is the underground portion of a tree, the part everything else rests on. The person, not the state or ideology, is the root of all legitimate policy.
以少胜多	yǐ shǎo shèng duō	"win against many with few" — overcome a larger force; quality over quantity — 以 (by means of) + 少 (few) + 胜 (overcome, win) + 多 (many). The archetype is military: a smaller but better-prepared force defeating a larger one. The Battle of Red Cliffs (208 CE), in which Sun Quan and Liu Bei's combined forces defeated Cao Cao's much larger army, is the classic example.
以身作则	yǐ shēn zuò zé	to set a standard through personal conduct — lead by example — 以 (by means of) + 身 (one whole person; conduct) + 作则 (set a rule/standard). 身 is the entire person understood as embodied conduct , the leader is not separate from the example set. Among the most-cited leadership principles in Chinese management and parenting discourse. 作为领导者，必须以身作则。
以史为鉴	yǐ shǐ wéi jiàn	use history as a mirror — learning from the past to illuminate the present — A classical Chinese phrase with new resonance in the post-Cultural Revolution context. 鉴 (mirror, warning reflection) + 史 (history).
以史为镜	yǐ shǐ wéi jìng	use history as a mirror — learn from the past to govern the present — Taizong's most famous political aphorism: "Using copper as a mirror, one can straighten one's appearance; using history as a mirror, one can understand the rise and fall of dynasties; using people as a mirror, one can perceive one's own gains and losses." The "people as mirror" referred to his minis…
以天为则	yǐ tiān wéi zé	"taking Heaven as the standard" — modeling conduct on the cosmic pattern — 则 zé (standard; rule; model; then). The classical injunction to take Heaven's order as the measure for human conduct. Appears in Mohist and Confucian texts as a basis for ethical argument: if a practice conforms to Heaven's pattern, it is right; if it violates that pattern, it is wrong.
以文会友	yǐ wén huì yǒu	"to make friends through writing" — cultural exchange as the basis of friendship — 以 (by means of) + 文 (writing, culture) + 会 (to meet, to gather) + 友 (friend). From the Analects (Book XII): Zēngzǐ says the exemplary person uses 文 (cultural cultivation) to gather friends. In classical usage, 文 here means poetry, ritual texts, and the cultivated arts.
以小见大	yǐ xiǎo jiàn dà	see the great through the small — the particular reveals the universal — Lit: use-small see-great. A principle of classical aesthetics and moral observation: close attention to a small thing , a grain of sand, a single conversation, a minor act of character , reveals large truths.
倚老卖老	yǐ lǎo mài lǎo	leaning on age to trade on age — exploiting seniority — Lit: lean-on-old-sell-old. The critical idiom for elders who demand deference they have not earned, throwing their age around as a trump card to win arguments or avoid accountability.
异想天开	yì xiǎng tiān kāi	wildly fanciful ideas; to have one's head in the clouds — 异 yì (strange; different; fantastical) + 想 xiǎng (thought; imagine) + 天 tiān (sky; heaven) + 开 kāi (to open). Fantastical thoughts that open the sky itself: thinking so far outside reality that heaven itself cracks open.
驿寄梅花	yì jì méi huā	sending plum blossoms by postal relay — a token of remembrance sent across great distance — From a Tang poem, but the Yuan postal relay system (驿站 yìzhàn) was the most elaborate in East Asian history, running thousands of stations across the empire with fresh horses. The phrase evokes distance, longing, and the slender thread of connection maintained across vast space.
意味深长	yì wèi shēn cháng	"meaning runs deep and long" — deeply meaningful; thought-provoking; pregnant with implication — Lit: meaning-flavor-deep-long. Used for words, actions, or expressions that reward reflection , where the surface content is less than what is being communicated. 他的话意味深长，我想了很久。"His words were deeply meaningful , I thought about them for a long time." Common in literary and critical contexts.
因材施教	yīn cái shī jiào	to teach according to aptitude: tailor instruction to each learner — Lit: based-on-talent-apply-teaching. A principle traced to Confucius, who answered the same question differently for different students. The enduring ideal of the teacher who meets each pupil where they are rather than drilling all alike.
因地制宜	yīn dì zhì yí	adapt measures to local conditions: act according to what the place calls for — Lit: follow-the-land, make-it-fitting. 因 here is the older sense, to take an existing ground as one's basis. The phrase counsels suiting your plan to local circumstances rather than forcing one rigid method everywhere.
因时制宜	yīn shí zhì yí	act according to what the time requires — A closely related four-character phrase drawing explicitly on the 时中 tradition. Where 与时俱进 emphasizes forward movement alongside the era, 因时制宜 emphasizes responsive calibration: making decisions that fit the specific circumstances of the moment.
因小失大	yīn xiǎo shī dà	lose much over a trifle: sacrifice the great for the sake of the small — Lit: because-of-the-small, lose-the-big. A warning against penny-wise foolishness: chasing a minor gain or saving while letting something far more valuable slip away.
引经据典	yǐn jīng jù diǎn	to cite the classics and quote the canon: to argue from authoritative texts — Lit: draw on classics, rely on canon. Praise for someone whose argument is grounded in scripture and the classic texts, though it can also tease a person for being bookishly pedantic.
引吭高歌	yǐn háng gāo gē	stretch the throat and sing loudly: to sing out at full voice — 吭 is the throat; the image is of someone tipping the head back to let the voice carry. Used warmly for hearty, unrestrained singing.
应接不暇	yìng jiē bù xiá	too busy to cope: more coming in than one can attend to — Lit: responding and receiving without a spare moment. 应接 is "to attend to"; 不暇 is "no leisure." Said when guests, sights, or tasks arrive faster than one can handle them. The yìng reading, "to meet."
应有尽有	yīng yǒu jìn yǒu	everything one could expect is there — 应有 (what ought to be there) + 尽有 (is exhaustively there). The stock phrase for a well-provisioned shop or feature-complete product: 这家超市商品应有尽有 "This supermarket has everything you could want." The structural mirror of 一无所有: total presence against total absence.
有来有往	yǒu lái yǒu wǎng	"there is coming and there is going" — mutual exchange; reciprocity — 往 wǎng = to go; toward. 来 and 往 together make up the full back-and-forth of social exchange: gifts, visits, words, favors. A relationship characterized by 有来有往 is balanced and healthy , neither party gives without receiving. Often used to encourage reciprocity in social or diplomatic contexts.
有始有终	yǒu shǐ yǒu zhōng	to see a thing through to the end — Literally "to have a beginning and have an end", to finish what one starts rather than abandon it midway. The 终 of completion again, valued as a virtue of persistence. The opposite is 有始无终, all start and no finish.
有缘千里来相会	yǒu yuán qiān lǐ lái xiāng huì	"those with fate meet across a thousand miles" — destiny brings people together regardless of distance — The full saying: 有缘千里来相会，无缘对面不相识。"Those with karmic connection will meet across a thousand miles; those without it won't recognize each other face to face." One of the most beloved Chinese sayings about fate and connection.
余音绕梁	yú yīn rào liáng	the lingering sound circles the roof-beams — music that haunts after it ends — From the Liezi: the singer Han E, paying for a meal with song, left a melody that circled the beams of the city gate for three days (余音绕梁，三日不绝). The standard praise for a performance that stays with you, extended freely to speeches, films, and any experience with a long afterglow.
鱼和熊掌不可兼得	yú hé xióng zhǎng bù kě jiān dé	you can't have both the fish and the bear's paw — you can't have everything you want — From Mencius: both fish (common) and bear's paw (prized delicacy) are desirable, but you cannot have both. The Chinese equivalent of "you can't have your cake and eat it too." Frequently invoked when someone has to choose between two things they want equally. 鱼和熊掌不可兼得，你只能选一个。"You can't have both.
鱼肉百姓	yú ròu bǎi xìng	to make fish-meat of the common people: to oppress and exploit them — Here 鱼肉 is a verb: to treat people as meat to be carved up. A classical condemnation of tyrannical officials, traceable to the Shǐjì. Still used to denounce abuse of power.
愚公移山	yú gōng yí shān	The Foolish Old Man Moves the Mountains — Two mountains blocked his door. He and his sons began to move them, one basket at a time. A mocker laughed. He said: when I die my sons will continue; when they die, their sons.
与时俱进	yǔ shí jù jìn	to advance together with the times — A classical phrase that became a political slogan in 2001, used to justify the Communist Party accommodating private enterprise, and criticized for justifying whatever the party currently does.
雨后春笋	yǔ hòu chūn sǔn	spring bamboo shoots after rain: springing up everywhere at once — The image of bamboo shoots erupting after spring rain. Modern usage describes new businesses, buildings, or trends multiplying rapidly.
语重心长	yǔ zhòng xīn cháng	words are weighty, feeling is deep — earnest, heartfelt advice — Lit: words-weighty-heart-long. Used to describe sincere, deeply felt words from someone who genuinely cares: a mentor's advice, a parent's warning, a teacher's final lesson before a student departs. The heaviness (重) of the words comes from the depth (长) of the feeling behind them.
欲速则不达	yù sù zé bù dá	Haste Does Not Bring Success — Speed desired, arrival missed: Confucius on why rushing produces the opposite of what you intend.
原封不动	yuán fēng bù dòng	the original seal unmoved: left exactly as it was, untouched — 原封 is the original seal on a package. To leave something 原封不动 is to return or keep it intact, never opened or altered. Everyday modern usage.
原原本本	yuán yuán běn běn	from source and root: in full and exact detail, from start to finish — The doubled 原 (source) and 本 (root). To tell something 原原本本 is to give the whole account faithfully, leaving nothing out and adding nothing.
缘木求鱼	yuán mù qiú yú	"climb a tree to look for fish" — to seek something by completely wrong means; a futile approach — Lit: climb-tree-seek-fish. Here 缘 means "to climb along" rather than "karmic connection" , a completely different sense of the character (the edge-clinging, tree-climbing meaning). A cautionary idiom from Mencius. Used when someone's method is fundamentally unsuited to their goal.
远走高飞	yuǎn zǒu gāo fēi	walk far and fly high — to go far away; to escape and seek freedom — Lit: far-walk-high-fly. The image of a bird launching into open sky. Used when someone leaves a constraining situation (a bad job, a difficult relationship, a suffocating hometown) to seek a better life elsewhere. Carries a tone of liberation and sometimes wistfulness.
约法三章	yu ē fǎ sān zhāng	a covenant of three clauses — simple, clear rules agreed upon in advance — Liu Bang's proclamation upon entering Qin territory: abolishing Qin's thousands of regulations in favor of just three (no murder, no assault, no theft). Now: any simple, clear agreement or set of rules established upfront between parties. 我们约法三章：不准迟到，不准说谎，不准逃课.
越来越好	yu è lái yu è hǎo	"better and better" — a wish or observation of continuous improvement — Not classical 成语 but a near-fixed conversational formula and a cultural mantra of optimism. The opening line of a famous song by Sun Yue (孙悦) used at the 2000 Lunar New Year gala, 越来越好 ("Better and Better"), fixed it in popular memory.
越陷越深	yu è xiàn yu è shēn	"the more one sinks, the deeper one falls" — to become increasingly trapped — A 越…越… frame applied to 陷 (xiàn, to sink, to become trapped). Used for any situation where each step makes escape harder: debt, addiction, scandal, a bad relationship.
晕头转向	yūn tóu zhuàn xiàng	"dizzy head, spinning direction" — disoriented; thrown off; bewildered — 晕 (yūn, dizzy) + 头 (tóu, head) + 转 (zhuàn, to spin) + 向 (xiàng, direction). The character 向 alone , not 方向 , is doing the directional work in the chengyu. Used when someone is overwhelmed, confused, or has lost track of what is going on.
再接再厉	zài jiē zài lì	to make further efforts; to keep working hard; to do even better next time — 再接 (take it up again) + 再厉 (sharpen again; work harder again). Used as encouragement after success or after failure: "keep it up" or "do even better." Common in school settings, performance reviews, and sports. 你这次考得很好，希望你再接再厉。
载歌载舞	zài gē zài wǔ	both singing and dancing: to celebrate with song and dance — 载…载… is a classical frame meaning doing two things at once. Standard description of festive crowds at a 春节 (chūnjié) gala or village celebration.
造反有理	zào fǎn yǒu lǐ	rebellion is justified — Mao's most radical formulation — Mao Zedong's phrase, originally from a 1939 speech, given new prominence and murderous application during the Cultural Revolution. "马克思主义的道理千条万绪，归根结底，就是一句话：造反有理。
瞻前顾后	zhān qián gù hòu	"look ahead and glance behind" — to be overly cautious; to hesitate by considering every angle — 瞻 zhān (to look ahead; to gaze forward) + 前 qián (front) + 顾 gù (to look back; to attend to) + 后 hòu (back). Looking anxiously in both temporal directions , toward the past and the future , before acting. Used critically of someone who deliberates so carefully they become paralyzed.
斩草除根	zhǎn cǎo chú gēn	cut the grass and pull the root: to eliminate a problem at its source — Lit: cut-grass-remove-root. Weeds cut at the surface grow back; only by tearing out the roots are they truly gone. The idiom urges finishing a job completely so trouble cannot return, often with a ruthless edge.
张灯结彩	zhāng dēng jié cǎi	hang lanterns and tie up silks: to decorate festively for a celebration — Lit: hang-lanterns-tie-colored-silks. The classic picture of a place dressed up for a wedding, a festival, or New Year, with lanterns strung and bright silk streamers tied everywhere. Here 结 is the literal tying of decorations.
长此以往	cháng cǐ yǐ wǎng	if this goes on for long — a warning about sustained bad behavior — Lit: long-this-by-going. Used to warn that if a current (usually bad) situation continues indefinitely, serious consequences will follow. 长此以往，身体会垮掉的 "If this keeps up, your health will collapse." Almost always negative; rarely used for positive sustained trends.
长篇大论	cháng piān dà lùn	a long-winded discourse: a lengthy, overdrawn speech or piece of writing — Literally "long chapters, big discoursing." A speech or text that goes on at excessive length. Usually mildly critical: the listener or reader feels the argument could have been made in a fraction of the words.
长生不老	cháng shēng bù lǎo	long life without ageing — immortality — The obsession that drove the First Emperor, who sent expeditions in search of the elixir of immortality and is said to have died from mercury pills meant to grant it. The chengyu names the dream of eternal life free from ageing, a recurring theme in Daoist alchemy and in the legends around 秦始皇.
长吁短叹	cháng xū duǎn tàn	long sighs and short groans: to sigh repeatedly in distress — Describes a person worn down by worry, audibly lamenting their troubles. Literary and slightly old-fashioned in tone.
着手	zháo shǒu	to set about; to begin tackling (a task) — 着 zháo (second tone, resultative) + 手 hand. Literally "to have the hands land on something" , to get a grip on a task and begin. The image is of hands making contact with work.
着眼	zháo yǎn	to focus on; to have one's eye on; to consider from the angle of — 着 zháo + 眼 eye. "To land the eye on" a target or consideration. Most commonly found as 着眼于 (focused on, with an eye to) or 着眼点 (focal point, angle of concern). Common in political and analytical writing: 着眼长远 "with an eye to the long term." A formal way to name what frame or priority is operative.
真相大白	zhēn xiàng dà bái	the true face comes fully to light — 真相 (the true state of affairs) + 大白 (made fully plain, the white of 明白). The headline phrase when a mystery resolves or a cover-up collapses. Its verb form 水落石出 ("the water recedes and the stones emerge") describes the same moment hydrologically.
真心实意	zhēn xīn shí yì	with sincere heart and genuine intent — utterly in earnest — Lit: real-heart-real-intent. The standard four-character emphatic for sincerity. 我真心实意地感谢你 "I sincerely thank you with my whole heart."
真知灼见	zhēn zhī zhuó jiàn	true knowledge and penetrating insight — genuinely wise judgment — Lit: real-knowing-burning-seeing. 灼 = scorching, here "piercing." High praise for someone whose understanding cuts through to the essential.
振兴中华	zhèn xīng Zhōng huá	revitalize China — the rallying cry of national renewal — Attributed to Sun Yat-sen and the Revive China Society (兴中会). One of the most durable slogans in modern Chinese political history, used by the Nationalist government, the Communist government, and by Xi Jinping's "China Dream" (中国梦 Zhōngguó Mèng) rhetoric.
振振有词	zhèn zhèn yǒu cí	to argue with apparent conviction: to speak plausibly while in the wrong — Lit: assured-and-having-words. Describes someone who defends a weak or unjustified position with a confident flow of arguments, sounding righteous though the case does not hold. Mildly critical in tone.
争论不休	zhēng lùn bù xiū	to argue without end: an endless, unresolved dispute — Literally "dispute and discuss without ceasing." Describes a debate that drags on with no side yielding and no resolution in sight. 不休 bù xiū "without stopping" stresses the wearisome, going-nowhere quality of the argument.
蒸蒸日上	zhēng zhēng rì shàng	"rising like steam, day by day" — prospering and flourishing continuously — 蒸 zhēng = steam; to rise in vapor. The image of steam rising without ceasing, day after day. Used of businesses, careers, nations, or any enterprise that is consistently growing and thriving. A very positive set phrase, commonly used in well-wishes and assessments of success.
正经八百	zhèng jīng bā bǎi	"upright and eight hundred" — perfectly serious; genuinely proper — 正经 (proper, serious) intensified by 八百 (eight hundred), where the large number simply amplifies the quality. The phrase insists that something is done in earnest, no joking, the real and proper thing: 正经八百地谈 (to discuss in all seriousness).
正中下怀	zhèng zhòng xià huái	hitting exactly what I hoped for — exactly what I wanted — Lit: exactly-hit-one's-heart/intention. Note: 中 here is zhòng (tone 4, to hit). The expression describes something that lands exactly where you hoped (a proposal, a result, a turn of events) as if it aimed directly at your wishes. 这个方案真是正中下怀 "This plan is exactly what I had in mind."
郑和下西洋	Zhèng Hé xià Xī yáng	Zheng He's expeditions to the Western Oceans — shorthand for Chinese maritime greatness — Used as a historical reference point in modern discussions of Chinese global engagement, maritime strategy, and the "peaceful rise" narrative. The voyages are cited as evidence that Chinese power projection has historically been tributary rather than colonial.
政通人和	zhèng tōng rén hé	government flowing smoothly, people in harmony — the ideal of good governance — From Fan Zhongyan's (范仲淹) "Yueyang Tower Preface" (岳阳楼记, 1046 CE): 政通人和，百废具兴 , when governance flows and the people are in harmony, all that was ruined can be restored. The four-character condensation became a standard formula for describing a well-governed state.
支离破碎	zhī lí pò suì	limbs apart and shattered to bits: fragmented, in pieces — Here 离 means scattered or come apart. Describes something broken into incoherent fragments, a wrecked argument, a shattered country, a disjointed text.
知错就改	zhī cuò jiù gǎi	recognize a fault and at once correct it — Praise for the rare person who, the moment they see their error, fixes it without excuse. Echoes the Confucian ideal of 过则勿惮改, do not fear to mend a fault.
知法犯法	zhī fǎ fàn fǎ	to know the law and break it anyway: to offend with full awareness — Lit: know-law-violate-law. Said of someone who understands exactly what the law forbids and breaks it regardless, which makes the offense worse, not more excusable.
知己知彼	zhī jǐ zhī bǐ	know yourself and know your opponent — thorough strategic understanding — From Sun Tzu's Art of War (孙子兵法·谋攻篇): 知彼知己，百战不殆 (zhī bǐ zhī jǐ, bǎi zhàn bù dài) , "Know your opponent and know yourself, and in a hundred battles you will not be imperiled." The fuller phrase is often compressed to 知己知彼.
知己知彼，百战不殆。	zhī jǐ zhī bǐ , bǎi zhàn bù dài .	Know yourself and know the enemy, and in a hundred battles you will never be in peril. — The single most quoted line in the Art of War and one of the best-known Chinese sayings worldwide. The full passage continues that one who knows only himself will win some and lose some, and one who knows neither will lose every time.
知难而退	zhī nán ér tuì	to retreat on seeing the difficulty: to back off when the odds are clearly against you — Lit: know-hard-and-withdraw. On recognizing how hard a thing is (知难), one 而 wisely pulls back (退). It can praise prudent judgment or, more pointedly, chide a lack of nerve.
执法如山	zhí fǎ rú shān	to enforce the law like a mountain: to uphold it firmly and without bending — Lit: enforce-law-like-mountain. Praise for an official or judge who applies the law unshakably, immovable as a mountain, favoring no one and yielding to no pressure.
纸上谈兵	zhǐ shàng tán bīng	strategize on paper — armchair theorizing without practical ability — From the Battle of Changping (260 BCE): Zhao Kuo (赵括) could discuss military theory fluently but had no battlefield experience. His appointment as Zhao's commander, reversing the caution of the veteran Lian Po, led to the encirclement and massacre of 400,000 Zhao troops.
指鹿为马	zhǐ lù wéi mǎ	point at a deer and call it a horse — deliberate falsification to identify loyalists — Zhao Gao presented a deer to the court and declared it a horse, watching who agreed and who dared contradict him. Those who said "deer" were later executed.
指指点点	zhǐ zhǐ diǎn diǎn	to point and gesture: to criticize or gossip about others behind their backs — The reduplicated 指点 zhǐdiǎn (to point out, to instruct) turns sour here: to keep pointing fingers and passing judgment on what is not one's business.
治国安邦	zhì guó ān bāng	"govern the state, pacify the realm" — the task of statecraft — 治国 (govern the state) + 安邦 (stabilize the realm; 邦 bāng, polity, domain). The paired formulation for the ruler's task: governing the state and maintaining peace across the whole domain. A classical political phrase that appears in pre-Qin texts.
致良知	zhì liáng zhī	Extending innate moral knowledge. — The core practice of Wang Yangming's philosophy. 致 means "to extend" or "to bring to bear fully." The practice is not the construction of moral knowledge through study but the removal of whatever obstructs the 良知 that is always already present.
置身事外	zhì shēn shì wài	to place oneself outside the matter: to stay aloof, keep clear of it — Lit: place-body-affair-outside. To keep oneself out of an affair, neither helping nor interfering. Often mildly critical, of someone who stands apart when they ought to be involved.
中流砥柱	zhōng liú dǐ zhù	a pillar standing firm in the middle of the current — a mainstay; steadfast support — Lit: mid-current-firm-pillar. The image of a rocky pillar in the middle of a fast-moving river, standing unmoved while the water rushes past. Used to describe a person, organization, or principle that holds firm and provides stability amid turmoil.
众所周知	zhòng suǒ zhōu zhī	as everyone knows; it is common knowledge — A formal phrase, literally "what the crowd all knows," used to introduce a widely accepted fact. 众 (the masses) + 周知 (universally known). Common in writing and speeches as a way of appealing to shared knowledge, the Chinese equivalent of "as is well known."
重蹈覆辙	chóng dǎo fù zhé	to follow the overturned cart's tracks again: to repeat a past disaster — Lit: again-tread-overturned-ruts. The chóng reading. To make the same ruinous mistake that wrecked others before, having failed to learn from it.
重于泰山	zhòng yú tài shān	weightier than Mount Tai: of the gravest possible significance — From Sima Qian: a death may be 重于泰山 (weightier than Mt Tai) or 轻于鸿毛 (lighter than a goose feather), depending on what it is given for. The zhòng reading, weight as worth.
周公吐哺	Zhōu Gōng tǔ bǔ	the Duke of Zhou spits out his food — a ruler's eagerness to receive talent — From a passage in the Shiji: the Duke of Zhou was so eager to receive visiting scholars and advisors that when eating he would stop mid-meal, spit out his food, and go to meet them, lest a talented man leave without an audience.
助人为乐	zhù rén wéi lè	to take joy in helping others — Literally "to help others as one's joy," to find genuine pleasure in aiding people. A prized social virtue, often used to praise the selflessly helpful. Built on 助 (to aid), the same character at the heart of 帮助.
装模作样	zhuāng mú zuò yàng	to fake a shape and put on a show: to playact, to put on airs — Lit: feign-mould-make-shape. To strike a false pose, going through the motions of sincerity or effort while meaning none of it. A pointed way to call out pretense.
追本溯源	zhuī běn sù yuán	trace back to the root and seek the source — 追 (pursue) + 本 (root) + 溯 (go upstream) + 源 (source). Used for rigorous investigation of origins: of a word, a practice, a family line, a historical claim. The metaphor of going upstream to find the source of a river runs through classical Chinese thought about genealogy and textual scholarship.
字斟句酌	zì zhēn jù zhuó	weighing every character, deliberating every sentence — writing with extreme care and precision — 斟酌 zhēnzhuó = to deliberate; to weigh carefully. Used for careful writing, diplomatic language, or painstaking editing. A compliment to someone who writes with rigor. 这份合同是字斟句酌写成的。(This contract was written with extreme care.)
自相矛盾	zì xiāng máo dùn	to contradict oneself: spear against one's own shield — Lit: self-mutually-spear-shield. From the fable of a peddler who swore his spear (矛) could pierce anything and his shield (盾) could stop anything, until a bystander asked what would happen if he used the one on the other.
自由自在	zì yóu zì zài	free and at ease: unhurried, answerable to no one — Lit: self-free-self-present. A doubled 自 that paints a life lived on one's own terms, comfortable and unconstrained, like a bird with the whole sky to itself.
自作自受	zì zuò zì shòu	what you make, you suffer: to reap the consequences of your own actions — Lit: self-do-self-bear. The Chinese counterpart of "you made your bed, now lie in it." Said of trouble that a person has brought entirely upon themselves.
走马观花	zǒu mǎ guān huā	"look at flowers from a galloping horse" — a superficial, rushing view — 走马 (galloping horse) + 观花 (observe flowers). The mounted rider moves too fast to see the flowers properly , their gaze skims the surface without stopping to appreciate what is there. A criticism of rushed, inattentive engagement with a subject.
左顾右盼	zuǒ gù yòu pàn	"look left, glance right" — looking around hesitantly or alertly; distracted attention — 左 (left) + 顾 (to look back; to turn and look) + 右 (right) + 盼 (to look; to gaze with expectation). The eyes move ceaselessly left and right , taking in everything around, unable or unwilling to settle on a single point.
左右逢源	zuǒ yòu féng yuán	"meet a spring on the left and on the right" — resourceful; successful in every direction — 左右 (left and right) + 逢 (to encounter; to meet with) + 源 (a spring, source of water). Wherever you turn, you find a source of nourishment or support. Originally from Mencius (孟子·离娄下): a scholar who has deeply internalized principle will find it arising naturally from every direction.
左右为难	zuǒ yòu wéi nán	"left and right both difficult" — caught in a dilemma; no good option in either direction — 左右 (left and right) + 为 (to be; causing) + 难 (difficult). Whichever direction you move , left or right , you face difficulty. The idiom captures the specific kind of dilemma where the problem is bilateral: pleasing one side offends the other, taking one path closes the other.
作茧自缚	zuò jiǎn zì fù	to spin a cocoon and bind oneself: to trap yourself by your own doing — Lit: make-cocoon-self-bind. As a silkworm spins the cocoon that encloses it, a person can create rules, habits, or schemes that end up trapping them.
做贼心虚	zuò zéi xīn xū	having done the theft, the heart is uneasy — a guilty conscience betrays itself — 做 + 贼 zéi (thief) + 心 xīn (heart) + 虚 xū (empty, weak, unsteady). One who has done wrong cannot keep an easy heart; the inner unease leaks out as nervousness and over-caution. The phrase observes that guilt is self-announcing, that the wrongdoer's own jumpiness gives the game away.
的话	de huà	if; in the case that — The soft conditional: how 的话 marks a premise without committing to it.
多少 / 几	duō shao / jǐ	how many, how much — The two question words for quantity: 几 for small countable numbers and 多少 for larger or unknown amounts, why 几 needs a measure word and 多少 may skip it, and how to ask prices and ages.
而且	ér qiě	moreover, and furthermore — Additive conjunction that layers information: not just "and" but "and on top of that."
还是 / 或者	hái shi / huò zhě	"or" in questions vs. statements — Chinese splits English "or" in two: 还是 offers a choice in a question, 或者 offers an alternative in a statement. Using the wrong one is a classic error.
结果补语	jié guǒ bǔ yǔ	resultative complements — Chinese verbs describe the attempt; a second syllable reports the outcome. Listening is 听, but understanding what you heard is 听懂.
就 vs. 才	jiù / cái	sooner vs. later — The timing and emphasis pair: speaker attitude encoded in one syllable.
可能	kěn éng	maybe, possible — The all-purpose word for possibility, working as adverb, adjective, and noun, and how it differs from 也许, 或许, 大概, and the modal 会.
可能补语	kěn éng bǔ yǔ	potential complements — Slide 得 or 不 inside a verb-result pair and it states ability: 听懂 is understood, 听得懂 is can understand, 听不懂 is the learner's anthem.
来	lái / qù	去 · directional complements — 上来, 下去, 出去, 进来: the motion complement system mapped.
离	lí	away from; distance — The coverb for distance and separation: the A 离 B + distance pattern for "how far", the verb sense "to leave / part from", and how 离 differs from 从 and 到 in marking space and time.
离合词	lí hé cí	separable verbs — Words that unzip: 见面 is to meet, but meeting him is 跟他见面 and meeting once is 见一次面, because the "word" is secretly verb plus object.
量词	liàng cí	measure words — 个, 只, 条, 本, 张: the classifier taxonomy and the logic behind it.
没有	méi yǒu	negation of possession and past action — The two most important negation characters, 不 and 没, and why they cannot be swapped.
起来	qǐ lái	directional complement: upward, inward, beginning — The most versatile directional complement in Mandarin: three distinct grammatical lives.
如果…就	rú guǒ jiù	if … then — The foundational conditional frame: the engine of hypothetical reasoning in Mandarin.
时态	shí tài	past, present, future — Mandarin verbs never change form: time words, 了, 过, 着, 在, and 会 do the work English does with conjugation.
是…的	shì … de	the cleft construction — Focus and past-detail marking: the construction that trips every learner.
虽然…但是	suī rán dàn shì	although … but — The concession frame: granting the premise but pivoting to the real point.
太…了	tài le	too, excessively — The emphatic degree structure: 太…了 expresses excess and strong feeling, from complaints to enthusiastic praise.
为什么	wèi shén me	why — The neutral question word for cause and reason, how to answer it with 因为 or 为了, and how a plain 为什么 differs from the surprised 怎么 and the purpose-asking 为了什么.
一边…一边	yī biān yī biān	while doing; at the same time — Simultaneous actions: the frame for describing two things happening at once.
一点儿 / 有点儿	yì diǎn r / yǒu diǎn r	a little — The classic confusion pair: 有点儿 comes before an adjective and leans negative ("a bit too…"), while 一点儿 comes after and is neutral ("a little more").
一下	yí xià	the verb-softener — The verbal-quantity word that makes an action brief and casual, how verb + 一下 softens a request, and how it differs from verb reduplication and from the time-word 一会儿.
一样 / 像	yí yàng / xiàng	same and similar — How to say two things are the same (A 跟 B 一样) or alike (A 像 B), and how this differs from the unequal comparison with 比.
已经	yǐ jīng	already — Mandarin's 'already' adverb: how 已经 differs from English, its placement before the verb, and its relationship with 了 and 还没有.
因为…所以	yīn wèi suǒ yǐ	because…therefore — The causal conjunction pair: 因为 states the cause and 所以 announces the result, forming a two-clause logical structure that Chinese uses more explicitly than English.
应该	yīng gāi	the modal "should" — The modal verb for "should, ought to," covering both moral obligation and confident inference, and how it differs from 得, 要, and 必须.
越来越	yu è lái yu è	more and more; increasingly — The degree escalation pattern: expressing a quality that grows continuously over time.
在	zài	location, existence, and the progressive — One of the busiest words in Mandarin: the verb "to be at," the location coverb "at/in," and the progressive marker for ongoing action.
正反问	zhèng fǎn wèn	A-not-A questions — Chinese asks yes-no questions by offering both answers: 去不去 is "going, not going?", and the pattern replaces 吗 with built-in neutrality.
只有…才	zhǐ yǒu cái	only if…then; the restrictive conditional — The exclusive condition: only when X is met can Y happen. The grammatical frame for necessity.
*把他喜欢	* bǎ tā xǐ huān	WRONG — stative/emotional verbs cannot take 把 — Stative verbs (是, 有, 像), mental-state verbs (喜欢, 爱, 恨, 讨厌, 知道, 认识, 觉得), and perception verbs (看见, 听见, 闻到) cannot be used in the 把 construction. The 把 construction requires an agent who deliberately acts on a concrete object with a physical or tangible result.
*我把他看见了	* wǒ bǎ tā kàn jian le	WRONG — unaffected objects cannot take 把 — The object of the 把 construction must be affected by the action , it must undergo a change, be moved, be consumed, be transformed, or be disposed of. Perception and cognition verbs (看见 see, 听见 hear, 闻到 smell) involve objects that are registered but not physically affected.
阿弥陀经	Ā mí tuó Jīng	Smaller Sukhāvatīvyūha Sutra — the daily liturgy sutra — Also called the 小经 Xiǎo Jīng (Small Sutra). The briefest of the three, translated by Kumārajīva in 402 CE in a Chinese so elegant that it became the definitive liturgical text.
阿是穴	ā shì xu é	Ashi points — Ashi points: tender spots with no fixed location, needled wherever the patient says "Ah, yes, that is it!" when pressed. Not on any meridian map, but recognized in classical texts as clinically valid.
艾草	ài cǎo	mugwort; wormwood (Artemisia argyi) — 艾 (ài, mugwort) + 草 (cǎo, herb, grass). Hung over doorways on the Double Fifth to repel evil and insects, owing to its strong fragrance. Also used in traditional Chinese medicine (艾灸 àijiǔ, moxibustion) and in cooking (青团 qīngtuán, green rice cakes flavored with mugwort, eaten at Qīngmíng).
爱国	ài guó	patriotic; to love one's country — 爱 ài (love) + 国 guó (country). Patriotism. A deeply freighted term in Chinese political culture: 爱国主义 àiguó zhǔyì is patriotism as ideology, 爱国人士 patriotic personages. In everyday use, simply "to love one's country."
爱好	ài hào	hobby; interest; pastime — 爱 ài (to love) + 好 hào (to be fond of). A thing one loves doing , a hobby or passion. One of the first personal vocabulary items learners need: 你有什么爱好？(What are your hobbies?)
爱护	ài hù	to cherish and protect; to take loving care of — 爱 ài + 护 hù (to protect; to guard). To love something by protecting it. Used for public property (爱护环境), younger people (爱护学生), and cultural heritage. A civic and ethical register.
爱面子	ài miàn zi	to be very concerned with face; to care about appearances — 爱 (to love; to value; to be attached to). 爱面子 describes someone who places unusually high value on face , so much so that their behavior is shaped by the desire to maintain appearances even when it conflicts with practical sense.
爱情	ài qíng	romantic love; love as a feeling — 爱 ài + 情 qíng (feeling; sentiment; romance). The noun for romantic love as an abstract force or state. 爱情故事 = love story; 爱情电影 = romance film; 谈恋爱 is the verb for being in a relationship, but 爱情 names the thing itself.
安定	ān dìng	settled; secure; to settle (a society, a life) — 安 ān (peace; safe) + 定 dìng (settled). A secure, untroubled state of life or society, with the calm of 安 added to the fixity of 定: 安定的生活 "a settled life," 安定团结 "stability and unity."
安全	ān quán	safe; safety
安全第一	ān quán dì yī	safety first; safety above all — 安全 ānquán (safety) + 第一 dì-yī (first). A fixed slogan ranking safety as the top priority. Here 第一 means "the foremost concern," not a numbered position.
鹌鹑蛋	ān chún dàn	quail egg
案件	àn jiàn	a legal case; an incident under investigation — 案 àn (case; record; desk on which records were kept) + 件 jiàn (a piece). A discrete legal or police matter, treated as a single file of business. 案 alone meant the official desk and then the file of papers on it; with 件 it becomes one such case, counted and pursued on its own.
八成	bā chéng	eighty percent; most likely; probably — 八 bā + 成 chéng (a tenth, a tenth part). Eight tenths, eighty percent. Literally a proportion (八成新, eighty percent new, that is, in good used condition), but very commonly an adverb of high probability: 八成是他, "it's most likely him," "I'd say eighty percent it's him." A natural everyday way to express…
八大菜系	bā dà cài xì	the Eight Great Cuisines — every regional banquet runs on baijiu
八股文	bā gǔ wén	the eight-legged essay — the rigid Ming-Qing examination form — The prescribed essay format mandated by the Ming dynasty (from 1487 onward) and retained through the Qing: eight sections in a fixed sequence, strict parallelism requirements, a prescribed length, and no deviation from the topic as given.
八卦	bā guà	the eight trigrams; gossip — 八 bā + 卦 guà (trigram; divinatory figure). The eight three-line figures of yin and yang at the foundation of the Yijing, used in divination, feng shui, and cosmology. In colloquial modern usage the same word means gossip: 八卦新闻 (tabloid news), 别八卦了 (stop gossiping).
八卦镜	bā guà jìng	The ba-gua mirror , an octagonal mirror surrounded by the eight trigrams (八卦) of the I Ching, hung above doorways to deflect negative energy or bad feng shui directed at the entrance. The mirror reflects harmful influences back toward their source.
八旗	bā qí	Eight Banners (Manchu military system)
八仙	bā xiān	the Eight Immortals — 八 bā + 仙 xiān (immortal; transcendent). The Eight Immortals of Daoist legend, a band of figures each with a distinctive emblem and power, including Lü Dongbin, He Xiangu, and Iron-Crutch Li.
八月	bā yu è	August; the eighth month — 八 bā + 月 yuè (month). August in the solar calendar. The fifteenth day of the eighth lunar month is the Mid-Autumn Festival (中秋节), one of the most important holidays of the year, when families gather to admire the full moon and eat mooncakes.
八字	bā zì	birth horoscope (Four Pillars)
把包放下	bǎ bāo fàng xià	put the bag down — 放 fàng (to place; put) + 下 xià (down; into a lower position). A simple directional 把 sentence , the object (bag) is moved to a lower location. 下 here signals downward movement or lowering into position. The 把 foregrounds the deliberate, targeted nature of the placing.
把窗户打开	bǎ chuāng hu dǎ kāi	to open the window (strike it into an open state) — 打 dǎ (to hit; to strike , used as a general action verb with many objects) + 开 kāi (open; apart; into an open state). The combination 打开 means "open" , the object is moved into an open or separated state. 开 as a resultative complement implies separation, opening, or dispersal.
把门关上	bǎ mén guān shàng	to close the door (shut it into the closed position) — 关 guān (to close) + 上 shàng (up; into a closed/attached state). Here 上 is a resultative complement indicating that the door has been moved into the closed, shut position.
把椅子搬进来	bǎ yǐ zi bān jìn lái	bring the chair inside (move it in-coming direction) — 搬 bān (to move heavy things; to transport) + 进来 jìnlái (in-coming; into this space). The directional complement 进来 specifies both the direction (in) and the orientation toward the speaker (来). The 把 construction signals the chair is a definite, specific object being moved as a deliberate act.
把作业做完	bǎ zuò yè zuò wán	to finish the homework (do it through to completion) — 做 zuò (to do) + 完 wán (to finish; to exhaust; resultative complement). The 完 is the result , the homework has been completely done. Without 完, the sentence would be ungrammatical: *把作业做. 完 is one of the most common resultative complements in the 把 construction.
霸主	bà zhǔ	hegemon lord — power exercised without moral mandate — 霸 bà (dominate by force) + 主 zhǔ (lord, master). Mencius drew the sharpest formulation of the distinction between 王 wáng (true kingship through moral virtue) and 霸 bà (hegemonic dominance through military and economic power): 以力假仁者霸 "one who uses force while pretending to benevolence is a hegemon" (…
白白	bái bái	in vain; for nothing
白菜	bái cài	Chinese cabbage; bok choy family — 白 bái (white) + 菜 cài (vegetable). The pale-stalked cabbage that anchors northern winter cooking. So cheap and plentiful that 白菜价 "cabbage price" means dirt cheap.
白吃	bái chī	to eat for free; to freeload
白毫	bái háo	white down — the fine hairs on young buds
白话	bái huà	vernacular Chinese; plain speech
白话文	bái huà wén	vernacular written Chinese — 白 bái (plain; white; clear; spoken) + 话 huà (speech; talk) + 文 wén (written text). Writing that matches how people actually speak. 白话 literally = "plain speech," the ordinary spoken language as opposed to the elevated classical register.
白酒	bái jiǔ	baijiu — the spirit most often paired with a hotpot meal
白米	bái mǐ	white rice; polished rice
白切鸡	bái qiē jī	White-Cut Chicken
白色	bái sè	white (the color); the color white
白天	bái tiān	daytime
白衣	bái yī	white-robed (form of Guanyin)
白衣观音	Bái yī Guān yīn	White-Robed Guanyin — the most common home altar image — 白衣 báiyī (white robe) + 观音. The seated figure in a white robe, often with a willow branch and a small vase, depicted on or near a lotus throne. This is the Guanyin of domestic altars across Taiwan, Hong Kong, Macau, and Southeast Asian Chinese diaspora communities.
百花	bǎi huā	a hundred flowers; the 1956 campaign
百货	bǎi huò	all kinds of goods; department store — 百 (all kinds of) + 货 huò (goods, merchandise). 百货公司 (bǎihuò gōngsī, "all-goods company") is the standard Chinese term for a department store.
百家	bǎi jiā	a hundred schools; all schools of thought
百科	bǎi kē	encyclopedia; all branches of knowledge — 百 (all) + 科 kē (branch of study, department, subject). The term for encyclopedia: 百科全书 (bǎikē quánshū, "complete book of all branches of knowledge"). 百度百科 is the major Chinese-language wiki encyclopedia.
百年	bǎi nián	a hundred years; a full generation; a lifetime — 百 (hundred) + 年 nián (year). A century, but also a metonym for a full human span or a generational unit of history. 百年大计 (bǎinián dàjì, "a great plan for a hundred years") means a long-term fundamental strategy.
百年屈辱	Bǎi nián Qū rǔ	Century of Humiliation
百日	bǎi rì	The hundred-day mourning period following a death — The hundred-day mourning period following a death. During this time the bereaved family observes restrictions on celebration and festive activity. A commemoration ceremony at the end of the hundred days formally closes the mourning period.
百姓	bǎi xìng	the common people; ordinary citizens — 百 (hundred; all) + 姓 xìng (surname). The full range of surnames = everyone who is not nobility. The warm, slightly literary word for "the people." 老百姓 (lǎobǎixìng) adds 老 (old, familiar) for a more affectionate, colloquial tone. Political speeches say 人民; everyday speech says 老百姓.
败北	bài běi	to be defeated; to suffer a loss — 败 (bài, to be defeated, to lose) + 北 (běi, to flee , the original meaning). The classical military compound where 北 carries its original "flight in defeat" meaning. Used in formal and literary contexts for losing a battle, an election, or a competition.
败坏	bài huài	to corrupt, ruin
斑马	bān mǎ	zebra
办法	bàn fǎ	a way; a means; a solution — 办 bàn (to handle; to manage) + 法 fǎ (way). The practical way to get a concrete thing done or to solve a problem at hand: 想办法 "to think of a way," 没办法 "there's nothing to be done."
办公室	bàng ōng shì	office
帮手	bāng shǒu	helper; assistant — 帮 bāng (to help) + 手 shǒu (person; hand). A helper , someone who lends a hand. Also 帮忙 bāng máng (to help out , the common spoken form) and 帮助 bāngzhù (to assist , more formal).
帮我翻译一下	bāng wǒ fān yì yí xià	please translate this for me — For your interpreter, or for the English-speaking salesperson when the engineer goes too fast. Using an interpreter for one hard passage and Chinese for the rest is common and costs no face.
帮我一下	bāng wǒ yí xià	give me a hand; help me out — A model of 一下 as a politeness softener. The bare 帮我 ("help me") is blunt; adding 一下 frames it as a small, quick favor and sounds far friendlier. Note that 一下 can follow the object here (帮我一下) because 帮 takes a person directly. The same softening works across requests: 看一下, 等一下, 问一下.
榜样	bǎng yàng	role model; good example to follow — 榜 bǎng (a posted list, a board) + 样 yàng (model). A person held up as a pattern for others: 树立榜样 "to set an example," 学习的榜样 "a model to learn from."
棒喝	bàng hè	the shout-and-blow — spontaneous awakening techniques — 棒 bàng (staff, blow) + 喝 hè (shout, roar). Two of the most distinctive Chan teaching methods: a sudden shout (associated especially with the master Linji 临济, whose shout 喝 became the name of the Rinzai school) and a strike with a staff (associated with Deshan 德山).
包间	bāo jiān	private dining room
宝船	bǎo chuán	treasure ship (Zheng He's flagship)
饱餐	bǎo cān	to eat a hearty meal — 饱 (full) + 餐 (cān, meal): to eat heartily, to feast. Often appears as 饱餐一顿 (bǎocān yí dùn, to have a good filling meal), with a faint literary warmth.
饱含	bǎo hán	to be brimming with — 饱 (full) + 含 (hán, to hold inside): to be charged with, brimming with. Used for tears, feeling, or meaning, as in 饱含深情 (bǎohán shēnqíng, brimming with deep feeling).
饱和	bǎo hé	saturated, saturation — 饱 (full) + 和 (hé, to mix, to combine): saturation. A technical term for a solution, market, or air that can absorb no more, as in 市场已经饱和 (shìchǎng yǐjīng bǎohé, the market is already saturated).
饱满	bǎo mǎn	full, plump; brimming — 饱 (full) + 满 (mǎn, full to the brim): plump and rounded, or figuratively brimming with vigor. Describes grain, fruit, and spirits alike, as in 精神饱满 (jīngshén bǎomǎn, full of energy).
保全	bǎo quán	to preserve intact; to keep safe and whole — 保 bǎo (to protect) + 全 quán (intact). To keep something whole and unharmed through a threat: 保全性命 "to preserve one's life," 保全名誉 "to keep one's reputation intact." The intact sense made active.
报仇	bào chóu	to avenge, take revenge — 报 (to requite) plus 仇 (chóu, enmity): to pay back a wrong. The dark twin of 报答, turning the same logic of return toward vengeance.
报酬	bào chou	remuneration, pay
报答	bào dá	to repay (a kindness) — 报 (to requite) plus 答 (dá, to answer): to answer a kindness with thanks made real. Reserved for repaying gratitude, a warmer word than the neutral 回报.
报警	bào jǐng	to call the police; sound an alarm
报名	bào míng	to sign up, register — 报 (to report) plus 名 (míng, name): to report your name onto a list. The standard verb for signing up or enrolling for a class, a contest, or an exam.
报销	bào xiāo	to claim reimbursement
报纸	bào zhǐ	newspaper — 报 (the thing announced) plus 纸 (zhǐ, paper): the daily report rendered on paper. The everyday word for a printed newspaper, and the head noun behind titles like 《人民日报》.
暴力	bào lì	violence; brute force — 暴 bào (violent; cruel) + 力 lì (force). Force used to harm, the dark side of 力: 家庭暴力 "domestic violence," 暴力行为 "violent behavior." Here strength has turned destructive.
暴雨	bào yǔ	torrential rain; downpour
悲观	bēi guān	pessimistic
北斗七星	běi dǒu qī xīng	the seven stars of the Big Dipper — 北斗 (the Northern Dipper) + 七星 (seven stars). The seven bright stars of the Big Dipper, central to Chinese astronomy, navigation, and Daoist ritual.
北方	běi fāng	the North; northern
北京	Běi jīng	Beijing (capital of China) — 北 (north) + 京 (capital). The Northern Capital , counterpart to 南京 Nánjīng (Southern Capital). Beijing has served as the capital of the Yuan, Ming, and Qing dynasties, and of the People's Republic. The name dates to 1403 when the Yongle Emperor moved the Ming court north from Nanjing.
北京话	Běi jīng huà	Beijing dialect — Beijing dialect: the basis of standard Mandarin (普通话 pǔtōnghuà). Beijing speech is characterized by heavy retroflex consonants and the 儿化 (érhuà) suffix that makes 这里 → 这儿, 哪里 → 哪儿. A strong Beijing accent is audible and distinct.
北洋军	Běi yáng Jūn	Beiyang Army (Yuan's power base)
背后	bèi hòu	behind someone's back; in secret — 背 bèi (back of the body; to turn one's back on) + 后 (behind). The space behind a person's back , the zone they cannot see. In figurative use, 背后 means to act covertly, without someone's knowledge or consent. 在背后说人坏话 (to speak ill of someone behind their back) is a strong social criticism.
被打了	bèi dǎ le	got hit; was beaten (by an unspecified agent) — When the agent is unknown, irrelevant, or deliberately omitted, the 被 construction works without naming who did the action. The 被 alone signals passive and adversarial meaning.
被动	bèi dòng	passive
被发现了	bèi fā xiàn le	was discovered; got caught (typically unwanted) — Discovery can be neutral or positive in English (a new species was discovered), but in Chinese the 被 frame typically implies the subject was caught or uncovered against its will or to its disadvantage. 被发现 often implies that someone was hiding something that has now been exposed.
被自杀	bèi zì shā	to be "suicided" — killed and declared a suicide — The most striking 被XX neologism. 自杀 zìshā means "to kill oneself" , a self-directed, voluntary act. Making it passive (被自杀) creates a grammatical impossibility that points to a political reality: a death classified as suicide that was, in the speaker's understanding, not self-chosen.
奔跑	bēn pǎo	to dash; to run hard; to gallop — 奔 (bēn, to rush; to gallop) + 跑. A more vivid and literary form than plain 跑. 奔 originally described the gallop of a horse; 奔跑 carries that sense of full-speed exertion.
本草	běn cǎo	Materia medica — Materia medica: the traditional term for the pharmacological literature and its subject matter.
本纪	běn jì	Basic Annals — rulers and dynasties — Twelve chapters chronicling the central line of power: the legendary sage-kings, the Xia, Shang, and Zhou, the Qin, and the Han emperors down to Wu.
本来	běn lái	originally; at first; (it goes without saying that) — 本 běn (root; origin) + 来 lái (come). What was the case from the start, before things changed: 我本来想去 "I originally meant to go (but didn't)." It often carries a quiet sense that the original state was the natural or proper one.
本领	běn lǐng	skill; capability
本人	běn rén	oneself; the person in question; I (formal) — 本 běn (this; one's own) + 人 rén (person). The very person concerned, as opposed to a representative or a rumor. On forms and in formal speech it means "I myself": 本人同意 "I personally agree."
本事	běn shi	ability; skill; what one is capable of — 本 běn (root; one's own) + 事 shì (matter). The capacities a person carries in themselves, their real skill rather than their luck or connections: 有本事 "to have what it takes." A close synonym is 本领 běnlǐng.
本质	běn zhì	essence
本子	běn zi	notebook; exercise book; pad — 本 běn (volume) + 子 zi (suffix). A blank or ruled book to write in. Here 本 takes the toneless noun-suffix 子, exactly as in 桌子 and 房子.
笨蛋	bèn dàn	idiot, fool — 笨 (bèn, stupid, clumsy) + 蛋: a fool or dimwit. Among close friends it softens into teasing, even affection, but aimed at a stranger it lands as a real insult.
鼻子	bí zi	nose
比如	bǐ rú	for instance
比如说	bǐ rú shuō	"For example" or "like, for instance — "For example" or "like, for instance." Introduces a concrete illustration of an abstract point. Among the most useful phrases for explaining anything. The shorter 比如 works identically; 比如说 just adds a beat of preparation.
笔法	bǐ fǎ	brush method; stroke technique
笔画	bǐ huà	stroke; brushstroke — 笔 bǐ (brush; pen) + 画 huà (to draw; stroke). The individual strokes that make up a character. Chinese characters are analyzed by stroke count (几画 jǐ huà "how many strokes?") and stroke order (笔顺 bǐshùn).
笔记本	bǐ jì běn	notebook; laptop computer — 笔记 bǐjì + 本 běn (a bound volume; classifier for books). Originally: a bound notebook for handwritten jottings. Now also: laptop computer (笔记本电脑 bǐjìběn diànnǎo), the device that replaced the paper notebook for many people, while keeping the name.
笔名	bǐ míng	Pen name — Pen name: a literary pseudonym. Many Chinese writers and poets are better known by their pen names than their given names. Lu Xun (鲁迅) was born Zhou Shuren (周树人); his pen name is how he is universally known.
笔墨	bǐ mò	brush and ink; brushwork as a concept
笔顺	bǐ shùn	stroke order
笔者	bǐ zhě	the present writer; this author (formal self-reference) — 笔 (brush; pen; writing) + 者. Used in formal written Chinese as a modest self-reference: "the one holding the brush," meaning the author of the text you are reading.
毕业	bì yè	to graduate; graduation — 毕 bì (to finish; to complete) + 业 yè (studies). To complete one's course of study: 大学毕业 "to graduate from university," 毕业典礼 "graduation ceremony." A separable verb, so the object slots in the middle: 毕了业 "have graduated."
边关	biāng uān	border pass; frontier checkpoint — 边 (biān, border; edge) + 关 (guān, pass; strategic gate). The fortified checkpoints through the wall where military control, taxation, and passage were managed.
边远	biān yuǎn	remote and peripheral (border regions)
便宜一点	pián yi yì diǎn	a bit cheaper — The basic request, best softened. 价格有点高 (the price is a bit high) states the problem without accusing; 还有空间吗 (is there still room?) invites a counteroffer without naming one.
辩论	biàn lùn	to debate; to argue (a formal case) — 辩 biàn (to dispute, to argue points) + 论. To debate opposing positions, each side marshalling arguments to win. The standard word for a debate competition (辩论赛 biànlùnsài) and for adversarial argument generally.
辫子	biàn zi	the Manchu queue hairstyle
标点	biāo diǎn	punctuation
标记	biāo jì	a marker; a label; to mark — 标 biāo (to label, to mark out) + 记 jì (a mark). A marker or tag that identifies a thing, and the act of placing it: 在地图上标记位置 "to mark a location on a map."
标题	biāo tí	headline; title; caption — 标 biāo (to mark; label) + 题 tí (heading). The title line printed at the top of an article, book, or section: 新闻标题 "a news headline," 大标题 "a big banner headline." Literally the marked heading.
表达	biǎo dá	to express: to convey; to articulate (thoughts and feelings) — 表 biǎo (to bring to the surface) + 达 dá (to reach; to arrive at; to get through). To convey thoughts or feelings so that they reach another person clearly. The 达 element adds the sense of successful transmission: not just showing something, but getting it across.
表哥	biǎo gē	older male cousin — 表 (maternal/external) + 哥. An older male cousin on the mother's side, or through a paternal aunt. The 表 prefix marks cousins outside the father's patriline, distinct from 堂哥 (tánggē).
表格	biǎo gé	form: a table; a chart; a grid laid out for filling in — 表 biǎo (a chart; a surface where data is laid out) + 格 gé (a grid; a square; a cell). A printed or digital form, table, or grid: anything with rows and cells to be read or filled in.
表姐	biǎo jiě	older female cousin
表面	biǎo miàn	surface; outside; outward appearance — 表 biǎo (outer; to show) + 面 miàn (face). The outer surface of a thing, and by extension the surface meaning as opposed to the truth beneath: 表面上 biǎomiàn shàng "on the surface, ostensibly."
表面处理	biǎo miàn chǔ lǐ	surface finish — The finishing family: 抛光 (pāoguāng) polishing, 电解 (diànjiě) electrolytic polishing for liners, 喷涂 (pēntú) spray painting, 喷粉 (pēnfěn) powder coating, 丝印 (sīyìn) screen printing, 镭雕 (léidiāo) laser engraving. 涂层 (túcéng) is the coating itself; 掉漆 (diàoqī) is paint coming off.
表皮	biǎo pí	outer skin: the epidermis; the outermost layer or rind — 表 biǎo (outer) + 皮 pí (skin; hide; rind). The outermost layer of a living thing: the epidermis of human skin, the rind of a fruit, the bark-like surface of a plant. A precise term used in biology, medicine, and everyday talk about peels and surfaces.
表情	biǎo qíng	facial expression
表示	biǎo shì	to express: to indicate; to show (a feeling, attitude, or position) — 表 biǎo (to bring to the surface) + 示 shì (to show; to indicate; to make manifest). To express or indicate an attitude, opinion, or feeling, often through a word, gesture, or symbolic act. 表示 frequently introduces what someone states or signals: 表示同意 (express agreement), 表示感谢 (express thanks).
表现	biǎo xiàn	to show; to display; (noun) performance, conduct, showing — 表 biǎo (outer; to express) + 现 xiàn (show). To make an inner quality visible on the outside. As a noun it is one's showing or conduct: 他今天表现很好 "he did very well today."
表演	biǎo yǎn	to perform: to act; a performance; a show — 表 biǎo (to show outwardly) + 演 yǎn (to act; to perform; to develop). To perform before an audience, and the performance itself: music, dance, drama, magic, acrobatics.
别人	bié rén	other people; someone else (colloquial) — 别 bié (other; different; another) + 人 rén. The default, everyday word for "other people" in spoken Mandarin. Neutral register, very high frequency. Used in advice, comparisons, complaints, and social commentary; the most natural choice whenever you mean "other people" in conversation.
宾馆	bīng uǎn	hotel, guesthouse — 宾 bīn (guest) + 馆. A house for guests, that is, a hotel. The word keeps the old hospitality sense of 馆 right on its sleeve.
兵法	bīng fǎ	military strategy; the art of war
并非	bìng fēi	is not in fact; really is not — 并 bìng (actually; emphatic) + 非 fēi (not). A firm, slightly formal "is not at all," used to push back against an assumption: 并非如此 "that is not the case," 并非不可能 "not in fact impossible."
病毒	bìng dú	virus — 病 + 毒 (dú, poison): a "sickness poison," the standard word for a biological virus and, by extension, a computer virus. The same term covers the flu and the malware on your hard drive.
病房	bìng fáng	hospital ward, sickroom — 病 + 房 (fáng, room): the room where patients are kept, a hospital ward. It pairs naturally with 病床 (bìngchuáng, hospital bed), bringing the original sickbed of the radical 疒 full circle.
病故	bìng gù	to die of illness; to pass away from sickness — 病 (bìng, illness) + 故 (here, to pass away). A measured, somewhat formal way to say that someone died of illness, used in obituaries, official notices, and dignified speech. The 故 draws on the deceased sense: the dead belong now to what has passed.
病假	bìng jià	sick leave
病菌	bìng jūn	germ, pathogenic bacterium — 病 + 菌 (jūn, bacterium, fungus): a disease-causing germ. Where 病毒 names a virus, 病菌 names the bacterial culprit, the everyday word a parent uses when telling a child to wash their hands.
病情	bìng qíng	condition of an illness — 病 + 情 (qíng, situation, state): the state of a sickness, how the illness stands. Doctors use it to report whether a patient is stable, worsening, or improving.
病人	bìng rén	patient, sick person — 病 + 人 (rén, person): a sick person, the general word for a patient in a hospital or clinic. It names the person by their condition rather than their role, unlike 患者 (huànzhě), which is the more clinical register used in medical writing.
玻璃被打破了	bō lí bèi dǎ pò le	the glass was broken — 打 dǎ (to hit) + 破 pò (broken; shattered , resultative complement). The 破 is the result that makes the sentence grammatical. Breakage is typically undesirable , a window, a vase, a screen. The 被 construction here makes the glass the focus: it is the thing that suffered the breaking.
博物馆	bó wù guǎn	museum — 博 bó (broad, extensive) + 物 wù (things) + 馆. The hall of broad things, where relics and specimens are kept and shown. 故宫博物院 uses 院 instead, but the everyday word is 博物馆.
补语	bǔ yǔ	complement
不错	bú cuò	pretty good, not bad; correct, right — 不 bù (not) + 错 cuò (wrong). Two readings from one shape: as praise, "quite good, not bad" (成绩不错 "good grades"); as confirmation, "right, correct" (一点不错 "exactly right"). Note the tone sandhi: 不 shifts to bú before the falling 错.
不但	bú dàn	not only
不得不	bù dé bù	cannot but; have no choice but to — 不得不 (not-obtain-not) = double negation creates strong obligation: "cannot not do" = must do, compelled by circumstances. Stronger than 必须 bìxū in the sense of reluctant necessity. The speaker has no choice.
不得了	bù dé liǎo	extremely; terrible; amazing (intensifier) — 不 + 得了 déliǎo (can be concluded; can be over). "Cannot be concluded" → extreme beyond measure. Used after adjectives as a strong intensifier: 好得不得了 = incredibly good. Also standalone: 不得了！= This is unbelievable / It's a disaster! (depending on context).
不懂	bù dǒng	to not understand
不都	bù dōu	not all; not every one (partial negation) — 不 precedes 都. The negation falls on the universal claim: "it's not the case that all of them do X." Some do, some don't. This is logically weaker than 都不 and much more common in careful, qualified speech.
不断	bú duàn	continuously; without stopping
不妨	bù fáng	might as well; no harm in
不过	bù guò	"However" or "but" (softer) — "However" or "but" (softer). The default conversational pivot. Lighter than 但是, which can feel direct or even adversarial in spoken contexts. 不过 carries a gentler "that said…" tone, ideal for adding a qualification.
不会吧	bù huì ba	"No way!" or "you can't be serious — "No way!" or "you can't be serious." Expresses disbelief, often at bad news or an unwelcome outcome. The 吧 softens it from an outright denial into an incredulous reaction.
不见了	bú jiàn le	to be missing, to have disappeared
不仅	bù jǐn	not merely (formal pair for 而且)
不可	bù kě	must not; absolutely cannot; impermissible — The strong negation of 可: not merely "cannot" (unable) but "cannot be permitted to." Formal and classical in flavor. 不可 carries the weight of prohibition rather than incapability.
不可能	bù kěn éng	impossible; cannot be — The flat negation, "impossible, no way." 这不可能 ("this is impossible"), 不可能的事 ("an impossible thing"). It is strong and absolute, unlike the softer 不太可能 ("unlikely") which leaves a sliver of chance. As an exclamation, 不可能！means "no way! / that can't be!"
不快	bù kuài	unhappy; displeased; feeling off — 不 bù (negation) + 快 kuài. The heart is not flowing freely , something is blocking it. Milder and more internal than 不高兴 (visibly in a bad mood); 不快 often describes a quiet dissatisfaction or sense of offense that hasn't been expressed.
不来了	bù lái le	not coming anymore; decided not to come — 不…了 is the negation of sentence-final 了 , it signals a reversal of an expected or prior state. The 了 here paradoxically signals a new situation: the new situation is "not doing X anymore." Extremely common for announcing changed plans, habits that have been given up, or states that have ended.
不劳而获者	bù láo ér huò zhě	one who gains without working; one who reaps without effort — 不劳而获 (to gain without labor) + 者. A full predicate (verb phrase plus adverb) nominalized by 者. The resulting noun phrase can serve as subject, object, or object of a preposition. This is 者 at its most generative: even a four-character predicate can be converted into a noun phrase by appending 者.
不良率	bù liáng lǜ	defect rate — Percentage of defective units; 良品率 or 良率 (liánglǜ) is the yield, its complement. 不良品 (bùliángpǐn) and 次品 (cìpǐn) are defective goods. Agree an acceptable rate in the contract and what happens above it.
不论	bù lùn	no matter; regardless of — 不 bù (not) + 论. Functionally interchangeable with 无论 in most sentences, slightly less formal. Same grammar: an open element follows, and 都 or 也 marks the main clause.
不明不白	bù míng bù bái	unclear, fishy, ambiguous
不平等条约	bù píng děng tiáo yu ē	unequal treaties
不然	bù rán	otherwise; or else; if not so — 不 bù (not) + 然 rán (so). Literally "if it is not so," hence "otherwise." Warns of the alternative outcome: do this, 不然 the bad thing happens. Close to 否则 fǒuzé but more colloquial.
不如	bù rú	not as good as; would be better to — 不 bù (not) + 如 rú (as good as). Either ranks one thing below another (A 不如 B, "A is not as good as B") or proposes a better course ("we'd be better off..."). Context tells the two apart.
不食人间烟火	bù shí rén jiān yān huǒ	"not eating the smoke and fire of the human world" — otherworldly; too aloof to be of this world — Literally: "does not eat the smoke and fire of 人间." Originally said of immortals (仙 xiān) and deities, who subsist on pure air rather than cooked food and are therefore free of the desires and needs that tie mortals to this world.
不是	bú shì	is not; to not be; it's not that… — The negation of 是 , always 不是, never 没是 (unlike verbs of action, which can be negated with 没 for past tense).
不是我说的	bú shì wǒ shuō de	it wasn't me who said it (correcting the agent) — The classic defensive use of 不是…的: denying agency for a known past statement or action. Both parties know something was said , the 不是…的 corrects the attribution. This is one of the most practically useful forms of the construction in daily social life.
不是昨天买的	bú shì zuó tiān mǎi de	it wasn't bought yesterday (correcting the time) — Time correction: both speakers know the item was bought , the correction is about when. 不是昨天 denies the specific time; the speaker may then supply the correct time with 是…的.
不舒服	bù shū fu	Unwell — Unwell: "not comfortable." The all-purpose symptom, the polite sick-day excuse, and the doctor's opening question in negative form: 哪里不舒服?
不太	bú tài	not very; not too; not quite — 不 bù (not) + 太 tài (very). The gentle negative, softer than a bare 不: 不太喜欢 "don't really like it," 不太方便 "not very convenient." The standard polite way to decline or downplay.
不太会喝酒	bú tài huì hē jiǔ	not much of a drinker — Decide before the dinner whether you drink, and say it early with a reason that leaves no one at fault: health, medication, an early meeting, or driving. Once you have drunk one toast, refusing the next is harder.
不谈生意	bù tán shēng yi	no business talk — Price is rarely negotiated at the table, but the boss may ask a soft question about volume or the future. Answer warmly and without numbers; numbers belong in the meeting room the next day.
不同	bù tóng	different; not the same; difference — 不 bù (not) + 同 tóng (same). The standard word for "different": 不同的意见 "differing opinions." As a noun it names the difference itself: 两者的不同 "the difference between the two."
不退转	bù tuì zhuǎn	non-regression
不喜欢	bù xǐ huan	to not like; to dislike — Negate with 不 before the verb. To soften, insert 太: 不太喜欢 ("don't really like") is gentler and more polite than a flat 不喜欢. For a stronger feeling, 讨厌 tǎoyàn means "to find annoying, to dislike intensely."
不孝	bù xiào	unfilial
不锈钢	bù xiù gāng	stainless steel — Grades by number: 201 is cheaper and less corrosion-resistant, 304 is the common food-contact grade, 316 adds molybdenum for better resistance to acids and salt. 材质 (cáizhì) is "material" in the sense of grade and composition.
不要	bú yào	don't (prohibition)
不一定	bù yī dìng	"Not necessarily — "Not necessarily." A gentle pushback that leaves room for the other person's view to be partly right. Often used to introduce a qualification: 不一定吧, 也可能是… ("not necessarily, it could also be…").
不一样	bù yí yàng	different
不应该	bù yīng gāi	should not; ought not to — Negates the obligation: putting 不 before 应该 says something ought not to be done: 你不应该对她说谎 ("you shouldn't lie to her"). Often it voices regret about the past, "should not have": 我不应该那样说 ("I shouldn't have said that"). Do not confuse with 应该不, where 不 falls after and negates a guessed event instead.
不用	bù yòng	no need to; don't have to
部队	bù duì	military unit; troops; armed forces — 部 bù (unit) + 队 duì (team; formation). A body of troops under one command, from a single unit up to the armed forces in general: 参军入部队 "to join the army." Soldiers say 在部队 "in the service."
部分	bù fen	part; section; portion — 部 bù (section) + 分 fen (a share). A part of something larger, the everyday word for a portion: 大部分 dàbùfen "the greater part, most of," 一部分 yí bùfen "a portion." Note the second syllable goes neutral.
部件	bù jiàn	component; assembly
部门	bù mén	department; division; branch — 部 bù (section) + 门 mén (gate; category). One demarcated wing of a company or institution: 销售部门 "the sales department," 政府部门 "government departments." The standard word for an organizational unit.
部长	bù zhǎng	minister; head of a department — 部 bù (ministry) + 长 zhǎng (chief; head). The head of a ministry or department: 外交部长 "the Foreign Minister." The 长 here is the leader-sense, read zhǎng, not the cháng of "long."
才华	cái huá	brilliance; flair
才两个人	cái liǎng gè rén	only two people (not enough — disappointing) — 才 before a quantity expresses that the amount is smaller than expected or desired. This is the scarcity / insufficiency use of 才: the number is barely enough, disappointing, a minimum.
才能	cái néng	ability; talent; capability — 才 cái (talent) + 能 néng (capacity). The abilities a person actually possesses and can put to use: 发挥才能 "to give full play to one's talents." Note the toned reading cáinéng (the noun); the adverb 才 plus 能 (cái néng, "only then can") is a separate construction.
菜单	cài dān	menu; (computing) a menu — 菜 cài (dish) + 单 dān (list; slip). The list of dishes a restaurant offers, and by extension the drop-down 菜单 of a computer program. 看菜单 "to look at the menu."
菜鸟	cài niǎo	(slang) novice; newbie; noob — 菜 cài (here: weak, lousy) + 鸟 niǎo (bird). A fledgling, hence a beginner who is still bad at something, especially in gaming and the workplace. Self-deprecating or teasing: 我是个菜鸟 "I'm a total newbie."
菜系	cài xì	cuisine school; culinary lineage — Cuisine system , the term for a recognized regional cooking tradition. The 八大菜系 (eight great cuisine systems) are the canonical eight, though culinary scholars recognize anywhere from four to dozens of regional traditions.
参观	cāng uān	to visit; to tour (a place) — 参 cān (to take part) + 观 guān (to view). To visit a place in order to see it: a museum, factory, school, or exhibition. Reserved for touring sites, not for visiting people, where 看望 kànwàng or 拜访 bàifǎng is used instead.
苍白	cāng bái	pallid, ashen, sickly white
草地	cǎo dì	lawn; grassland; a grassy patch — 草 cǎo (grass) + 地 dì (ground). Any stretch of ground covered in grass, from a park lawn to an open meadow. 在草地上 "on the grass."
草稿	cǎo gǎo	rough draft; manuscript draft — 草 cǎo (rough) + 稿 gǎo (manuscript). The first, unpolished version of a piece of writing or a plan. 打草稿 "to make a draft," to sketch something out before committing.
草率	cǎo shuài	careless; hasty; rash — 草 cǎo (rough; hasty) + 率 shuài (rate; rashly). Done quickly and without care, like grass thrown up overnight. 做事草率 "to do things carelessly."
草帽	cǎo mào	straw hat — 草 cǎo (straw) + 帽 mào (hat). A hat woven from dried straw, the broad-brimmed shade of farmers and summer beaches. 戴草帽 "to wear a straw hat."
草药	cǎo yào	herbal medicine; medicinal herbs — 草 cǎo (herb) + 药 yào (medicine). Medicine made from plants, the leaves and roots brewed in traditional Chinese pharmacy. Often expanded to 中草药 zhōng cǎoyào, Chinese herbal medicine.
草原	cǎo yuán	grassland; steppe; prairie — 草 cǎo (grass) + 原 yuán (plain). A vast open plain of grass, the wind-rippled steppe of Inner Mongolia or the prairies of the world. 内蒙古大草原 "the great grasslands of Inner Mongolia."
测量	cè liáng	to measure: to survey, to gauge with an instrument — 测 cè (to gauge, to fathom) + 量 liáng (to measure). The formal verb for taking precise measurements: surveying land, gauging distance, recording temperature or pressure with an instrument. 测量土地 (to survey land), 测量距离 (to measure distance).
曾国藩	Zēng Guó fān	Zeng Guofan, scholar-general
曾经	céng jīng	once, formerly
差不多	chà bu duō	almost; about the same
差错	chā cuò	a slip, an error; a mishap going wrong — 差 chā (off, deviating) + 错 cuò (error). A small slip or accidental error, and by extension an unforeseen mishap: 万一出了差错 "in case something goes wrong." Both halves mean "off the mark," doubling the sense of deviation.
拆开	chāi kāi	to tear open; to unwrap
常常	cháng cháng	often; frequently; time and again — The reduplicated 常. A common, conversational adverb for frequent action. Its negative is usually 不常 bùcháng "not often," since 不常常 is avoided.
常识	cháng shí	common sense; general knowledge — 常 cháng (constant; common) + 识 shí (knowledge). The basic knowledge that holds across a community, what one is expected to know: 生活常识 "everyday know-how," 缺乏常识 "to lack common sense."
厂房	chǎng fáng	factory building
厂家	chǎng jiā	manufacturer
唱歌	chàng gē	to sing — 唱 (sing) + 歌 (song). The social institution attached is KTV (卡拉OK), where 唱歌 is what colleagues, classmates, and business contacts do together; declining every song is borderline antisocial, and one rehearsed number is sound survival strategy.
唱片	chàng piàn	record; disc (vinyl, album) — 唱 chàng (to sing) + 片 piàn (a thin flat piece). A singing-disc: the physical record that holds recorded sound. 唱片公司 chàngpiàn gōngsī is a record label. The word predates and survives digital formats.
